Tech > Mobile > Headphones
Bose's $99 Noise Canceling Wired Earbuds have been hard to get after earning a CNET Editors' Choice award.
Executive Editor / Reviews
Executive Editor / Reviews
October 30, 2026, 11:25 am ET
After I got my hands on an early review sample of Bose’s new $99 Noise Canceling Wired Earbuds a few weeks ago, I posted a review and described them as possibly the best USB-C wired earbuds out there at the moment, tagging them with a CNET Editors’ Choice award. That review proved popular with CNET readers, and our unboxing of the buds on Instagram was viewed over 500,000 times. The buds quickly sold out at launch, and Bose has had trouble keeping them in stock.
While you still can’t get the buds in the white smoke color, you can now order the dewdrop mint and black versions, though the shipping dates have been slipping. Whether Bose anticipated that the buds would be so popular, I don’t know. But wired headphones have had a resurgence in the marketplace as a certain cohort of consumers, many on the younger end of the age spectrum, are eschewing wireless buds for wired ones that cost a lot less, offer plug-and-play connectivity via USB-C and, as such, don’t need to be charged.
Sure, the Noise Canceling Wired Earbuds are pricier than most USB-C wired buds, which tend to cost $30 or less, including Apple’s $19 EarPods (USB-C). But they have a premium design, excellent sound, decent noise canceling and reasonably good voice-calling performance. They’re also Bose’s most affordable earbuds. You can read my full review here.
More from CNET

4,2026-10-30,"AI giant Anthropic is set to go public in mid-November, but there are ways for eager investors to get exposure ahead of the IPO.
In a client note on Wednesday, Sevens Report Research founder Tom Essaye highlighted five funds that invest the creator of the Claude AI chatbot.
We'll list those below, but first, here are some facts, figures, and things to keep in mind before putting your money into them.
Not all of the funds listed below are ETFs, which are highly liquid since they trade like stocks.
Instead, some are closed-end and interval funds, which may only be redeemed once every quarter.
Also, some of the funds, like Corgi's MANGOS ETF, use complex derivative such as perpetual futures and swaps to simulate exposure to a private company.
If you want in on Anthropic before its IPO, you may have to pay a hefty premium.
While low-cost index funds often have expense ratios well below 0.1%, specialized funds that invest in firms like Anthropic and other private companies can carry high price tags. Some expense ratios you'll see below climb well above 2%, which can eat into returns over time.
Anthropic is expected to IPO at around a $1.5 trillion to $2 trillion market cap, which would make it one of the top 10 largest most valuable companies in the world. That implies a steep valuation considering it's still losing money.
But the company's revenue continues to increase. It recently reported $16.8 billion in revenue in Q3, up 45% from Q2.
All of the hype around an IPO can cause a stock to soar in its first few days of trading. Yet, over the following months, performance is more lackluster, historical data shows.
Truist data on 30 high-profile tech IPOs published earlier this year showed that the average stock-price drawdown within a year of going public was -55%.
SpaceX went through a rough patch shortly after its launch on public markets. After an initial surge, it fell as much as 46% from June 16 highs. Since August 5 lows, it has recovered, gaining 34%. The shares remain volatile, down 10% since their debut at Thursday's close.
With all that in mind, let's take a look at some of the funds offering exposure to Anthropic before it hits the public market. Commentary from Sevens Report Research is included for each fund.
Ticker: VCX
Anthropic weighting: 20%
Expense ratio: 1.85%
Comments: ""NYSE-listed public venture fund that provides access to top private tech companies.""
Ticker: DXYZ
Anthropic weighting: ~14%
Expense ratio: 2.5%
Comments: ""NYSE-listed closed-end management investment company that invests in top venture-backed private technology companies.""
Ticker: MN
Anthropic weighting: ~9%
Expense ratio: 0.2%
Comments: ""Concentrated actively managed ETF that offers exposure to Meta, Anthropic, Nvidia, Google, OpenAI, and SpaceX or the MANGOS.""
Ticker: LAZR
Anthropic weighting: ~10%
Expense ratio: 0.75%
Comments: ""Invests in companies addressing a critical AI bottleneck of moving data faster—and had the most pre-IPO exposure to Anthropic by portfolio weight of any U.S.-listed ETF as of Sept. 2026.""
Ticker: ARKVX
Anthropic weighting: ~4%
Expense ratio: 2.90%
Comments: ""Actively managed closed-end interval fund that seeks long-term growth by investing in both private and public companies that are disruptive innovators.""

Tech > Services & Software > Cybersecurity
It's a bad week to own crypto.
Reporter 2
Reporter 2

				Edited by Jon Reed 
October 30, 2026, 11:04 am ET
The popular cryptocurrency exchange Binance was hit by hackers this week. The company confirmed on Thursday that attackers gained access to its servers and stole $6.3 billion, with analysts calling it one of the biggest thefts in cryptocurrency history.
Binance said hackers likely used an employee’s laptop to gain access to their servers. The company says it “will cover all losses” for affected traders using its secured reserves and funds. Binance is working with the FBI and US Secret Service on the investigation, Reuters reports. The company has temporarily paused all withdrawals.
Binance's initial investigation suggests that the security incident may involve a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions. We have found no evidence of any wallet private… pic.twitter.com/5REncAI77y
Crypto prices fell across the board upon the news of the Binance breach. Cryptocurrency isn’t regulated like other stock markets or banks. Crypto markets are constantly open, and traders “mine” currency using complex computations that use a lot of energy. (If that sounds familiar, it’s because a lot of crypto operations were the blueprint for AI data centers.) Binance is not insured by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the government agency that protects your bank deposits (up to a certain amount) if your bank fails.
More from CNET
Binance told CNET that of the $6.34 billion the company is committed to covering traders’ losses, $5 billion is being held in exact token assets, with plans to purchase the rest through other market channels. “We are doing this to match the format of the remaining token assets and address any asset shortfalls,” a spokesperson said. So if you lost money in the form of bitcoin, you would ideally be reimbursed in bitcoin, not another cryptocurrency. “This step can take longer because some tokens may have low liquidity and OTC settlement also takes time.”
Cybersecurity breaches are increasingly escalating, thanks to AI powering both defenders and attackers. Polymarket, another crypto trading platform, temporarily paused trading on Thursday morning due to a third-party vendor issue, which was also referred to law enforcement, according to the company.
But the Binance hack may be the “largest hack in history,” according to blockchain research firm TRM. Blockchain analyses from TRM and Chainalysis attribute the hack to a North Korean group called TraderTraitor, but Binance and law enforcement officials have not confirmed that. The FBI told CNET the agency “is aware of the incident and is engaged with relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate potential impacts, and coordinate appropriate investigative steps.”

11,2026-10-30,"Apple's new OLED touchscreen MacBook Pro could ship between 1.2 million and 1.5 million units in 2027, according to a new forecast from market intelligence firm TrendForce.
Announced on Wednesday, the new MacBook Pro is the first to feature a tandem OLED touchscreen, with pricing starting at $2,899 for the 14-inch model and $3,399 for the 16-inch model. Pre-orders are available now, with a launch coming on Friday, November 6. 
Given that Apple is selling the OLED models alongside its existing mini-LED MacBook Pro lineup, TrendForce says that the premium starting price could temper near-term replacement demand.
Even so, the firm expects Apple's arrival in the OLED laptop market to accelerate the market landscape more broadly. Up until now, it has been driven mainly by Windows brands such as Lenovo, HP, and ASUS. 
The overall laptop market has been contracting for two consecutive years, but TrendForce forecasts OLED laptop shipments will grow nearly 40% year-over-year in 2026, followed by another increase of more than 40% in 2027, with brands increasingly prioritizing OLED models in their product roadmaps.
Samsung Display is currently the exclusive panel supplier for the new MacBook Pro. The company has designated Phase 1 of its G8.6 A6 production line at its Asan campus in South Korea as a dedicated facility for Apple, and the MacBook Pro is the first product to enter mass production on Samsung Display's G8.6 fine metal mask (FMM) OLED line. The display's tandem OLED architecture and 254 PPI pixel density impose exceptionally demanding production requirements, particularly in material costs and manufacturing yields, notes TrendForce.
Meanwhile, rival panel makers are conducting qualification programs, but TrendForce believes the cost of meeting Apple's specifications and Samsung Display's established position present considerable barriers to entry. LG Display, BOE, and TCL CSOT are instead said to be directing their efforts toward the supply chain for the OLED MacBook Air, which isn't expected to debut until at least 2028.

Equibles Research. Nasdaq's $1 Warning: Most Companies Stay Listed, Most Shares Fall. 2026-10-30. https://equibles.com/research/nasdaqs-1-warning-most-companies-stay-listed-most-shares-fall
Methods, sample dates, limitations, and source references are described in the study below. Each table's CSV download contains its published cells, not the underlying raw dataset. Spreadsheet-formula text is escaped for safety.
Please cite this study and retain its source attribution when sharing a figure or table.
We followed 145 Nasdaq companies that disclosed their first minimum bid price notice in the first half of 2025. By October 2026, three in four were still listed and 59% had done a reverse split, but the typical stock still listed was down 47% from the day of the letter while the S&P 500 rose 32%.
On May 28, 2025, Nasdaq told Opendoor (OPEN $1.80 -5.26%) that its shares had closed below $1.00 for 30 business days in a row. The company did what most companies in that position do: it called a special meeting to ask shareholders for permission to do a reverse split. Then the stock ran. Opendoor closed at $1.00 or more on 12 straight trading days from July 15 to July 30, Nasdaq closed the matter, and the board cancelled the meeting.
Opendoor is the story everyone remembers, but it isn't the typical one. We followed every Nasdaq company that disclosed its first minimum bid price notice in the first half of 2025, 145 of them, to October 26, 2026. Most are still listed. Most did a reverse split to get there. And for most of the people who held the stock on the day the letter was disclosed, the shares are worth less now.

            Explore the primary data Equibles carries for OPEN:
        

            Connect the free Equibles MCP server to ChatGPT or Claude and get price history, congress
            trades, earnings-call transcripts, institutional investor transactions
            and much more — or ask ALVIS, our AI agent, about OPEN for a live quote right here.
        
Nasdaq requires a listed stock to close with a bid of at least $1.00. When the closing bid has been below that for 30 consecutive business days, Nasdaq sends a deficiency letter, and the company has four business days to disclose it, usually in an 8-K under Item 3.01. The letter starts a compliance period of 180 calendar days. To regain compliance, the stock must close at $1.00 or more for at least 10 consecutive business days, and Nasdaq's staff can ask for up to 20.
A company that hasn't recovered after 180 days can get a second 180 days if it meets the other initial listing standards for the Nasdaq Capital Market, moving there if necessary, and tells Nasdaq it will cure the deficiency with a reverse split if needed. After that comes a delisting determination, which the company can appeal to a Hearings Panel.
Two changes approved by the SEC on January 17, 2025, made the end of that road shorter, and the companies in this study went through the process under them:
A separate rule applies at the bottom: a stock that closes at $0.10 or less for ten consecutive trading days during a compliance period gets a delisting determination immediately.
Three in four companies are still listed, which sounds better than it is. Of the 30 still listed but trading under $1, 20 are on their second trip through the process: since the first letter they have received a new one, or an extension or delisting decision that could only follow a new one. Blink Charging (BLNK $0.40 -2.84%) is one: it got its first letter in May 2025, was back above $1 by September, received a new letter in January 2026, and in July was granted a second 180 days, until January 25, 2027, after telling Nasdaq it would do a reverse split if necessary. It closed at $0.38 on October 26.
The 19 delisted companies include Stardust Power, which did a 1-for-10 reverse split in September 2025, fell below $1 again, and then closed at $0.10 or less for ten trading days in October 2026. Nasdaq said it would suspend its shares on October 23, and under the January 2025 rule a hearing request doesn't stop a suspension.
Some companies don't need to do anything. Of the 126 companies with price history in our data, 59 (46.8%) closed at $1.00 or more on ten straight trading days before doing any reverse split. The median company got there 102 days after the letter, and 46 of the 59 did it inside the first 180 days. 44 of them never split at all.
Recovering didn't always last. 24 of the 59 later started the process over with a new letter, and 22 are back under $1 today.
The best predictor of a recovery was the price when the letter arrived. A stock at $0.85 needs an 18% rise to clear $1; a stock at $0.30 needs to more than triple.
The median stock closed at $0.68 on the day its letter was disclosed.
85 of the 145 companies (58.6%) did at least one reverse split after the letter. Most waited. Only 31 split within the first 180 days; 45 split between day 181 and day 360, which is when the second compliance period runs and when Nasdaq expects the split it was promised.
The median first split was 1-for-20. The ratio is usually chosen with room to spare, because a split that lands the stock at $1.10 can be undone by a few bad weeks.
Some companies split again and again. 14 did more than one reverse split after the letter and 3 did at least three. Vivakor did a 1-for-200 split in March 2026, a 1-for-20 in July and a 1-for-15 in October, so 60,000 shares from before March are now one. Its stock closed at $1.32 on October 26, on a split-adjusted basis a tiny fraction of the $0.88 it traded at when its letter was disclosed in March 2025.
A split also doesn't fix whatever pushed the stock under $1. At least 13 of the 85 companies that split fell back below $1 long enough to start the process again. Under the January 2025 rule, a company that falls under $1 again within a year of its split gets no new compliance period, only a delisting determination.
The split worked best when something else changed too. Nektar Therapeutics (NKTR $38.87 -0.79%) got its letter in April 2025 at $0.55, did a 1-for-15 split in June, and closed at $37.45 on October 26, 2026, equivalent to about $2.50 per share from before the split.

            Connect the free Equibles MCP server to pull OPEN's earnings-call transcripts,
            specific KPIs, SEC filings and market data into ChatGPT, Claude or Gemini.
        
For anyone who held the stock, the more useful question is what the shares did. We measured the change from the close on the day each company disclosed its letter to its latest close in October 2026, adjusted for splits, for the 107 still-listed companies with a price on both dates.
The gap between the last two isn't evidence that reverse splits destroy value. It's the other way around: the companies whose stock recovered on its own never needed a split, so the split marks the companies that didn't recover. What the numbers do show is that the typical stock kept falling after the letter. And these figures leave out the 37 companies no longer listed: the 18 delisted or bankrupt ones with prices in our data lost a median 97.7% by their last close.
A bid-price letter often comes with company. 58 of the 145 companies also received a notice for another listing rule at some point in 2025 or 2026, most often minimum stockholders' equity (22 companies) or a late filing (16). 16 of those 58 (27.6%) were delisted or went bankrupt, against 10 of the 87 (11.5%) that only had the price problem.
Drug developers were the largest group: 61 of the 145 companies (42.1%) are classified by the SEC as pharmaceutical preparations or biological products. They were also the largest group in our study of going-concern warnings. Small drug developers usually pay for their trials by selling new shares, which keeps adding to the share count while they wait for results.
The letters themselves tend to arrive quietly. In our study of 8-K timing, 38.6% of listing deficiency notices went public on a Friday after 4 p.m.
A Nasdaq bid-price letter is not a death sentence: three in four companies that got one in early 2025 were still listed in October 2026, and nearly half got back above $1 at least once without a reverse split. But the way most of them stayed listed was a reverse split, usually at the last moment, and for shareholders the letter was more often a warning than a bottom. The price on the day of the letter is the first number to look at. Under 50 cents, only 4 of 23 companies recovered on their own and a third were gone by October 2026.
This study describes public filings and market prices. It is not investment advice.

We timed 89,040 8-Ks from Nasdaq and NYSE companies to the second they went public. Earnings almost never land on a Friday evening, but 38.6% of listing deficiency notices and 21.5% of auditor changes do, and the timing didn't soften the market's reaction.

Tech > Services & Software > AI
Open-weight models let you run an AI chatbot on your own computer, even offline. Here’s how they work and what that means for your privacy.
Contributor
Contributor

				Edited by Corinne Reichert 
October 30, 2026, 4:15 pm ET
You might use a chatbot without thinking about who controls the model behind it. Then an update changes how it answers, or you want help with something private you’d rather keep on your computer.
An open-weight model lets you download the pretrained AI and run it yourself. You’ll need software to use it and a computer that can handle it, but you get to keep a working copy. While chatbots get most of the attention, image and video generators can also have open-weight options.
Downloading an AI model doesn’t necessarily give you unrestricted permission or reveal everything that went into making it, though. Sure, you get more autonomy in how you use AI, but the word “open” still leaves a lot unsaid. Here’s what open-weight models are, what you can do with them and what to consider before trying one for yourself.
More from CNET
In a chatbot, the AI model is the software that generates the answers. During training, the model learns patterns by adjusting billions of numerical values called neural network weights. When you ask a question, the model uses your prompt and those learned patterns to predict what text should come next, building its answer one piece at a time. 
Developers of closed models, including those behind ChatGPT and Claude, keep their weights private. An open-weight model makes its trained weights publicly available. That means you can download them and run your own copy using compatible software. 
“Whenever you open-weight a model, all the weights are all over the place on the internet, like on Hugging Face and other servers,” Pierre Stock, VP of science at AI company Mistral, tells CNET. “So it’s virtually impossible to cut access to this model.”
With a free model and app, you can chat locally without a subscription or usage fees. More importantly, a fully local setup also keeps your prompts and files off an AI provider’s servers.
Don’t worry if you’re not tech-savvy, because you won’t have to write code or open a terminal to do it. Desktop apps like LM Studio, Ollama and Jan let you download and run open-weight models with a simple install, giving you a ChatGPT-like interface right on your Mac or PC. You still need a computer that can handle your chosen model. Once everything is installed, you can keep chatting offline.
If you know how, you can also adapt the model through further training, where permitted by its license. You don’t have to train an entirely new model from scratch.
People often use these terms interchangeably, but they don’t mean quite the same thing. An open-weight model can also be open-source, but publishing the weights alone doesn’t qualify under the Open Source Initiative’s definition.
Developers must share the code used to train and run it. They must also provide enough information about the training data for a skilled person to build a model that works much the same way. The terms must allow people to use, study, change and share it.
Under the OSI definition, developers don’t have to share the data they used to train a model. The Free Software Foundation says that should be part of the deal, with users free to use and change the data themselves.
Some open-weight licenses limit business use, meaning a model might be free to download but off-limits for an app you want to sell. That kind of restriction would disqualify it under the OSI definition. An open-source license can still require you to keep the same terms when you share a version you’ve changed.
Chinese models currently lead independent AI testing company Artificial Analysis’s intelligence rankings for open-weight models. You may already know about DeepSeek, but there is also Moonshot AI’s Kimi K3 and Alibaba’s Qwen3.8 family, which offer downloadable weights.
Some US alternatives come from the companies behind familiar chatbots. OpenAI offers gpt-oss models separately from ChatGPT, while Google has Gemma models. Meta has Muse Glimmer and added Muse Spark Open alongside its Llama models, and Nvidia has Nemotron. 
You don’t have to stick with the chatbot giants, either. A US-based startup, Reflection, released Beam, whose weights are now available to download. Another US startup, Thinking Machines Lab, offers its model Inkling.
Europe has a contender in France’s Mistral AI. Its downloadable models include Mistral Large 3 and the smaller Ministral 3 family. The newer Mistral Large 4, dubbed Le Chonk, is available now with downloadable weights.
Not every model mentioned here will run on a laptop. Some larger models need server hardware, while smaller versions can run on personal computers or even smaller devices, like phones.
An open-weight model can still hallucinate when it’s running offline on your laptop. It can also carry biases from its training. Developers may publish weights while keeping training data private, leaving outsiders unable to fully check what went into them.
Performance varies by model and task, so openness alone won’t tell you whether it will beat a closed alternative. 
The freedom to change a model also means people can weaken or remove its safeguards. 
“Guardrails are necessary to open-weighting a model,” Stock says. When I ask how to keep them safe if people can remove those protections after downloading a model, Stock says, “With a lot of work, you can partially overcome the guardrails,” comparing the effort involved to do so to fine-tuning a model yourself.
Once people have downloaded copies, the original developer has limited control over what happens next. A company can cut off someone misusing its online service, and releasing a safer replacement won’t update everyone’s download. 
Yet those same downloads allow independent researchers to examine models more closely and identify faults. Researchers can test changes themselves, rather than depend on the developer to investigate a complaint.
Microsoft and other companies cited that benefit in an open letter defending open weights after reports of potential US restrictions on Chinese models. They argued that keeping these tools available helps security teams defend against attackers using AI.
“When you open-weight a model, you’re essentially enabling the entire ecosystem to progress together,” Stock tells CNET.

Open-weight models can draft emails, summarize documents, analyze local files and help with coding just like cloud models. However, when you run one locally, its speed and ability to handle long documents depend on both the model and your computer’s memory and processing power.
Larger models usually run more smoothly on computers with plenty of memory or a powerful GPU, like a Mac with 16GB or 36GB RAM, though smaller models may work on an 8GB Mac. 
If you already own the necessary hardware, it’s worth considering installing an open-weight model. But if you have to buy a new computer to run them, weigh that cost against a $20-per-month subscription for a cloud model.
You can also use some open-weight models through their developers’ own websites that run them for you, like DeepSeek and Kimi. That’s less work than downloading and running the model yourself, but your prompts go to the provider.
If you want occasional help writing an email and don’t mind using an online service, a hosted chatbot may suit you just fine. ChatGPT, Gemini and Claude all have free plans, so avoiding a subscription doesn’t automatically require running your own AI.
Open weights give you the option to take more control yourself, not to mention keeping sensitive documents on your device. An assistant that keeps working when you don’t have access to the internet sounds great. So does being able to keep a model you like even after its developer retires the online one. 
“You don’t want to live in a world in which someone can cut off access to your model,” Stock says. 
The future also looks promising with open-weight models being installed on smaller devices. Google’s Gemma models include smaller versions for phones and laptops. Mistral has a similar range of models, including specialized ones intended for particular uses and devices.
“We also have models specialized in audio, for instance,” Stock says, “and those can fit literally on the smartwatch.”

Two officials confirmed to BBC the cancellation of the ""family photo"" scheduled for Friday, which usually include representatives from all nations attending.
""Let me say this directly to the Russian Foreign Minister: We all know who the aggressor in this war is,"" UK Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband later told his Russian counterpart at the summit, accusing his government of killing innocent civilians in Ukraine.
The meeting is being hosted in Atlanta by US Secretary of State Marco Rubio. 
Speaking with G20 foreign ministers on Friday, Miliband said that member countries  must ""work harder to secure just and lasting peace in Ukraine"".

20,2026-10-30,"Warren Buffett hasn't had a glass of water since at least the 1970s.
That's what the 96-year-old business icon once told Bill Ackman, the billionaire hedge fund manager recalled during an episode of the Equity Mates Investing podcast that aired this week.
Ackman said that Buffett came over for dinner at his home ""years ago,"" and he'd made sure to stock up on the legendary investor's favorite drink, Cherry Coke.
The Pershing Square founder and CEO said that once they were sitting at the table, he asked Buffett whether he'd like still or sparkling water. Buffett politely declined, opting for his preferred soda instead.
""But I said, you know, 'Don't you drink water?'"" Ackman recalled. ""And he said, 'Bill, I haven't had water in 48 years.'""
Ackman didn't immediately respond to a request for more detail about Buffett's hydration habits.
Not drinking water might sound unbelievable, but Buffett — who retired as Berkshire Hathaway's CEO after 60 years in charge last December, and as the company's chairman this September — is famous for drinking five cans of Coke daily.
He quipped in 2015 that he was ""one-quarter Coca-Cola,"" as it accounted for 25% of his daily calories.
Buffett's love of soda goes back decades. During Berkshire's 1986 meeting, he declared that after ""48 years of allegiance"" to Pepsi, he was switching to Cherry Coke, saying it would be the meeting's official drink going forward.

Coke is only part of Buffett's iconic diet. He's known for grabbing McDonald's for breakfast on his drive to work, and munching on hot dogs, fries, popcorn, cookies, candy, ice cream, and other comfort foods.
His love of fast food and sweet treats extends to his investments. Berkshire counts See's Candies and Dairy Queen among its subsidiaries, and Kraft Heinz among its largest stock holdings.
Buffett's childlike tastes have led to issues in the past, including a disastrous sushi dinner in Tokyo.
Bill Gates and his then-wife, Melinda, went to great lengths to ensure the investor didn't starve when he joined them on a 17-day trip to China in 1995.
They organized for their hotel's chefs to be taught how to prepare hamburgers and fries, and when they climbed the Great Wall, they made sure to have Cherry Coke waiting for Buffett at the top.

21,2026-10-30,"Now, before the hotel police come for me, or rather the Four Seasons police, I will say this: I have loved every Four Seasons property I've ever visited — from Egypt to Portugal to New York City.
Now that I've checked into more than 20 of them at this point, I wouldn't need a second to think about my favorite one. This particular hotel — which I'll share in a minute, don't worry — inches the others out because of what it offers.
This luxury hotel pushes you to do more than simply relax by the pool with a cocktail in hand, enjoy a massage, or dine at a good restaurant or two.
Astir Palace sits on a peninsula in Vouliagmeni, along the stretch of coast known as the Athens Riviera. 
The drive from the city center takes about half an hour. I can't overstate how much that matters. Usually, if I want water this blue in Greece, I'm getting on a ferry and hightailing it to Santorini, Mykonos, or one of the neighboring islands. 
Here, I spent the morning taking a tour of the Acropolis and was back in a beach chair before lunch, and that's what I call a win, if I do say so myself.
I also didn't realize how much history this hotel had until I got there. It first opened in August 1960, and for years it was where Greek royals and Hollywood stars spent their time, with Jackie Kennedy Onassis and Brigitte Bardot among those who spent summers there. 
Former President Barack Obama was actually the very last person to stay at the old hotel when he visited Greece in 2016, right before it closed for renovation.
Four Seasons bought the property, put roughly $700 million into rebuilding it, and reopened it in 2019 as the company's first hotel in Greece. 
They held onto a lot of the original mid-century architecture, so you get the look of the 1960s with all the comforts you'd expect from a Four Seasons today.
Some guests arrive by helicopter or by boat, but I came in a regular car like a regular person. Once I got settled, I realized just how big this place is. The grounds cover about 74 acres, and there are three private beaches, so I always found a spot no matter what time I showed up.
My room faced the water, and the Aegean Sea was so clear that I could see the rocks on the bottom from my balcony. 
The food deserves its own paragraph, so here it is. With nine restaurants on the property, you could eat your way through an entire week without leaving the peninsula, and I came pretty close. 
There's Matsuhisa Athens from chef Nobu Matsuhisa if you're craving Japanese, Taverna 37 when you want Greek food done right, and Pelagos, which holds a Michelin star. 
Through a program the hotel calls Crafted in Greece, guests can sign up for pottery workshops with local artisans, olive oil tastings, Greek dance lessons, and cooking classes at the market.
I went in a little skeptical: My family comes from the Bahamas, where many hotels offer ""local experiences,"" and they tend to be watered-down versions. 
Still, I have to say I was pleasantly surprised. This was the first time I experienced the local and the luxury, all in one.
The morning started with a trip to a local market with one of the hotel's chefs, and it quickly became clear that the vendors knew him well.
He stopped at nearly every stall to say hello, and we tasted our way through olives, cheeses, and different fruits as he explained what was in season.
He picked tomatoes by smell before he ever looked at them. Clearly, he was an expert at his craft.
We brought everything back to the hotel kitchen and cooked together. And by ""cooked together,"" I mean I fake-cooked, while the real chefs prepared the dishes. A couple of hours later, we sat down to a lunch made almost entirely from what we'd carried in that morning.
I've eaten at a lot of beautiful hotel restaurants over the years, but very few hotel meals have been quite like this one, mainly because I knew where every single thing on the plate came from and I had watched the people who grew or made it hand it over.
I've never had a bad room or bad service at any Four Seasons, and I don't expect I ever will. Still, what made Astir Palace different for me is that I spent more time experiencing local culture than I ever had at any hotel. 
I came home to Maryland with a crooked bowl I made at a local pottery class, a few recipes I'm still trying to get right, and a list of things I still want to try when I return.

22,2026-10-30,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
As technology and AI make professional content easier to produce, the real competitive advantage is shifting from production quality to having something genuinely worth saying.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Real estate might produce more marketing per transaction than almost any other professional services industry on earth. Professional photography, drone footage, property films, social reels, brochures, digital advertising, portal listings, email campaigns. A single home sale can generate more content than some companies produce in a quarter. And somehow, most of it sounds exactly the same.
That contradiction is worth sitting with. Because the instinct when marketing isn’t working is almost always to produce more of it. Better cameras. Bigger budgets. More platforms. Higher production value. Real estate has been running that experiment for years, and the result is a market where everything looks increasingly polished and increasingly identical.
Not long ago, professional photography was a genuine differentiator in real estate marketing. Most agents were shooting listings on a phone or hiring whoever was cheapest. The agent who showed up with a real photographer was doing something visibly different. That gap closed fast.
Now AI is closing the remaining gaps even faster. Listing descriptions that once took time to write get generated in seconds. Virtual staging that required a designer now takes minutes. Video editing tools that demanded technical skill are increasingly automated. The production floor for every agent, every brokerage, every proptech startup keeps rising.
Here’s the problem with a rising floor. When everyone’s production quality improves simultaneously, production quality stops differentiating anything. Better cameras didn’t solve real estate marketing. They created better-looking sameness. And AI is about to make mediocre content infinitely easier to produce at scale, which means the volume of indistinguishable marketing is going to increase dramatically before anyone figures out that more isn’t the answer.
This isn’t just a real estate problem. It’s happening across every industry that produces content. Design tools democratized. Video became accessible. Writing assistance arrived. The production barrier dropped everywhere. What used to be scarce, the ability to make something that looked and sounded professional, became abundant. The scarcity moved somewhere else entirely.
Walk through luxury real estate marketing for an hour and a specific vocabulary emerges. Stunning. Exclusive. One-of-a-kind. White-glove. Market leader. Exceptional. Timeless. These words appear so consistently across so many properties, agents and brokerages that they’ve stopped communicating anything. They’re filler. Placeholder language that signals luxury without describing it, claims distinction without demonstrating it and tells the audience essentially nothing about why this particular property or this particular professional is worth paying attention to.
Agent marketing has its own version. Top producer. Local expert. Client first. Results driven. These phrases are so universal that a consumer could copy and paste them between dozens of competing agent websites without anyone noticing. They describe an aspiration, not a reality. They make no claim that a competitor couldn’t make with identical credibility the same afternoon.
Proptech marketing runs the same pattern. Seamless. Transparent. Frictionless. Innovative. Reimagining. Disrupting. The vocabulary of disruption has become so widespread that it now signals nothing about actual disruption. Every company claims to be solving the same friction. Almost none of them tell you what specifically they understand about human behavior that nobody else has figured out yet.
If your story only gets interesting after someone learns the price, you probably don’t have much of a story.
The question most marketers ask is: What should we post? The question that actually matters is: What do we have that’s genuinely worth saying? Those questions produce different answers, and the gap between them is where most marketing fails.
At the property level, stories rarely live in the obvious places. Price is not a story. Square footage is not a story. Photographs of a great kitchen are not a story. Stories live in the things that made a property what it is. An architect who designed a home around how morning light moves through a specific valley at a specific time of year. A restoration that brought back materials the original builder sourced from a place that no longer exists. A working ranch that has passed through the same family for three generations and carries a particular relationship to the land that no newly built property can replicate. These things are specific. Specific is the opposite of forgettable.
At the professional level, the story underneath the obvious claims is almost always more interesting than the claims themselves. Not a top producer, but the agent who noticed a decade ago that a specific type of buyer was quietly moving into a region nobody was paying attention to and spent years learning that market before it became obvious. Not a local expert, but the broker who walked away from a larger firm to focus exclusively on a property type most of her competitors couldn’t competently sell. That kind of specificity is earned. Competitors can claim to be local experts tomorrow. They can’t claim ten years of transactions in a niche they didn’t bother to understand.
At the company level, the story worth telling is almost never about features. It’s about the assumption that turned out to be wrong, the behavior that changed, the customer experience that should have worked differently and didn’t. Why does this company need to exist right now rather than five years ago? What did the founders see that others were explaining away? Those are interesting questions. Feature lists are not.
For most of business history, the ability to produce something polished was itself a competitive advantage. Small companies couldn’t afford it. Individual professionals couldn’t access it. The production barrier kept a lot of people out.
That barrier is gone. Anyone can produce something that looks and sounds professional. The tools exist, the cost has collapsed and AI is accelerating the trend in ways that will make the current volume of mediocre content look modest by comparison.
What hasn’t been automated is having something genuinely worth saying. A perspective that comes from actual experience rather than market research. A story that could only be told by this company, this agent, this property. A claim specific enough that a competitor couldn’t make it without lying.
Real estate doesn’t have a content shortage. It has a story shortage. And the response most of the industry is reaching for, more content, better production, more platforms, more posting, is solving a problem that stopped being the real problem a while ago.
The companies that figure that out will stop asking how to make more marketing and start asking whether they have anything worth marketing in the first place. That’s an uncomfortable question. It’s also the right one.
Real estate might produce more marketing per transaction than almost any other professional services industry on earth. Professional photography, drone footage, property films, social reels, brochures, digital advertising, portal listings, email campaigns. A single home sale can generate more content than some companies produce in a quarter. And somehow, most of it sounds exactly the same.
That contradiction is worth sitting with. Because the instinct when marketing isn’t working is almost always to produce more of it. Better cameras. Bigger budgets. More platforms. Higher production value. Real estate has been running that experiment for years, and the result is a market where everything looks increasingly polished and increasingly identical.
Not long ago, professional photography was a genuine differentiator in real estate marketing. Most agents were shooting listings on a phone or hiring whoever was cheapest. The agent who showed up with a real photographer was doing something visibly different. That gap closed fast.

25,2026-10-30,"Intel shares plummeted as much as 30% on Friday after the chipmaker warned about a weaker market as supply shortages linked to the AI boom bite.
Shares in the California-headquartered company were trading at $63.76 as of around 2:30 p.m. ET, down from $92.07 at Thursday's close.
On Thursday, it reported third-quarter revenue of $16.5 billion, up 20% from a year earlier. However, it forecast fourth-quarter revenue of $15 billion to $16 billion and earnings per share of $0.25 — down from $0.52 in the third quarter.
The AI boom is increasing demand for memory and server chips needed to train and run AI models, creating shortages that have pushed up memory prices. That makes PCs more expensive to build and buy, prompting computer-makers to order fewer Intel chips.
""The industry continues to navigate one of the most significant supply shortages in history, driven by the unprecedented demand for super intelligence,"" Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan said on Thursday's earnings call.
Intel's client-computing group, which sells chips for PCs, generated $8.1 billion in the third quarter, down 9% from the previous quarter.
The company said it expects the division's revenue to decline by more than 20% in the fourth quarter.
Intel's share price has boomed in 2026, climbing more than 130% as of Thursday's close. That rise has stalled in recent weeks amid a wider sell-off in the tech sector. After Friday's open, its year-to-date rise is less than 65%.
Friday's sharp drop comes during a busy earnings week, with other tech firms also reporting disappointing numbers. On Thursday, Google recorded the largest single-day market cap loss in its history, shedding $500 billion in value after worse-than-expected revenue from its AI search tool pushed shares down 12%.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Intel said higher memory prices and limited availability were making computers more expensive. It expects global PC sales to decline about 12% this year, and by roughly another 10% in 2027.
""We do not expect a sustained recovery until memory availability improves and pricing begins to normalize,"" said Dave Zinsner, the chief financial officer.
The company's data-center business offered some hope, with revenue up nearly 80% year over year to $7.3 billion.
However, Intel said it could not meet all the demand for its server chips.
It cited a two-week shutdown at its Israeli plant following what Tan called ""an unfortunate and tragic accident,"" which it expects to cut fourth-quarter revenue by about $300 million.

26,2026-10-30,"Ever since my wife and I started dating about eight years ago, we've talked openly about our personal finances.
From the very beginning, we discussed our 401(k) withholdings and emergency funds. These conversations became even more important when we started living together and sharing expenses about a year into our relationship.
As a queer couple, my wife and I have always believed that we can write our own rules when it comes to how we approach our finances. 
Our incomes have varied over the years for many reasons, and we've tried several different payment breakdowns before finding what works for us.
About a year into our relationship, my now-wife moved into the condo that I had bought on my own. At the time, I was working in tech and earning a higher base salary, as well as annual performance-based bonuses and stock that added to our salary discrepancy.
I knew that my mortgage was higher than the rent on a place we would have chosen together based on our combined income. So, we decided I would pay the bulk of it, but we'd split the costs of groceries, utilities, and internet, as well as shared vacations.
Five years into our relationship, I sold my old condo, my wife increased her hours at her job, and we bought a townhome together. 
This time, we decided to split our mortgage payments in proportion to our base salaries, which felt like the fairest thing to compare since annual bonuses and stock awards from my company could vary. This came out to a 60/40 split.
Over the next two years, my wife's base salary got closer to mine as a result of union negotiations at her hospital. Eventually, once our salaries were essentially at parity, we shifted to a 50/50 split.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Almost eight months into our new system, I was laid off from my tech job.
I had savings in my emergency fund from my years working there and received severance, but I was suddenly in a different financial situation than I'd been before. 
For the first time in our relationship, my wife was the higher earner.
We talked about moving back to a proportionate split. My wife reminded me that we were a team, and said she'd be open to covering more of our mortgage and expenses. But between my savings, severance, and bonuses I'd made over the years, I was able to continue splitting the mortgage evenly, and I wanted to.
At the end of the day, reverting to a different split might have been ""fair,"" but it didn't feel fair of me to put the responsibility on my wife to pay more of the mortgage. 
I knew that paying a much larger share of the mortgage would bring undue stress on her, and I also knew that I could afford my half, even without my steady paycheck.
It's now been over a year since my layoff, and splitting our mortgage and expenses 50/50 has continued to feel right for both of us.
This isn't to say she's not supporting me during this time. Right now, my wife continues to work full-time while I'm doing freelance and contract work for various clients. She's covering more of our grocery expenses, and I'm also on her health insurance.
We're a team, and I'm learning to accept her help in ways that feel fair to both of us.
We both know that our jobs, housing situation, and approach to personal finance will evolve, especially as we build our careers. What matters to us is having ongoing check-ins about whether our mortgage breakdown feels right and supporting each other equally in other ways, too.
After all, what's just as important is celebrating each other's accomplishments, encouraging each other to learn new skills, and contributing to our household and the labor it takes to run it. Money is only one piece of the puzzle.

Every election, there is a crucial sliver of voters who help determine which candidates win.
These are voters who bounce from one side to the other — the rare swing voters.
And all throughout 2026, NPR has had a front-row seat to how these folks think, listening in as voters in different swing states reflect on President Trump, the political issues that matter to them, and who they might vote for going forward.
In this case, these voters supported Joe Biden in 2020 and then swung to backing Trump in 2024.
They all participated in the Swing Voter Project, which hosts online focus groups every month. They are run by messaging firm Engagious and market research recruiting firm Nelson Recruiting. NPR is a partner on the project.
So just ahead of Election Day, what have we learned from swing voters this year? Here are five takeaways:

28,2026-10-30,"
AIInternetReddit 
The dream of an open internet continues to die following the news that Reddit will begin forcing people to be logged in to an account in order to browse its trove of arcane user insights and discussions. The reason seems clear: Google AI overviews are robbing the platform blind.
“While extraordinarily useful and powerful, AI search solves a different problem than Reddit,” Reddit cofounder Steve Huffman told investors during the company’s quarterly earnings call this week. “AI does not fulfill people’s fundamental need for community, connection, and belonging with other people, and it lacks context and nuance.”
To help protect “the Reddit corpus of data,” the company will begin shifting to a “logged-in only experience.” Testing will begin in other countries in the fourth quarter before a switch is eventually flipped for every Reddit user everywhere beginning in 2027. Despite revenue and profits both growing during the third quarter, and the company claiming that logged-in users already account for 80 percent of Reddit’s monetization, its stock fell off a cliff following the announcement.
It nose-dived over 25 percent in after-hours trading after already being down significantly from the start of the year. Transforming Reddit into a “logged-in only experience” essentially undercuts its claims to being the “front page of the internet” and will seemingly lead to a massive drop in casual users coming to the platform to exchange random answers to the most random questions.
The irony of the whole thing is that Reddit previously cut deals with OpenAI and Google to let the companies train on its valuable “corpus of data.” And the thing that has subsequently happened, as anyone who gave it any thought could have predicted at the time, is that the AI giants now threaten to cannibalize Reddit rather than enter into a symbiotic relationship predicated on mutual growth.
Reddit is far from the first company to try to block the vast and growing army of online AI bots scraping its data for free. The “logged-in only experience” is akin to Amazon blocking Meta’s Muse AI agents from buying stuff on its platform. While new AI models are clearly becoming much more powerful, it’s far from obvious how they can start generating trillions in new value if they can’t keep stealing everyone else’s stuff.

The OPPO Find X10 Ultra might be testing MediaTek power, but Qualcomm isn't done yet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OPPO could be planning a big change for its next flagship camera.
The upcoming Find X10 Ultra is reportedly being tested with MediaTek's Dimensity 9600 Pro, a move that would take the Ultra series away from Qualcomm’s top-tier Snapdragon chips. But don’t write off Qualcomm just yet: Another configuration with Snapdragon silicon is said to be in development as well — and it may still be the one that hits the market.
The latest news comes from Weibo tipster Smart Pikachu, who says OPPO is testing the Dimensity 9600 Pro alongside custom imaging algorithms. The algorithms are said to be designed to work with OPPO’s Lumo True Image Engine, which is an indication that the company wants to hold onto its camera-processing abilities no matter what chip it ultimately settles on.
The potential switch makes sense on paper. The Dimensity 9600 Pro is built on TSMC’s 2nm process and consists of two C2-Ultra cores running at up to 4.55GHz and six performance cores. OPPO has already confirmed this processor for the Find X10 Pro Max, so it’s a natural fit for the Ultra.
But Smart Pikachu says the version running on Snapdragon, which is going to be Qualcomm’s Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6, is now more likely to come.
There’s more to this phone than just its processor, however. Leaks in the past suggest an ambitious camera setup, including a 200MP main camera with a 1/1.12-inch sensor and Lateral Overflow Integration Capacitor (LOFIC) technology, cutting-edge camera sensor tech that dramatically increases dynamic range and prevents highlight clipping, according to DPReview. There's also a second 200MP periscope telephoto camera, as well as a 50MP periscope lens that offers 10x optical zoom. There are also said to be plans for a front camera with 100MP.
If OPPO opts for MediaTek, the Find X10 Ultra and Find X10 Pro Max could share the same flagship SoC. That would put the pressure on OPPO to differentiate its more expensive Ultra model based on its camera hardware and imaging capabilities, rather than raw chipset specs.
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
The Find X10 Ultra is expected to be released in China in the first half of 2027. OPPO has not confirmed the handset or its final hardware configuration yet, so there is still plenty of time for plans to change. Qualcomm has the edge for now, but MediaTek has earned a seat at the table.

Tech > Mobile > Phones > Foldable Phones
Samsung's advertisement features a sliced green apple that rolls right along through the entire Galaxy Z Fold line.
Senior Editor
Senior Editor

				Edited by Gael Cooper 
October 30, 2026, 6:27 pm ET
Samsung’s new Galaxy Z Fold 8 advertisement makes a direct jab at Apple’s iPhone Duo for its late arrival into the foldable phone market. In an Instagram video, Samsung’s “Slice” ad shows off an apple (the fruit, not the company) that’s chopped into pieces before one slice rolls along past the Galaxy Z Fold lineup, spanning from 2019 to 2026’s Z Fold 8.
It’s not unusual for phone-makers to bash Apple for bringing features and hardware ideas to the iPhone long after they’ve been available on Android devices. Samsung’s $1,900 passport-style Z Fold 8, which debuted in July, has a similar shape to the $1,999 iPhone Duo that debuted in September.
Samsung has also had years to optimize its One UI 9.0 software overlay for foldable phones, with plenty of options that take advantage of the phone’s larger and smaller displays. While the Duo does run Apple’s iOS 27, my colleague Abrar Al-Heeti noted in her review that third-party apps and services are still being optimized for different screens. The Duo is breaking new ground on iOS as Apple’s first foldable phone, but that also gives rivals like Samsung the opportunity to poke a little fun at Apple in their advertising.
Samsung’s ad ends by touting that the Z Fold 8 is thinner and lighter than the iPhone Duo, which Samsung was able to achieve in part by using a new display tech called Flex Titanium and higher-capacity silicon-carbon battery tech.
While it’s fair to say that the iPhone Duo is late to the party, the company has a history of catching up quickly with its competitors. Apple began including wireless charging with 2017’s iPhone 8, years after other Android rivals. Now, Apple has helped develop the Qi2 wireless charging standard with faster speeds and magnets, and last spring the iPhone 17 Pro won CNET’s Lab Test of fastest wireless charging.
But until Apple’s new foldable phone is firmly established, I wouldn’t be surprised to see other foldable phone-makers like Google or Motorola creatively slice up Apple for waiting until 2026.

33,2026-10-30,"Your lurking days on Reddit may be numbered.
Reddit plans to begin testing a primarily logged-in experience, a shift that could make it harder for casual visitors to browse the site without an account.
CEO and cofounder Steve Huffman said on Thursday's earnings call that Reddit will test the change in select markets outside the US in the fourth quarter. The company has not said when it will roll out a fully logged-in experience globally.
The tests will help Reddit determine how much content logged-out visitors can still access, including through shared links, search, and its front page.
This is a departure from the open platform it has been for over two decades, where internet users could view its content without logging in.
""It's a big one for us, and it's a big one for me,"" Huffman said to investors about the change. He gave a few reasons for the shift.
First, logged-in users mean Reddit can target ads more effectively, as they provide the platform with more information to make ad decisions. COO Jen Wong added that more than 80% of Reddit's monetization ""sits on the shoulders of logged-in and app users,"" calling them the company's ""bedrock.""
Second, the move aims to protect Reddit content from third-party scraping.
""The vast majority of the scraping and stealing of Reddit happens in our logged-out user base,"" Huffman said. He did not specify who the third parties involved in content scraping were, AI agents or otherwise.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Reddit addressed similar concerns in 2024, updating its ""Robots Exclusion Policy"" to put guardrails in place against unknown bots crawling its site. It also sued Anthropic, Perplexity, and several other data-mining companies last year, accusing them of scraping Reddit comments for AI training.
Reddit reported a 53% increase in third-quarter revenue, from $584 million in 2025 to $895 million in 2026. Its global daily active unique users in the quarter rose 12% to 130.3 million, even as US users declined 2%.
Huffman said Reddit's search-referral traffic was down in the third quarter. In October, search traffic fell by more than 1 million referrals per week on average, he said. If that rate continues, he said Reddit could lose a significant percentage of its current 130 million daily active unique users in the fourth quarter.
The company's stock dropped more than 17% in after-hours trading on Thursday.

38,2026-10-30,"Tech stocks tumbled for the second day in a row as concerns rise over the hundreds of billions of dollars firms are investing in artificial intelligence (AI).
Shares in Intel plummeted by 28% on Friday, while Google owner Alphabet saw its stock price continue to fall another 1.4% after a 12% plunge on Thursday.
Other tech stocks, including Reddit and Amazon also fell, while the Nasdaq index of the largest tech firms listed in the US, ended October with its worst monthly slide since March 2025.
While tech firms are spending big on AI, their earnings are not always meeting analysts' expectations, spooking investors and creating fears of an AI bubble.
Major tech companies have ramped up artificial intelligence spending, with Meta, Alphabet, Oracle, Amazon, and Microsoft committing to spending $800bn between them in 2026.
Alphabet's stock market value fell sharply on Thursday, taking more than $500bn (Â£374bn) off its market value after it revealed the AI summaries it now provides as the top answer to search queries were affecting its core search advertising revenue.
At the same time, Alphabet expects to spend up to $205bn this year on investments, almost entirely related to AI.
The bigger losses on Friday were elsewhere however, with Intel shedding more than $130bn in value. 
The chip maker said it planned to increase spending on AI despite weaker demand for computers, a major source of demand for its micro-processors.
On Thursday, Intel's finance chief David Zinsner said the firm did not expect to bring in more cash than it spent this year and forecast lower-than-expected revenue for its fourth quarter.
""Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,"" he said in the chip maker's third quarter earnings report.
In the earnings call, he said the firm had significantly ramped up investment in equipment in the third quarter and expected that to be ""even more significant"" in the following three months.
Amazon on Thursday revealed it had also spent more cash than it was making, and that spending had increased six-fold over the span of three months.
The company has committed to spending $220bn this year, putting the money towards investments such as AI data centres, network equipment, and servers.
Amazon shares recovered some overnight losses, but were still down by 1.4% at the end of Friday trading.
Reddit stock plunged by 29%, after the social media platform revealed weaker-than-expected daily user figures and warned that Google's growing AI-driven search was hurting referral traffic.
Stock in online game platform Roblox also fell, down by 18%, as its daily active user numbers dropped.
Several major tech firms have reported earnings this week, including Microsoft, and analysts said there were questions about the payout from the huge investments in AI.
Chris Low, from investment management firm FHN Financial, said the bulk of what was happening in the stock market was in reaction to some of the biggest tech players such as Amazon and Google.
He told Reuters the companies were ""showing some pretty significant growing pains as they aggressively pursue the AI build-out"".
""The thinking is that these signs suggest that the entire sector is going to slow down.""
Many analysts have been debating the possibility of an AI bubble in the stock market. 
The worry is that some tech firms will not be able to justify their sky-high valuations because the cost of building AI data centres and developing the software will end up being greater than the money they can make from it.
Kathleen Brookes, research director at trading firm XTB, said there have been many ups and downs in AI trade in recent months.
""Investors have turned ruthless, punishing any signs of disappointment in [third quarter] earnings,"" she said.
""The AI trade has had a good run in recent years, but now investors want payback, and companies like Alphabet and Intel are in the crosshairs.""
However, stock market values for many of these companies remain well above where they were a year ago.
While the Nasdaq closed 2.8% lower, it is up 7.8% over the last year. Similarly Amazon is up nearly 8% over the past 12 months, while Alphabet is down 1.3%.
Brookes noted that November is typically a strong month for stocks.
""If we see AI stocks continue to struggle in the coming days, it could suggest the AI trade is past its peak, and investors will be looking for a new theme to drive markets,"" she said.

                                    T-Mobile wants you to build your own bundle with T-Mix

Your newsletter sign-up was successful
T-Mobile draws the line as SpaceX eyes a bigger wireless play.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

T-Mobile isn’t interested in turning SpaceX into a wireless partner, and CEO Srini Gopalan isn’t exactly being subtle about it. While Starlink has larger aspirations to branch into mobile connectivity, the carrier says a deeper partnership would add little value to its business.
Speaking during T-Mobile's third-quarter 2026 earnings call, Gopalan reiterated that the company has no plans to enter a mobile virtual network operator (MVNO) agreement with SpaceX. Such a deal would let SpaceX offer wireless services on top of T-Mobile's existing cell network, paving an easier route into the U.S. mobile market for the satellite company.
Gopalan said T-Mobile evaluates potential MVNO partnerships based on whether they open up new addressable markets, distribution channels, or capabilities. He doesn't believe a SpaceX deal will accomplish any of those things. The company would still consider a straight commercial or resale arrangement, but that’s a very different proposition to providing SpaceX access through an MVNO deal.
The main problem is that T-Mobile doesn’t see satellite connectivity as a big alternative to its current broadband services. Gopalan described satellite internet as an inferior alternative to fixed wireless access (FWA), except in some remote rural locations, where traditional cellular coverage is harder to deliver.
That assessment isn’t entirely surprising. Roughly two-thirds of T-Mobile’s FWA customers live in densely populated urban areas, where the carrier believes there is plenty of unused network capacity to support more growth. Meanwhile, satellite connectivity is constrained in terms of beam size and available coverage capacity.
There is also little reason for the company to merge the two technologies when its current infrastructure can already cater to its largest customer markets.
SpaceX’s ambitions go way beyond satellite messaging. The company reportedly wants access to at least 80,000 U.S. cell sites as it seeks to build a more meaningful terrestrial wireless presence. It also has FCC approval to deploy up to 15,000 next-gen Starlink satellites that can connect directly to smartphones.
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
That growth could put SpaceX in closer competition with established U.S. carriers. But T-Mobile isn’t the only major carrier keeping its distance. Verizon CEO Dan Schulman also dismissed an MVNO deal, and AT&T boss John Stankey downplayed the competitive threat.
That said, T-Mobile’s existing T-Satellite service already uses SpaceX technology, but Gopalan previously revealed that T-Satellite accounts for just 0.0002% of T-Mobile’s total network usage during normal conditions. It’s hardly a vote of confidence in satellite connectivity as a big growth driver.
Right now, T-Mobile would prefer to spend money on its own 5G network and get ready for 6G than to make a wider deal with SpaceX. The carrier sees more opportunity to squeeze more capacity out of its existing network than to bring a satellite operator into its wireless business.
Jay Bonggolto always keeps a nose for news. He has been writing about consumer tech for as long as he can remember, and he has used a variety of Android phones since falling in love with Jelly Bean. Send him a direct message via X or LinkedIn.

Tech > Home Entertainment > TVs
No one likes clunky TV interfaces or their activity being tracked, but using AI isn’t how to avoid it. There’s an easier way.
Contributor
Contributor

				Edited by Joshua Goldman 
October 30, 2026, 8:00 am ET
Recently, some posts on X/Twitter and discussed elsewhere have claimed that AI has helped people “vibe code” their way to a better TV interface. Less clutter, no ads, less tracking and more are all ascribed to AI’s magical powers. Not only is this nonsense, but it’s also stupid. 
First, let me say up front that I understand the frustration. TV interfaces have gotten bloated; they often have ads, and their creepy and invasive tracking properties have long been discussed and warned about by me and others. 
That said, AI is not doing anything unique here. Not only can you do this without AI, but it’s also probably better if you don’t. Using AI risks potentially TV-ruining issues or worse.
More from CNET
“Vibe coding” is the colloquial term for using AI to assist or fully write programming code for apps, websites and so on. Instead of learning a programming language, people tell AI agents in plain English (or their language of choice) what they want, and the AI does it, or at least something close to it. 
In this case, people are claiming they’re using an AI agent, usually Claude, to write a new operating system for TVs. This “new” OS is, they claim, faster, easier to use, has no ads and so on.
As with all claims about AI, you should be suspicious. First, TV operating systems are far more complex than they appear. Messing around “under the hood,” especially with no programming knowledge, can easily cause serious problems.
One TV engineer I spoke to for this article, who asked to remain anonymous, literally laughed out loud when I asked him about this. He explained that his engineers will often fix one bug in their software, and it will cause 10 other bugs in random, seemingly unrelated parts of the TV. That’s pretty typical with software development, by the way. There are reports already that people have “bricked” their TVs trying this. As in, they turned their TV into an expensive and unusable brick.
There are also a lot of potential issues down the road, even if you have initial success. For instance, will the new vibe-coded OS actually run Netflix and other apps? They might seem to load and then not be able to play anything. What happens if one of the apps needs an update? Will you still be able to? What if that update requires a specific Android version? Maybe you won’t have any issues, but there’s no guarantee of that, and if you don’t have any background in software or coding, can you be sure that more AI vibe coding will fix the issue? Will you be able to perform a factory reset and restore it to the way it was when you bought it? Hopefully yes, but not necessarily.
If you’re not a programmer, how sure are you that the code you’re using is secure? How sure are you that it’s only doing what you want and not exposing your TV to external access? This isn’t as likely as just breaking your TV, but if you don’t know or understand what’s going on “under the hood,” can you really be sure?
And what’s the end goal here? If it’s a faster interface with fewer ads, you can do that already without AI, as I’ll discuss in the next section. If it’s to minimize tracking, maybe changing the OS or interface will help, but not entirely.
AI can’t invent anything, and you can already change your TV’s interface with just an app install. Android TV launchers, as in the user interface, can be swapped out. So if your TV runs on Android, you can change the interface without any AI vibe coding. These launchers are often far simpler than the stock Google TV interface and are highly customizable. 
Launchers like Projectivity, Monet and TV Launcher Premium Smart UI are some of the top-rated and most popular ones. The AT4K Launcher even makes your Android TV interface look like the Apple TV interface. There are many more options.
If you have a TV with a company-specific operating system, like LG’s webOS, Samsung’s Tizen, Hisense’s VIDAA and so on, the AI vibe coding trick isn’t likely to work anyway. Companies do their best to lock down their operating systems to prevent exactly the kind of thing we’re talking about here. 
It’s entirely arguable that AI wasn’t doing anything clever in the first place. It was just regurgitating what human programmers had already figured out.
I think there’s a hilarious irony in not wanting your personal data to be out there, so in order to block your TV from tracking you, you give your personal data to AI. If you don’t understand why that’s ironic, you definitely shouldn’t give your data to AI. As I’ve talked about before, there is no easy way to prevent your TV from tracking you and selling your data. You can minimize it, or go to more extreme measures like Pi-holes and not connecting it to the internet at all, but then you’ll just be switching which device is tracking you. Still want streaming? All the streaming devices track you to some extent (the Apple TV 4K less so). 
Using AI to vibe code your way into a better TV experience, however, is a dumb idea. Try a launcher app first and see if that gets you most or all the way to what you’re looking for. Will it be a magical fix to speed up a slow TV? No, but it could be much closer to what you’re looking for, and it’s far less likely to nuke your TV. If you want to go even further, there are apps that give you even more control, but with more potential for issues. Or hey, if you have a TV you don’t mind ruining, and you want to see what you can come up with using an AI that is just stealing from other people’s work, go for it.

		© 2026 CNET, a Ziff Davis company. All rights reserved. 	"
41,2026-10-30,"Author of the First Trade newsletter
Third-quarter earnings were expected to be a referendum on the AI trade: Is demand holding up? Are companies translating big spending into actual results? How are the finances looking?
Now that many of the biggest players have reported, we've certainly gotten an indication of how the AI buildout is going. It just might not be in the direction people wanted.
The easiest way to see this is to split out the winners and losers, which ends up being quite a lopsided affair. While there are certainly some encouraging bright spots, the tech-heavy Nasdaq 100 is still about 6% off highs, down 1.4% since tech earnings season kicked off. 
That return is about to take a hit on Friday, with Nasdaq futures down roughly 2.5% on the heels of some particularly nasty reports.
Keep in mind that these are stock-market verdicts, not judgments on which companies had good quarters. In multiple cases, solid results still weren't enough to quiet the AI-spending skeptics.
Here's a company-by-company breakdown of winners and losers, including main earnings highlights and lowlights for each:
Microsoft
What happened: Microsoft hit the nail on the head with 48% sales growth in Azure, its key AI-powered cloud business. Capex came in below expectations, while Azure revenue guidance for next quarter also exceeded forecasts.
But while Microsoft was a relative standout and a clear-cut winner, its stock still fell on Thursday as Alphabet dragged the tech sector lower. (More on that lower.)

Jump to"
42,2026-10-30,"Ahmad Umar Agha, a Syrian Electronic Army hacker known online as ""Th3Pr0,"" has been sentenced by a U.S. federal court to 42 months in prison for his role in a hacking conspiracy that targeted Western news outlets.
The conspiracy was organized and led by members of former Syrian President Bashar al-Assad's inner circle, including the commander of Syria's General Intelligence Directorate, the U.S. Justice Department said on October 29.
The pro-Assad Syrian Electronic Army began its attacks in 2011. Its members sent phishing emails to steal employees' passwords, then used those passwords to deface websites, redirect web traffic, and hijack social media accounts, according to a 2014 FBI affidavit.
On April 23, 2013, the group hijacked the Associated Press's Twitter account and posted a false report that President Barack Obama had been injured in two explosions at the White House.
Twitter's message confirming a password reset for that account was sent to a Gmail account used primarily by Agha, the affidavit says.
The Justice Department said Agha carried out that hack, which caused a temporary $136 billion drop in the value of stocks in the Dow Jones Industrial Average. Agha later told Business Insider that a big drop in the Dow ""was our goal of that hack attack"" and that ""it was a big victory for us.""
Agha was first charged in June 2014 in a complaint that remained secret until March 2016. That month, the FBI added him and co-defendant Firas Dardar to its Cyber's Most Wanted list with a $100,000 reward. A federal grand jury indicted both men in May 2018.
Court Watch reported in December 2025 that Agha was in U.S. custody and had appeared in federal court the month before.
Three earlier public documents in the case do not describe Syrian officials organizing or leading the group: the 2014 FBI affidavit, the Justice Department's March 2016 announcement of the charges, and its May 2018 announcement of the indictment.
In the 2016 announcement, the department said the group acted ""in support of"" the Syrian government.
An early study of the group, published by the OpenNet Initiative, found no credible evidence directly linking the attackers to the Syrian government beyond the unspoken support any such group would need to operate on Syrian networks.
In announcing the sentence, the department also said the conspiracy collected intelligence for Assad's government, which fell in December 2024. ""Agha deployed his hacking skills in the service of Assad's regime to silence reporting on the regime's atrocities, intimidate its opponents, and incite U.S. servicemembers to refuse their orders,"" said John A. Eisenberg, Assistant Attorney General for National Security.
In all, Agha hacked about 47 Western media outlets, businesses, non-profit groups, and U.S. and allied government bodies, the department said.
He got into the company that hosted the U.S. Marine Corps recruiting website and redirected its visitors to an image urging Marines to refuse their orders and fight alongside the Syrian Army. About 3,500 potential visitors saw that image instead of recruiting material.
He also twice tried to take over the White House's social media accounts by stealing White House staff email passwords. Once, he got into staff email but failed to trick other staff into giving him access.
The FBI affidavit describes the group breaking into a White House staff member's personal email account in July 2013 and says no official White House account was compromised.
Many victims spent large sums on cleanup and security upgrades, according to court filings cited by the department. One company that supplies online publishers with content, ads, and links lost about $35,000 a day for about six months. A global non-profit group lost $480,000, including about $250,000 on new network monitoring tools, and had to retrain more than 800 staff members.
Dardar remains a fugitive, the department said. He was also charged in a separate case with Peter Romar, a Syrian national then living in Germany. Romar was extradited to the U.S. and pleaded guilty in 2016.
