43,2026-10-30,"Yves here. James Galbraith provides an intriguing analysis of what he depicts as the driver of the Great Crash, that of huge oil discoveries that were set to greatly lower oil and gas prices and accelerate the displacement of coal.
I have problems with this thesis. The big announcements were too close in time, a mere few days, to the great price plunge and it would have taken some time for the impact of this announcement to be digested and analyzed. In 1987, the Brady Commission found it took about ten days for the floating of a Treasury Department proposal to put a tax surcharge on borrowings on highly leveraged transactions to reverberate back to stock. Treasuries had also gotten wobbly due to bad trade deficit news.
However, Galbraith does turn to an AI economics unwind at the likely trigger of the much-expected crash. Honestly, AI’s money math so obviously never will work out that I remain mystified as to why the bustup hasn’t already happened.
By James K. Galbraith, Lloyd M. Bentsen Jr. Chair in Government and Business Relations, University of Texas at Austin. Originally published at the Institute for New Economic Thinking Website
It is an odd fact that 100 years after the Great Crash of 1929 and the ensuing Great Depression, no satisfactory and certainly no agreed explanation for these events has been arrived at.
My father’s book, largely written from newspaper accounts at Dartmouth in the summer of 1954, was the first history focused on the Crash. Up until then, the turbulence of the Depression, the New Deal and the war had absorbed scholarly, popular and political attention, and the drama of October, 1929 was beginning to fade in memory. The Great Crash 1929 assured that it would not.
By placing the Crash front-and-center, my father could tell a fine tale of financial folly, replete with chicane and rooted in the almost-innocent all-American desire to get rich without really working. It is a motif with many antecedents and also many recurrences. The elements of exuberance, hubris and comeuppance – just or not – tie financial markets to the larger human experience. And this humanizing approach has been followed by others who have since tackled the theme; Charles Morris and Andrew Ross Sorkin notably come to mind.
To the extent that a theory has been built around the Crash, it is Hyman Minsky’s “Financial Instability Hypothesis.” The story is of phase transitions in the structure of debts – hedge, speculative, Ponzi – similar to the transition of water from ice to liquid to steam as temperature rises. Stability breeds instability, Minsky wrote, and episodes like the Crash are endemic to capitalism; the dynamism and the fragility are inseparable parts of the same beast. Benoit Mandelbrot’s “multifractal generator” of financial prices conveys the same message. The system generally delivers gains, but the same process can, with utter unpredictability and no warning, deliver a colossal blow that takes them all away.
A second relevant – though highly inconvenient and therefore, studiously neglected – concept is William K. Black’s “control fraud,” defined as (what else?) fraud committed by those in control. Control fraud is collapse by design, or bankruptcy for profit as George Akerlof and Paul Romer described it. The canonical case was the Savings and Loan debacle of the 1980s and 1990s, when deregulation facilitated the criminal takeover of major institutions, notably in California and Texas, protected for too long by their political friends. For those watching closely, the detection of control fraud can make collapses predictable in certain situations, such as those leading up to the Great Financial Crisis of 2007-2009. This is why the Big Short, chronicled by Michael Lewis, could occur.
These narratives and their associated theories focus attention on financial processes and financial markets. Such processes and markets have, in this construction of the nature of things, a life of their own. They are detached from something called the “real economy” and can, it seems, be analyzed without reference to specific legal systems, cultures or technologies. The approach universalizes financial instability, so that any one such episode becomes more-or-less comparable with any other, going back to the Dutch tulip craze of the late 17th century and forward to the current moment with artificial intelligence and data centers.
The comparability of financial debacles is captured, but not explained, by the physical metaphor of the bubble. That metaphor conveys, in a single image, both the steady inflation and the abrupt collapse – a certain denouement at an unknown time. Yet the metaphor substitutes for thought; it obscures the fact that there is no cause-and-effect explanation for either one. And there is no method for distinguishing a bubble from something else, amenable to that other deceptive metaphor, the “soft landing.”
The Crash and the Depression
What then is the relationship of financial instability to the so-called “real economy”? In particular, how did the Great Crash relate to the Great Depression?
The Depression is not the central focus of The Great Crash 1929, and my father gave only a cursory survey of five inter-related causes. These were a bad income distribution, a bad corporate structure (meaning too much leverage), a bad banking structure (meaning no deposit insurance), a “dubious foreign balance” (meaning vulnerability to debt default), and the “poor state of economic intelligence,” meaning the pre-Keynesian commitment to balanced budgets. The implication, one supposes, is that if these matters had been dealt with during the 1920s, the Depression might not have occurred. But then, the 1920s would not have been the 1920s. I would not lean too heavily on such a view, and I don’t think my father would have either. The fact is, he didn’t feel it necessary to explain the Depression in this short and focused book – and so far as I know, he didn’t do it elsewhere.
My father did believe that the Crash helped to kick off the Depression; otherwise he might not have written his book. But on this point, mainstream economists have disagreed. For the dominant school of modern economics, the Crash could not have “caused” the Depression or even influenced it. For this school, the market economy is inherently stable or self-stabilizing, and the financial markets are only indicators of the underlying drift. To affect the “real economy,” something must hit the real economy, as it were, from the outside. Some kind of “shock” is necessary. Some economists have also argued that the slump was underway before the Crash, so that the stock market becomes an incidental effect,rather than a cause, for the dark years that followed.
A Brief Survey of the Chattering Economists
The mystery of the Crash and the Depression has given risen, over a century, to many mutually inconsistent views [1].
John Maynard Keynes, writing in 1930, argued that the very distinction between “financial” and “real” was untenable, misleading and beside the point. The collapse in the stock market was a collapse in profit expectations – a profound change in the social psychological foundation of business capital investment. Without expectation of profit, business investment also collapsed, and without the flow of investment, so did the aggregate demand for output. Thus Keynes could argue that the remedy was to increase effective aggregate demand by whatever means might be available, notably public expenditure and public works. This was the essence of Keynesianism: you don’t need to reverse the cause to effect the cure. But Keynes gave no very specific reason for the collapse in profit expectations, and his latter day followers (notably, George Akerlof and Robert Shiller) throw up their hands and repeat his reference to “animal spirits [2].” But is capitalism really subject to massive collapse on no precipitating grounds at all? And if so, why was the Great Crash such a rare, indeed practically unique event?
Milton Friedman and Anna Schwartz, writing in the early 1960s, blamed the Federal Reserve for not preventing the collapse of the money supply, and by extension the collapse of the money supply for the Depression. This interesting argument disconnected the crash from the Depression, and supposed (without establishing) that the power to prevent a crash of the money supply rested with the Federal Reserve in the first place. But it flowed from Friedman and Schwartz’s prior belief in the natural stability of the private economy, and therefore by construction the responsibility of government for any failures that might have occurred.
Charles Kindleberger, writing in 1973, stressed the weakness of the international monetary system, in the process of transition away from the hegemony of sterling but having not yet arrived at the hegemony of the dollar. Barry Eichengreen follows in and largely develops this tradition.
Peter Temin, writing in 1976, sought to critique Friedman and Schwartz, by then the dominant view among academic economists. Temin followed Keynes in stressing demand, but with the modification that he rooted the drop in demand in a shift of the consumption function, ostensibly exogenous and not itself easily explained. Did something happen to the American household in the late 1920s, not seen before or replicated since?
Jude Wanniski, writing in the mid-1970s, blamed the Smoot-Hawley tariff, an argument with a bit of support in these pages, which note that the tariffs helped precipitate Peruvian debt defaults, which helped undermine confidence in the banks that had made those loans. But this was not Wanniski’s mechanism; for him the essential offense was the sacrilege against free markets and free trade.
Ben Bernanke, writing in 1983, blamed a rise in “uncertainty,” and this argument was largely recapitulated by Christina Romer in 1988. Their argument linked the Depression to the Crash, but left the crash unexplained, as either an exogenous shock or something endemic to the financial markets. Bernanke focused on investment spending, while Romer added the dimension of consumer durables purchases. She writes that “the stock market crash caused consumers and producers to become uncertain about the course of future income.” (emphasis added).
I need not comment on some of the more recent work, dating to the 2000s, that blames the Depression on the New Deal, including on the price-fixing efforts of the National Industrial Recovery Act and the crop reduction programs of the Agricultural Adjustment Administration. This work conveniently places responsibility for events in 1929, 1930, and 1931 on policies enacted in 1933, a true feat of rational foresight. Indeed it has become somewhat fashionable to argue that the New Deal was entirely counterproductive, inhibiting the natural recuperation that would otherwise have occurred [3].
Meanwhile, for acolytes of the “real business cycle,” the Depression was normal, not anomalous, and therefore not an event that justifies the concern with which it has come to be viewed. Cycles happen thanks to the ebb and flow of technologies. Applied to this instance, the doctrine specifies that “total factor productivity” fell, and the rest followed. Yet total factor productivity is a vague notion, and no specific asset or specific technology appears to be associated with this idea. This led even so dedicated a “new classical” as Robert Lucas to remark in 1994, “What technological or psychological events could have induced such behavior in a large, diversified economy? How could such events have gone unremarked at the time, and remain invisible in hindsight?” A very sensible question.
In short, what a mess! Though some of it is plainly nonsense, the above list is not entirely wrong. It includes proximate causes, contributing factors, spurious correlations, and also empty, irrefutable assertions based on unobservables, such as Larry Summers’ 1986 remark that “What happened was a failure of the exchange mechanism.” What there isn’t, is a clear-cut precipitating event, that might explain both the why the stock market crashed when it did and why capital investment collapsed, why the banks collapsed, and why demand and finally production also collapsed. It seems we have a better view of the extinction of the dinosaurs, the fall of Rome and the collapse of the Maya than our economists have given us of the defining economic events of the last century.
But suppose there was a precipitating event? Suppose we just missed it, because we did not understand the underlying economic processes that could turn such an event into a disaster?
Basics of Entropy Economics
All life requires resources, which must be extracted from the environment, with a sufficient surplus so as to make it possible to work, develop, grow and reproduce. And this is no less true of any mechanical system, or of any economy. Access to resources requires energy, which comes in the first instance from sunlight, or from its stored forms, including wood, coal, oil, natural gas – as well as the nuclei of certain very heavy atoms. To get at the energy requires tools, which are built according to plans, and at increasing levels of complexity as technologies advance. But complexity is a two-edged sword. It permits ever-greater efficiency in the use of resources, thus ever-greater levels of production and the improvement in human life – but it induces fragility in the system as a whole. Once a system is built, it functions in a certain, predictable and predetermined way, so long as the conditions for which it was designed hold good. But those conditions may change. Simple systems are robust; complex ones are prone to major breakdowns and disasters.
Economic value arises because that which is necessary, or desired, is often scarce. And this is particularly true of goods or services that are newly created, imagined, or packaged in such a way as to appear to be new. Innovation is by definition recent, and by definition has a single originator. Innovation is usually achieved by introducing a new level of complexity, as well as other improvements of design or recombination of existing technical forms. During the window, however brief, when imitations are few, the originator enjoys a degree of market and monopoly power, enhancing the economic value associated with the scarcity of the innovation. This is the moment when fortunes can be made. And economic life is dominated by the search for fortune, arising from producing that which is new, better, or made more cheaply but just as well, than that which already exists.
These are the basic elements of what Jing Chen and I, in a small book published in 2025, have called “entropy economics.” Our goal was (and is) to bring economics into alignment with the understanding, now prevalent in every other field of human inquiry, of the second law of thermodynamics – the entropy law – which is the most fundamental law of nature. We believe that this is the key to understanding that there was, in fact, a precipitating technological event behind both the Great Crash and the Depression.
For mainstream (neoclassical) economics, technological change is a largely continuous process, with phases of acceleration and deceleration. It cannot make things worse. That is why mainstream economists are relentless techno-enthusiasts – they see the dynamism of capitalism as its principal justification, in the face of inequalities, environmental degradation, and the working population’s native suspicion of the capitalist class. Joseph Schumpeter, a conservative but definitely non-mainstream observer of the 1930s, took a much colder and more clear-eyed view, summed up in the phrase “creative destruction.” Schumpeter’s insight was that new technologies destroy older civilizations and ways of life, leading (he argued) to improvements for the survivors but not without severe costs for those invested in the previous systems and methods. Indeed for this reason peasant farmers and medieval guilds were by nature technologically conservative: they wanted to keep what they had and were not concerned about the great gains accruing later on to someone else. Schumpeter however, so far as I’m aware, did not specifically identify a new technology bridging the crucial moment in this story.
We believe that the precipitating event, in this instance, was the announcement of the discovery, in Texas, Oklahoma and California, of vast reserves of oil.
Oil and the American Energy Revolution
The Industrial Revolution, which began in Britain in the early 19th century, was a revolution based on coal, and on the application of coal to machinery powered by steam. Its first application was to pumps, increasing the efficiency and reducing the cost of extracting coal from the ground. It spread from there to metalworking, especially steel, and to the application of steel to transportation, through the railroads and then the ocean-going fleet, and (still later) to electrification. British power was coal power, as it remained well until the time of Margaret Thatcher and North Sea oil.
The United States followed the British path, though with greater reserves of wood and substantial water power as well, finding coal in Appalachia to power a rail system that by mid-19th century spanned the entire continent. By the first decades of the 20th century the railroads were the largest (and notoriously predatory) American industry, and their derivative demand supported the iron mines of Minnesota and the steel mills of the Great Lakes. When the Federal Reserve was created in 1913, two of its regional banks were located in Missouri (at St. Louis and Kansas City), reflecting the centrality of the rail network to both agriculture and industry in the American heartland.
By the second decade of the century, however, a new energy source was developing. Trucks are powered by oil, as are airplanes; both came into heavy use in the first world war, and as Thorstein Veblen observed, invention is the mother of necessity. In 1919 a colonel named Dwight Eisenhower set out to cross the continent with a motor convoy; it took 60 days to make a transit that could be done on the rails, by that time, in less than a week. A transcontinental highway was created (the Lincoln highway), though when my father drove it in 1930 to reach Berkeley from Cleveland, the road was unpaved from Lincoln, Nebraska to the California line.
The 1920s were boom years for the automobile industry, with production rising to 5,358,000 units by 1929, a figure, reported in The Great Crash 1929, that “compares very decently with the 5,700,000 new car registrations in the opulent year of 1953.” It was also a booming time for tractors, which were transforming American agriculture. Around one-third of American arable land in the late 19th century was used for forage, including heavily for horses that did most of the hard work in front of the plow. Tractors liberated that land, some for food crops, some for cattle. They also worked faster, and required fewer people. Agricultural productivity soared, outrunning the American ability to eat, and even to export. Any significant interruption in demand was likely not to end well.
Yet none of this necessarily had to end, least of all with a resounding crash, over a few days in October, 1929. Oil was available, but it was relatively scarce, and therefore expensive. Coal, steam, steel and the railroads enjoyed the efficiencies of a large, developed, complex infrastructure, around which American cities and factories had already been built. There were indeed also urban rail systems – the subways that still survive in New York, Boston and Chicago, but also streetcars that were allowing Los Angeles, San Francisco, Washington DC and other, relatively new and growing cities to expand. These ran on electrical cables that were powered by coal. There was good reason to expect this technological paradigm to endure – until, suddenly, there wasn’t.
The key to the mystery has been found by Christopher Kennedy of the University of Victoria, British Columbia – an industrial/ecological economist with a strong record of research into climate and similar issues [4]. It lies not in any new invention, but in discoveries that changed the valuation of oil and therefore the prospective profitability of everything that ran on some other fuel. This was the discovery, in the late months of 1929, of vast new reserves in Texas, Oklahoma and California [5].
Specifically, as Kennedy found, on October 22, 1929, The New York Times announced that Standard Oil of California was cutting the price of oil by 50 to 75 cents per barrel, about 50 percent, “due to big overproduction”, with other majors expected to follow. Stock losses for the 23d were reported at $4 billion. The next day was Black Thursday, “the first of the days which history, such as it is on the subject, identifies with the panic of 1929 [6].” Then, on October 29, alongside $14 billion in losses on the 28th (“another terrible day”), the Times reported that Standard Oil of New Jersey would cut the storage of oil: there was more, readily available, than could be sold. The same day came Black Tuesday, “the most devastating day in the history of the New York stock market, and it may have been the most devastating day in the history of markets.” The Great Crash of 1929 had occurred.
But how could such a massively favorable development, the discovery of an essentially unlimited, high quality source of energy for every conceivable industrial, agricultural and home use – not to mention military – lead to a panic in the financial markets and a collapse of real activity? This is not something that the mainstream view of technical change can hope to explain! By all rights, what was found across the American West in 1929 should have led to an economic boom – to an explosion of productivity, production and prosperity. But it did not. Or to be more precise about it, it did not do so at once, and did not promise to do so for the immediate or even medium term future.
On the contrary. The problem is that the stock market captures the expected future profitability, not of the economy in general, but of the existing economic system. In 1929, that system was centered on rail, steel, coal and steam, and on factories built to use the system of transport and marketing that had been built around the great railroad corridors that came to span the North American continent after the Civil War. Suddenly, the future of that existing system was not merely in doubt. It faced an abrupt and certain demise, as coal declined and oil took its place. With no profit expectations there would be no investment, and without investment total demand would fall – just the sequence described by Keynes.
Why not just convert the rails to oil? Because the writing was on the wall, not just for coal-fired locomotives but for the railroad economy as a whole. And you cannot build a new economy based on cars, trucks and airplanes without roads and airfields, and refineries and pipelines and gas stations and fuel depots. These, as yet, did not exist at scale. And in 1929 it was very far from obvious that they would come to exist in the foreseeable future. Indeed, who would provide them? It is hard (even now) to make roads pay with tolls. And as the markets and activities declined, so did tax revenues and, therefore, so would the resources available to units of government below the federal level. Nor would the federal government step up, so long as those who ran it believed themselves constrained by the revenues they might be able to collect from taxes.
The path from Crash to Depression runs through the crisis of the banking system. The banks held assets of two very large types. One was mortgages on farm land and crop loans. These would fail as farmers could not unload their rising production at a profit. The ensuing foreclosures would put farm land on the market, cutting into the collateral of those who remained and deepening their ruin. And the other large-scale asset, held by banks, was the debts of the railroads. These too could not be honored. Banks that could not collect on either industrial or agricultural loans were at risk, and their depositors, once alerted to this fact, would not stick around. Runs ensured a large-scale contraction of the banking system. Bank failures doubled from 1929 to 1930, rose again in 1931, and peaked in the early months of 1933. After that, Roosevelt took office, took action, and the crisis eased.
The New Deal is remembered, in the then-contemporary literature and by most of the historians [7], mainly by the great rush of legislation that began with the Hundred Days: the Emergency Banking Act, Agricultural Adjustment, the National Industrial Recovery Act, the Public Works and Works Progress Administrations, the Civilian Conservation Corps, the Tennesee Valley Authority. These were followed in the Second New Deal by the Wagner Act and Social Security, and – after three decades, in Lyndon Johnson’s Great Society, by Medicare and Medicaid. But there is more to it.
The Reconstruction Finance Corporation had been created under Herbert Hoover, with limited purposes. Roosevelt greatly expanded them, investing vast powers in Jesse H. Jones of Houston, Texas – a man who was intimately familiar with the rise of oil [8]. Jones set up the RFC in the regional branches of the Federal Reserve System and proceeded to fund waves of infrastructure investment relevant to oil: 600,000 miles of paved roads (paved mostly with asphalt), a thousand airfields, the Bay Bridge, Triborough Bridge, Lincoln Tunnel and more [9], including diesel-electric locomotives for the railroads [10] and an oil-fired aircraft carrier for the Navy. By the end of the 1930s the infrastructure was in place, the technological uncertainties had been resolved, and the path had been cleared toward the highways-and-suburbs economy that would flourish (for better or worse) from the 1950s to the 1970s.
An energy-entropy interpretation of the Great Crash, the Depression and the New Deal thus achieves what no other effort has been able to manage: it integrates all three events into a coherent whole, and ties the financial economy to the real economy. It is consistent with Keynes’s core insight – a collapse of profit expectations leading to a collapse of capital investment – but without the seeming randomness of a purely psychological collapse of “animal spirits.” It shows why the private economy could not recover on its own, refuting the monetarist view of Friedman and Schwartz, and equally today’s libertarians whose faith is in the self-stabilizing properties of private markets and reversion to trend of growth statistics. It shows precisely in what way the technical contribution of the New Deal was decisive in charting the path forward – whether one likes the oil-based economy that resulted is an entirely separate question. And it casts light on the crisis of the 1970s, when oil suddenly became scarce and expensive once again, and also on the recovery since 2009, when fracking restored the energy economy of the United States – for the time being.
Entropy Economics and the AI Boom: Lessons from the Great Crash
As the centennial of the Great Crash approaches, the United States is once again caught up in a boom, fueled by the artificial intelligence investments of the “magnificent seven” companies whose valuations now dominate the stock market. Many of the conditions identified by my father for the 1920s are readily recognizable: the bad income distribution, the bad corporate structure (leverage and concentrated power), a bad (highly concentrated) banking system, the dubious foreign balance – and, once again, a “poor state of economic intelligence.” By my father’s lights, we should be all set, give or take, for a repeat of the Crash and an ensuing Depression.
What, then, about precipitating factors? In particular, what about energy, and what about the specific properties and characteristics of artificial intelligence and data centers?
At present writing, the energy economy is fraught with geological, geopolitical and technological unknowns. For the United States, oil and natural gas have been abundant since 2009 – quite unexpectedly so. Thanks to the advent of fracking and the high productivity of the Permian Basin, the United States has become once again the world’s largest producer of petroleum and a major exporter of natural gas. How long this will last is a first unknown: the well count has been declining, and production may have peaked in 2025. The future of the price is a second unknown: in particular whether it will remain above the level required to encourage new exploration and more intensive drilling. The effect of such a price on every other form of economic activity in the country is a third unknown, though it is obvious that the American economy, writ large, runs on low oil prices.
What does not appear to be in the offing is the much-vaunted “energy transition” that will leave oil, gas and coal in the dustbins of economic history. It is true that substantial increments from wind and solar have been achieved, and a modest revival of nuclear power may be getting underway. But Jevons’ Law, now more than 150 years old, states that for every new source of energy brought on-line, new uses also appear, and the end result is that all forms of energy are used, in ever greater quantities. Jevons’ Law has not been repealed, and the world today uses more coal, oil, gas, hydro and nuclear power than it ever did before [11]. Periodically since the dawn of the atomic age, new forms of energy have been trumpeted as likely to undermine the fossil fuel economy in the same way that oil broke the back of coal after 1929. There is no obvious sign of any such thing in the near future.
Artificial intelligence appears on the scene, first and foremost, as a voracious user of existing energy and a competitor with all other uses for electrical infrastructure: power plants, transmission lines, transformers, and so forth. As such, it is a vector of cost inflation aimed at the heart of the American household, which lives in a home built for, adapted to, and reliant upon reliable, cheap electrical power. If, as seems likely, the boom in data centers outruns the growth of electrical supply, the only way to meet the demand will be to raise the price of electricity to all other users. This will deepen inequalities and increase the precarity of life for American householders, who cannot easily adjust their electrical consumption. In simplest terms, AI emerges as a competitor for the juice of life.
 
A second risk is that the AI boom will come to an end thanks to radical over-investment and over-production of a good (or is it a service?) for which there is insufficient economic use. This result would resemble the Internet crash of 2000, a shakeout that deflated the NASDAQ and drove a great many young and once-promising startups to the wall. But the Internet survived, and the associated infrastructure, though overbuilt at first, was eventually absorbed into the fabric of economic life. The recession of 2000 was not the Great Depression.
As artificial intelligence continues to develop, then one must ask, for what does it substitute? The answer is not far to seek: artificial intelligence is a cheap substitute for the real thing. Human intelligence is something that is cultivated slowly, at great expense, over time. It requires vast investment in fixed capital with limited alternative uses: schools, universities, libraries, training grounds, and more. In the neoliberal era, the predominant rationale for maintaining these institutions has been their contribution to the future earnings of the inmates, and their role in maintaining something called the “competitiveness” of the country. Both are undermined by AI. Perhaps the school and the university can be returned to their older roles as a form of luxury consumption good, in demand for their own sake in a society liberated from the pressures of routine mental labor. Perhaps, but it’s not likely – the more likely outcome is that these institutions will shrink, and many will disappear.
There is yet another possibility. It is a possibility that comes closer to the 1929 scenario and to its consequences for the Depression. Suppose that something were to emerge that, all of a sudden, undercut the expected profitability of the entire AI sector, rendering the massive investment in data centers as stranded assets, like the steel mills of Youngstown or (still earlier) the textile mills of Lowell and Lawrence? And suppose, further, that that were something that emerged from outside the United States, so that it did not presage a later era of growth and prosperity in line with the new paradigm?
That possibility exists. There are hints of it already. They come, of course, from the People’s Republic of China. Should it happen – and it could – that the Chinese outplay the Americans in the AI race – then everything that is solid (and well, nothing is) would turn to dust. Watch for it.
The collapse of oil prices and the collapse of stocks, 1929
Note that the oil news and the stock collapse reported on October 24 and 29 reflect events on the previous day. October 24 and especially October 29 were worse.

An amusing physics side-note — carbon-oxidation, nuclear fission based and photoelectric energy sources are all, at root, powered by gravitation, the first and last via slow fusion processes in stellar cores during normal stellar evolution and the middle via fast fusion processes during the collapse of massive stellar cores.

I wonder to what uses the stranded assets of the AI over-investment boom will be put.
strictly speaking, sunlight does not require an energy source in the Sun’s core; the heat of the Sun’s surface is a purely gravitational phenomenon. Stars (and gravitation-dominated systems in general) have negative heat capacity — they get hotter as they lose energy and contract. The fusion energy source at the core slows this process by providing an energy source that compensates for the energy lost through radiation from the Sun’s surface.

This is an intriguing perspective. I have one question mark though:
Yet none of this necessarily had to end, least of all with a resounding crash, over a few days in October, 1929. Oil was available, but it was relatively scarce, and therefore expensive.
The site of the EIA has a page on the “250 year history of US energy consumption” showing that just at the time of the 1929 crash, oil consumption (measured in BTU) already represented about 45% of the coal consumption (also in BTU) — which was the main source of energy in the USA at that time — and was also 50% above wood, hydropower, and natural gas combined.
In what sense was oil “scarce”, then?
I’m guessing the stress was on “relatively” rather than “scarce”.
Well yes, but in 1929 oil already represented 25% of the total energy consumption (hydro and wood about 8%, gas about 8%, all the rest coal), so even “relatively”, I have difficulties to deem oil to have been scarce — especially since its share had been continuously increasing since about 1915, whereas the share of coal had been continuously decreasing since 1920.
The distinction to draw here is between the flow, which was certainly increasing and had been, as noted, for over a decade by 1929, and the stock — that is, the reserves.  It was the reserves that expanded rapidly with the discoveries of the late 1920s, and into the 1930s.  And it was the reserves that would govern the future.  
And the key event of October 1929 was the resulting drastic cut in the price, which was known immediately in New York.

He empasizes New Deal legislation at the expense of WWII as the economic driver that pulled the US out of depression. WWII also set the stage of the Golden Age of US prosperity that followed during the 1950s-early 70s.

44,2026-10-30,"Author of the Small Business Insider newsletter
A traditional job typically comes with a regular paycheck and benefits — a measure of stability and predictability.
Entrepreneurship offers neither. It comes with risk and uncertainty. There is potential for a big payoff, but no guarantees.
Popsmith CEO Tal Moore put it bluntly: ""You have to be insane to be an entrepreneur,"" he told Business Insider. ""Because there's so much risk. There's so much turbulence.""
He and his cofounder nearly ran out of money while building their high-end stovetop popcorn popper.
""I ended up selling $600,000 worth of my personal stock to keep us afloat,"" Moore said.
It was stressful, of course, but Moore felt he had to find a way through — not only because he had invested heavily in Popsmith, but because friends and family had, too.
""They bet on me. They bet on my brand,"" he said. ""If you lose your own money, that's one thing. But there was just no f---ing way that I was going to lose my friends' and family's money.""
Kyle Ray had a different source of motivation. In high school, one of his teachers told him he'd end up working at McDonald's.
Rather than feeling discouraged, Ray used the comment as fuel.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
He never finished college, but he did turn a window-cleaning side hustle he started with $100 into a six-figure business, Geek Window Cleaning, which now serves hundreds of clients across multiple Texas cities.
Ray said he had doubts ""all the time"" that the business would work, but wanting to disprove that teacher meant adopting a failure-is-not-an-option mindset.
""I'm going to make this work,"" he decided. ""I'm just going to figure it out.""
When Moore and his business partner had just two weeks of cash runway left, saving the business meant getting attention and moving product without spending much money.
""At this point, I had to throw some Hail Marys,"" Moore said.
His first idea, which he called ""harebrained,"" was to send poppers directly to the CEOs of Williams-Sonoma and Crate & Barrel. He'd designed the product with those retailers in mind, but couldn't get their attention.
""I know my popper will sell on their shelves,"" he thought. ""I just have to get it in their hands.""
Moore bought the most upscale, high-quality paper he could find at Kinko's, handwrote notes to both CEOs, and ""blind-shipped"" poppers to them. Within two months, buying teams from both companies had reached out, he said.
Then came Oprah.
Moore had no idea how to get on Oprah's annual ""Favorite Things"" list — ""it's just like a total black box,"" he said — until he spotted an opening on LinkedIn. A junior editor at Oprah Daily had posted about popcorn, so Moore sent her a cold message. She replied, requested a popper, and eventually helped get the product in front of Oprah.
An Oprah Daily editor told Moore that the team considers about 15,000 products each year before Oprah makes her final selections. In 2024, Popsmith made the cut.
After the list was announced, the company sold out of every popper within weeks. Popsmith went on to sell out three more times over the next five months.
As an entrepreneur, ""there's a pressure and a will to win,"" Moore said. ""There's an endless drive that just never goes away. I wake up hungry, and I will not stop.""
Like Moore, Ray has a refusal-to-quit mentality. He views entrepreneurship as a highway without an off-ramp — no Plan B waiting at the next exit.
""If there is no exit, then there's just one path,"" he said. ""And you just have to get through it.""
That doesn't mean every business will succeed. In fact, many won't. But Ray believes entrepreneurs need to be willing to take risks, potentially look foolish, fail, and move on to the next idea if necessary.
Often, he said, fear of failure — and other people's judgment — is the real obstacle.
At the core of his mindset is a willingness to care less about both.
""If Geek fails now, I'm going to look stupid, but it's like, 'Okay, cool. What's next?' Like, I don't care,"" he said. ""And the earlier you can develop that carelessness — the 'I don't care if I look stupid' or 'I can fall flat on my face. I am still going to go for it' — I think if you get to that point, anybody can go out and be an entrepreneur.""
That mindset can be learned, he said. Like any other skill, from swinging a tennis racket to ballroom dancing, it takes time, consistent work, and patience. Ray recommends three steps:
1. Start with books. They're cheap — or free at the library. Ray's top picks include ""Inner Excellence"" and ""Can't Hurt Me."" Moore prefers biographies of entrepreneurs and business leaders, from Warren Buffett and Richard Branson to John D. Rockefeller.
2. Find someone who is already doing what you want to do at a high level. Then ask questions, Ray said: ""What are you doing that I'm not? What am I doing that's preventing me from breaking through?""
3. Join a mastermind or hire a coach. Ray has multiple.
""When there's something I don't know, or I need to learn, I start to look for coaches,"" he said. ""It's like a wormhole, right? If you can find somebody that knows something about where you want to be, they can help you collapse time and just jump forward to that spot.""

          Intercept staff take stock of the big spenders this election cycle.        



It’s a superlative achieved again and again: The current election cycle is the most expensive on record, according to AdImpact, likely to be surpassed only by the next one. North of $16 billion have been raised ahead of Tuesday’s looming elections, with ad spending in House and Senate races topping $4 billion. Mega-donors this election season have overwhelmingly backed Republicans with contributions largely flowing from AI, crypto, and online gambling interests.
 
The Intercept tracked how super PAC spending is flowing into the midterms and how growing public skepticism of special-interest money in politics is affecting campaign strategies. Hundreds of candidates are still taking donations from the American Israel Public Affairs Committee and its super PAC, the United Democracy Project, despite public sentiment turning against the pro-Israel lobby. 
“The data overall reveals that [AIPAC is] still spending a ton of money on a lot of races, and more often than not, their candidates are still winning,” Intercept managing editor Maia Hibbett told The Intercept Briefing. “It is still quite meaningful money.”
Artificial intelligence super PACs have also poured tens of millions of dollars into this election cycle, most of it on just a few races in the primaries. The two leading AI super PAC networks, affiliated with OpenAI or Anthropic, dropped a whopping $50.7 million on the primaries, but are noticeably spending much less in the lead-up to the general election, $8.5 million — likely responding to public backlash in the same way that AIPAC has.
“The polls are just so startling on the public’s transformation on AI and data centers over the last year. … The entire life cycle of these AI super PACs, which started about a year ago, has followed that evolution of the public’s opinion,” said Intercept politics reporter Matt Sledge. “One of the things that’s been really interesting in the general election races is … candidates [trying] to paint themselves as the more pro-AI safety, pro-AI regulation candidate.”
This week on the podcast, host Jessica Washington speaks to Hibbett and Sledge about the spending trends in the most expensive election cycle to date and unpack the intended impact.
“No matter which party gets control of either or both houses of Congress, there will be a lot of people elected next week who got there on the backs of Big Tech and special interests,” said Hibbett.
“They’re backing a lot of people in safe seats who don’t really have a tight election,” Sledge added. “The goal may just be to buy themselves a few friends in Congress, because sometimes in Congress, it’s just as good to have a few people who can stop regulation as it is to have people who can shape and actually pass laws.”
For more, listen to the full conversation of The Intercept Briefing on Apple Podcasts, Spotify, YouTube, or wherever you listen to podcasts.
Jessica Washington: Welcome to The Intercept Briefing, I’m Jessica Washington, politics reporter at The Intercept. 
On Tuesday, voters will head to the polls to cast their ballots in the most expensive election cycle ever in terms of campaign ad spending, even outpacing past presidential elections. That’s according to AdImpact. 
North of $16 billion has been raised this cycle, with ad spending in House and Senate races topping $4 billion. Billionaire Elon Musk has dumped hundreds of millions into races across the country, using his enormous personal wealth to sway the election in favor of Republicans. Musk, who has a home and multiple businesses in Texas, has taken a particular interest in the state’s Senate race between Republican candidate Ken Paxton and Democratic candidate James Talarico.
Most of the money from Musk and other donors has flowed not to candidates, but to powerful super PACs representing a variety of different special interests.  
That super PAC spending is the subject of my colleagues, Intercept politics reporter Matt Sledge and senior politics reporter Akela Lacy’s new project, tracking how money is flowing into the midterms. 
Maia Hibbett, managing editor for The Intercept, and Matt Sledge join me now to talk about the project and how big money is shaping the midterms. 
Matt, Maia, welcome back to The Intercept Briefing 
Maia Hibbett: Thanks for having us. 
Matt Sledge: Thank you for having us, Jessie. 
JW: Maia, I want to start with you. As a part of the super PAC project, The Intercept tracked how millions of dollars flow through the pro-Israel lobby in the United States.
What did the reporting reveal about how the American Israel Public Affairs Committee and affiliated groups like the United Democracy Project mask their influence in U.S. elections in a political landscape where AIPAC’s contributions and endorsements have become largely politically toxic? 
MH: The reporting showed that AIPAC and its affiliated super PAC UDP are a little bit weakened, but they’re still pretty dominant.
The narrative out of this midterm cycle, especially during the primaries, was largely focused on a few high-profile races that an AIPAC-backed candidate lost — and that was a huge shock. These big upsets were AIPAC or UDP or a related group that, we can talk about a little bit later, had poured money into, and then the candidate didn’t succeed.
But if you look at it in the aggregate, what the data overall reveals is that they’re still spending a ton of money on a lot of races, and more often than not, their candidates are still winning. It is still quite meaningful money. And even though AIPAC has been discussed as going underground, they are still directly invested in a lot of races.
JW: I want to get into AIPAC’s shifting strategy and their latest announcement on that. But first, Matt, I wanted to talk about your reporting. AI super PACs have poured tens of millions of dollars into this election cycle. 
 
What do we know about the people and companies behind these super PACs? This may seem obvious, but what are they hoping to gain with this level of spending? Are there specific examples of friendly legislation these varied interest groups are hoping their candidates will see through if they win?
MS: To answer your second question, I think it’s really more about setting the tone for future regulation at the federal level. You have two big super PAC networks. One is affiliated with OpenAI, and they broadly favor a laxer regulatory scheme for AI. Then there’s another network that’s affiliated with Anthropic that paints itself as supporting a stricter regulatory scheme.
“It’s really more about setting the tone for future [AI] regulation at the federal level.”
Skeptics will point out that these networks are both backed by big AI companies. So at the end of the day, they’re probably going to favor regulation that is favorable to the AI companies. I get into this a little bit in my story, but, just in the last few months, there’s also been this constellation of other smaller groups that are sometimes backed by individual employees at these firms or just by people concerned about AI that are pushing for stricter regulatory regimes and aren’t as directly connected to one of the big companies.
JW: Matt, I’m curious about the impact on voters here. CNBC analysis from early October found that mega-donors this election season were overwhelmingly backing Republicans with contributions largely flowing from AI, crypto, and online gambling interests: “Fourteen of the 20 largest donors and donor groups this election cycle have exclusively or nearly exclusively backed GOP candidates or aligned groups.” 
Matt, are we seeing voters become more receptive to Republicans as a result of all this ad spending? Is it working on the voter base? 
MS: It’s always hard to tease out the impact of an individual super PAC or industry, because you have to keep in mind that a lot of the time, if you’re a crypto super PAC, or an AI super PAC — or more recently, a prediction market super PAC — they are not running pro-crypto or pro-AI ads.
They are running ads that look very similar to everybody else’s. Often, they’re doing negative campaigning that campaigns don’t want to do themselves, and they have such vague names that they’re not associated with crypto or AI. So I do think ads have an impact on the races, but overall what we’re seeing in polls is that it’s not a great environment for Republicans.
So it may have served, just writ large, to potentially soften the blow to Republicans this time around. It does help them to have this many corporate allies, but it may still be overwhelmed by a blue tide. 
JW: That makes a lot of sense in terms of just, this is a bad environment for Republicans. They have a president who is unpopular. We have an economy that is not amazing right now. It makes a lot of sense that despite all this ad spending, the baseline of the election is not in favor of Republicans. 
Maia, I wanted to get back to something that you had said a little bit earlier about AIPAC and their shifting strategy. AIPAC recently announced that they would no longer officially endorse candidates or give money directly to campaigns. 
Were we seeing an early signal of shifting strategies from AIPAC during the 2026 midterm elections? And what does this change actually mean for AIPAC’s influence on our elections?
MH: I’m so glad you asked, because we actually noted early in the 2026 cycle that AIPAC was starting to retreat and change its strategy. Our colleague Akela Lacy wrote a story in December 2025, and the headline was, “AIPAC is Retreating From Endorsements and Election Spending. It Won’t Give Up Its Influence.” I encourage anyone to go back and read that story because it was really prescient about what we would see in the cycle to come, which is that AIPAC used to do all of its spending behind closed doors.
 
In 2022, it made a big splash when it started doing direct spending in that year’s midterms. The group had helped fund some anti-Bernie Sanders ads during the 2020 presidential campaign, but it was 2022 when it went all in, rather than routing its donations through other groups or through dark-money pathways, it just started giving contributions to candidates. 
After it spent so much money in that midterm cycle and in last cycle‘s 2024 races, and as public sentiment started to turn against the pro-Israel lobby’s interests and people became very critical of the U.S. support for genocide in Gaza, AIPAC started to essentially return to its roots.
I think this is really interesting because in the press it has been talked about as a very new strategy. But really, it’s more that AIPAC is returning to the way it used to do business. 
JW: Is that as effective as a strategy — the old model that they had? Should we expect that it’ll have just as much influence on elections as they did previously?
MH: It’s a good question. I don’t know how effective it will be as a strategy. There’s a lot more scrutiny on AIPAC now than there was a few years ago, and so it’s much more difficult for them to hide what they’re doing because there are journalists like us who are going to try to follow the money and say, maybe you gave to something that’s called Elect Chicago Women, but it’s actually from UDP, which is the super PAC for AIPAC.
I think in some cases, they can’t get away from the public perception that now exists. But on the other hand, there are many voters who might not really pay attention to the name of the PAC that sponsored the ad that they saw, and then go look it up and see where the money came from. So it remains to be seen how effective it will be.
“It’s much more difficult for them to hide what they’re doing because there are journalists like us who are going to try to follow the money.”
JW: Not everyone is reading The Intercept before voting, as they should, but it’s fine.
Matt, I wanted to get back to you because there’s a lot happening with these big AI super PACs. You noticed in your reporting that, after spending a lot in the primaries in New York in particular, but in other places as well, the big AI super PACs have gone pretty much quiet in the general election.
Are AI super PACs responding to public backlash in the same way that AIPAC is? What have you observed in terms of why they’ve gone a little bit quieter? 
MS: In response to your question about backlash, I think the answer is yes.
“It’s just a dramatic decline in spending from the primaries to the generals — even though they still have money they can tap.”
They’re on this kind of accelerated timeline going through what AIPAC went through over the course of five years but essentially in five or six months. The polls are just so startling on the public’s transformation on AI and data centers over the last year. It’s just such a rapid change in public opinion, and the entire life cycle of these AI super PACs, which started about a year ago, has followed that evolution of the public’s opinion.
It’s just a dramatic decline in spending from the primaries to the generals — even though they still have money they can tap. Leading the Future, which is the OpenAI-aligned network, they started first, and then the Anthropic-aligned network, Public First, they started a few months later as a response to the Leading the Future network.
So it seems like, from what we’ve been able to tell, Leading the Future scaled back on spending as the general election came about, and Public First responded in turn and also scaled back its operations. That doesn’t mean they’re not spending anything, and we have seen a slight uptick in the final few days of this election when ads are probably just less effective than they would have been a couple weeks ago.
“If Darline Graham narrowly gets away with winning her race in South Carolina of all places … then you can still say that Leading the Future and the AI industry played a pretty important role in the general race.”
One really notable race is now South Carolina, where Leading the Future is helping prop up Sen. Darline Graham, who hasn’t been a great fundraiser on her own. So there’s actually still a world where AI plays a decisive role in this election in terms of control of the Senate. 
If Darline Graham narrowly gets away with winning her race in South Carolina of all places, and that narrowly hands control of the Senate back to the Republicans, then you can still say that Leading the Future and the AI industry played a pretty important role in the general race.
JW: Just to be clear, you’re referring to Darline Graham, Lindsey Graham’s sister, correct? 
MS: That’s correct. 



      We’re independent of corporate interests — and powered by members. Join us.

            Will you take the next step to support our independent journalism by becoming a member of The Intercept?          

By signing up, I agree to receive emails from The Intercept and to the Privacy Policy and Terms of Use.
JW: Maia, speaking of Big Tech, you also edited a story from Intercept reporter Jonah Valdez looking at how tech leaders, including John Hering, are dumping millions into the LA city attorney race to block Marissa Roy, a DSA-backed candidate, from winning. What is going on here — why such an interest in a race most people are likely not paying any attention to?
MH: City attorneys are one of those jobs that, as you said, a lot of voters don’t pay attention to them. I think a lot of people in cities all over the country maybe couldn’t tell you who the top lawyer of whatever city they live in even is. But this is a job that is largely tasked with enforcing regulations and laws and making companies that might be based in that city or might operate in that city comply with them.
Marissa Roy, this DSA-backed candidate, is currently a deputy attorney general for the state of California, and she’s one of the people who helped the state prosecute this historic Meta settlement where Meta ended up having to pay $17 billion for harms to children using its technology. 
We don’t know exactly why John Hering and other tech leaders, like the founders of Ring and Tinder and investors in Anthropic and SpaceX, are spending so heavily in this race, but you might be able to conclude that they’re interested in preventing a person who just helped carry out this huge settlement against Meta from becoming the city attorney of LA.
Some of them are funding a group that’s called Neighbors for John McKinney, which is backing the opponent in the race. John McKinney is the other candidate, and he is much less likely to win, but is propped up now by a lot of spending. They frame him as a tough-on-crime candidate; they say he’s going to restore order to LA, help clean it up is the idea.
Like Matt was saying, often these tech investments in races don’t actually foreground the interests of Big Tech, and they don’t say explicitly, “We want to stop this person from coming into office because we’re worried they’re going to regulate our industry.” Instead, they find a different issue and spend money on that.
It’s also worth noting that John Hering, his group, frames John McKinney as a lifelong Democrat and very anti-MAGA, is the language they use. But John Hering himself is a big ally of Elon Musk. He has been an eager investor in his companies. He said Musk was an “American hero” this year, and he has spent pretty heavily also to support Musk’s America PAC and the Trump campaign after previously spending on the Hillary Clinton presidential campaign.
Like many Silicon Valley types, he’s had an evolution. And although he lives in San Francisco, he also spent in favor of Spencer Pratt for LA mayor.
JW: Wow. Yeah, Big Tech has really come a long way from the ’08 years in terms of their relationship with Democrats and Republicans, and propping up Spencer Pratt.
Matt, I want to bring you in and get your perspective and kind of big picture. What has made this election cycle so popular for Big Tech? 
MS: When it comes to AI and crypto and the prediction markets, it’s knowing that the next few years are really going to be critical for them in terms of regulation. These are all fairly young industries, and the rules haven’t been set yet. The Trump administration is doing its best to set them, but Congress has not had a final word on any of those industries.
 
They see a chance to shape the rules in a way that favors them, and they’re just using the old Washington playbook of spending a bunch of money on campaigns and a bunch of money on lobbyists to try to shape the rules in their favor.
JW: Is this just also a matter of them having more money to play with than they’ve had before? Is this something tech probably would have done years ago if they’d had this access to capital? Or is this really, like you said, just they’re aware that there is existential risks to their industry if they don’t?
MS: Tech for the last few decades has had a lot of money. It’s just been a huge part of the U.S. economy. But I think this move into more regulated spaces is maybe what’s newer. 
 
When Microsoft is creating an internet browser or a word processor, that creates potential antitrust issues, and they have to spend political money in that sense. But they’re not playing in the same kind of highly regulated financial spaces, and nobody ever thought that Clippy posed an existential risk to humanity. So we are moving into new territory when it comes to techs. 
JW: I miss Clippy. That is my one comment on that. 
I want to get both of you back in here. We’ve been talking about some of the biggest donors in American politics today, and a few special interest groups in particular who’ve obviously been experiencing public backlash — AIPAC, AI, and data centers. 
What do you make of this new record-level spending in a midterm election in particular? We’ll obviously have more answers on this next week, but from what you’ve observed so far, is it having the intended impact on voters? Maia, let’s start with you. 
MH: I think to use the cop-out that we keep coming back to, we won’t really know if it worked with voters until after the results next week. But it’s clear in this midterm cycle and maybe this is a reminder of kind of a perpetual truth in, when it comes to money in politics, that these big spenders are always going to fund candidates on both sides of the aisle. Often election narratives and questions about special interests will really look to one party or the other and try to map this kind of spending onto partisan issues. 
While some of that certainly happens, I think no matter which party gets control of either or both houses of Congress, there will be a lot of people elected next week who got there on the backs of Big Tech and special interests. 
That is something that voters are more and more aware of and more and more frustrated by, and we did see a lot of rejection of that kind of big-money spending in the primaries. But ultimately, I don’t know how much that narrative has changed. 
JW: Matt, I know you’ve seen some specific examples of this impacting elections. Can you talk about that a little bit? 
MS: One of the things that’s been really interesting in the general election races is this race from candidates to paint themselves as the more pro-AI safety, pro-AI regulation candidate.
I’ve done some reporting in Michigan’s 7th [Congressional] District, where a co-founder of the Sunrise Movement is trying to unseat an incumbent Republican, and he has from the start really run on being an anti-data center candidate, campaigning with local activists in small towns of a few thousand people.
His Republican opponent is now also trying to paint himself as a data center skeptic — using different language, more language about local control than about big, bad companies. 
But there’s been such a backlash that we’ve seen some changing tactics from the big AI super PACs in the runoff. One has donated money to a nonprofit that’s more focused on building support for data centers. Good luck with that, I guess.
And then another, the Anthropic-aligned network, has essentially handed off money to a pro-Scott Wiener super PAC in California that already existed and already has funding from other tech leaders in the San Francisco area. But that donation has generated this whole fight between Scott Wiener and his opponent, Connie Chan, over who is the more pro-AI regulation candidate now. So it’s a lot of candidates fighting to be the more pro-regulation candidate.
Just thinking back to your question about the effect on voters, I think for the super PACs and for their funders, the impact on candidates may ultimately be more important, on elected officials. They’re backing a lot of people in safe seats who don’t really have a tight election. The goal may just be to buy themselves a few friends in Congress, because sometimes in Congress, it’s just as good to have a few people who can stop regulation as it is to have people who can shape and actually pass laws.
JW: That’s a good point. Speaking of the election, which we know is just days away, what are you two going to be following closely next week? Maia, I want to start with you.
MH: There’s obviously a lot to follow next week, and more races than we can keep track of. One I think that will be very interesting that I was thinking about in terms of AIPAC spending — when Matt just mentioned this thing about AI groups backing candidates who are not in close races — is, of course, the Senate race in Maine. 
One of the interesting takeaways we found in this AIPAC project is that in the primaries when AIPAC was spending, as it does in, in various races, one of the top recipients of its money was Susan Collins — who at the time did not have a primary challenger, but it spent heavily to support her primary campaign.
So we’ll obviously watch the Collins–Jackson race in Maine. We have a reporter going there. Matt’s going to go to Michigan. We also have a reporter going to Texas to track some of the big Senate races. Depending on how the results look, I think we’ll pretty quickly be pivoting to accountability for, coverage for the people who are elected, who, maybe entered office vowing not to take corporate PAC money or not to take special-interest money, and to keep an eye on those now Congress members and see if they vote like it or if they continue to have that policy once they’ve been elected.
JW: And Matt, what are you following? 
MS: As Maia mentioned, I will be in Michigan. Super interested in that 7th District race that I mentioned between Will Lawrence and Tom Barrett. But then the race everybody’s going to be spinning and talking about for weeks probably: the Mike Rogers/Abdul El-Sayed race. It just is going to carry so much narrative heft going forward about what the voters were trying to say. 
Where are you going to be, Jessie? What are you going to be up to? 
JW: I will be in D.C., and I’ll be looking at what it means to have yet another election with the National Guard in town and essentially in an occupied city.
I’ll also be looking into what it would mean for the district to have a socialist mayor, Janeese Lewis George, who is almost certain to win on Tuesday, particularly when it comes to ICE and the Metropolitan Police Department’s collaboration with ICE. That’s going to be something I’m really keeping an eye on, not just on Election Day, but going forward to see if she can implement some of these really bold ideas that she’s proposing in a city that’s very constricted in what it can actually do because of federal law.
But that’s enough previewing from me. We’re going to leave it there. Maia, Matt, thank you so much for joining me on The Intercept Briefing. 
MH: Thanks again. 
MS: Thanks for having us. 
JW: And thank you for listening. Next week we’ll be back in your feed early with a special midterm election episode, so stay tuned.
That does it for this episode. We want to hear from you. Tell us what you’re following or want to see more coverage of. Email us at podcasts@theintercept.com or leave us a voice mail at 530-POD-CAST, that’s 530-763-2278.
This episode was produced by Laura Flynn. Jordan Uhl is our social media producer. Ben Muessig is our editor-in-chief. Maia Hibbett is our managing editor. Nara Shin is our copy editor. William Stanton mixed our show. Legal review by David Bralow. Slip Stream provided our theme music.
This show and our reporting at The Intercept do not exist without you. Your donation, no matter the amount, makes a real difference. Keep our investigations free and fearless at theintercept.com/join. 
And if you haven’t already, please subscribe to The Intercept Briefing wherever you listen to podcasts. Do leave us a rating or a review, it helps other listeners find our reporting.
Until next time, I’m Jessica Washington.


IT’S EVEN WORSE THAN WE THOUGHT.
What we’re seeing right now from Donald Trump is a full-on authoritarian takeover of the U.S. government. 
This is not hyperbole.
Court orders are being ignored. MAGA loyalists have been put in charge of the military and federal law enforcement agencies. The Department of Government Efficiency has stripped Congress of its power of the purse. News outlets that challenge Trump have been banished or put under investigation.
Yet far too many are still covering Trump’s assault on democracy like politics as usual, with flattering headlines describing Trump as “unconventional,” “testing the boundaries,” and “aggressively flexing power.” 
The Intercept has long covered authoritarian governments, billionaire oligarchs, and backsliding democracies around the world. We understand the challenge we face in Trump and the vital importance of press freedom in defending democracy.
IT’S BEEN A DEVASTATING year for journalism — the worst in modern U.S. history.
We have a president with utter contempt for truth aggressively using the government’s full powers to dismantle the free press. Corporate news outlets have cowered, becoming accessories in Trump’s project to create a post-truth America. Right-wing billionaires have pounced, buying up media organizations and rebuilding the information environment to their liking.
In this most perilous moment for democracy, The Intercept is fighting back. But to do so effectively, we need to grow.
That’s where you come in. Will you help us expand our reporting capacity in time to hit the ground running in 2026?
I’M BEN MUESSIG, The Intercept’s editor-in-chief. It’s been a devastating year for journalism — the worst in modern U.S. history.
We have a president with utter contempt for truth aggressively using the government’s full powers to dismantle the free press. Corporate news outlets have cowered, becoming accessories in Trump’s project to create a post-truth America. Right-wing billionaires have pounced, buying up media organizations and rebuilding the information environment to their liking.
In this most perilous moment for democracy, The Intercept is fighting back. But to do so effectively, we need to grow.
That’s where you come in. Will you help us expand our reporting capacity in time to hit the ground running in 2026?

Tech > Services & Software > AI
With Google now injecting ""sponsored"" links directly into AI follow-up queries, traffic to online publishers will suffer even more.
Senior Editor
October 29, 2026, 8:43 pm ET
If you happened to listen to Alphabet’s quarterly earnings call on Wednesday, you might have heard that searches are up, but ad revenue is down. According to Google Chief Business Officer Philipp Schindler, search ad revenue growth slowed in the third quarter of 2026 because user queries are migrating to AI Overviews and AI Mode, which suffer from “lower monetization.”
You actually know how this works. When you type a question into Google, a summarized AI response often appears immediately, reducing the classic ad-clicking behavior further down on the page, which generates Google’s core revenue.
Now, the tech giant is expanding how and where we see ads when using AI Mode during searches. Google is scaling ads in AI Mode to 10 new countries (including Australia, Canada, India, Kenya and Singapore), with plans to launch in Brazil, Mexico and the UK later this year. 
More from CNET
In the US, Google is launching what it calls “multiturn ads,” allowing ads to appear directly within follow-up queries in AI Mode when a search or conversation has “commercial relevance.” Take, for example, if you start a search with “How does a smart fridge work,” and follow up with “What are the best-selling smart fridges?” You could then see refrigerator ads in sections labeled “sponsored.”
Google said that “multiturn ads” can better target what a user wants in a product because AI Mode gathers more information during a back-and-forth conversation. Ads can also appear on initial AI Mode queries, not just after follow-up questions, and sometimes ads won’t appear at all if there doesn’t appear to be purchase intent, according to Google.
But as Google relies more on AI Mode and AI Overview, there’ll be less reason to click on content links “below the fold,” meaning content that sits out of view until a reader scrolls down a web page. 
Lily Ray, a well-known SEO expert and founder of Algorythmic, told CNET that it’s going to become harder to use Google to find articles from preferred publishers and articles you want to read directly from a website. “You’ll have to dig deeper and look outside of Google’s products,” Ray said via email. 
According to Ray, Google’s AI tools aren’t designed to give you the exact link you’re looking for. Still, you don’t have to rely on AI Mode or AI Overviews to get search results. You can also scroll down to the search results the old-fashioned way or use a web filter to use Google as a list of links. She also recommends setting up your favorite publications as “preferred sources” (instructions here) so you can see more of those pages cited in your personalized results.
“Unless you’re satisfied with AI answers directly on Google, you’re going to need to start evolving your own search behavior to find the websites you’re looking for,” Ray said. 
Google is still the largest search engine in the world, accounting for nearly 90% of the global search engine market. In fact, the company said its year-over-year query volume growth in the third quarter was the fastest over the past five years.
But beginning with the launch of AI Overviews in May 2024, Google’s reliance on AI has slowed advertising. 
The impact has been even more devastating for online publishers — ranging from major newspapers and digital media sites to brands and trade associations — driving an unprecedented decline in website traffic. AI Overviews now appear on nearly half of Google search queries, with a corresponding 61% drop in raw traffic volume to other pages. Google is effectively scraping publisher information, summarizing it and satisfying the user before they leave the search results page. That’s why it’s often referred to as the “zero-click” crisis.
“AI Mode and AI Overviews answer questions directly in Google’s interface, so there’s less reason for people to click on publishers,” Ray said. 
AI Overviews are summary boxes at the top of a traditional search results page, while AI Mode is a conversational interface designed for ongoing dialogue and research. You can access AI Mode directly by selecting the AI Mode button at the far right of the search bar on the Google home page, or by selecting AI Mode in the horizontal menu near the top of other Google pages.
When you use AI Mode, you won’t get an AI Overview or a long list of links to other websites offering more information. Instead, AI Mode provides “more advanced reasoning and ways of interacting” with a conversational approach. For instance, if I use AI Mode and ask, “Where can I see the northern lights?” I get a map and a list of various locations with pros and cons for each. In regular search mode, I get an AI Overview at the top and, below that, pages of links to websites related to my query.
Ray said that with more ads, there’s going to be an even greater decline in organic click-through rates — in publishing speak, that’s the percentage of people who actually click on a natural, nonpaid search engine result, out of the total number of people who simply saw the headline on the page. Not only will the AI text answer your question, but Google will now aggressively route you toward advertisers rather than publisher links.
“Like many things in Google Search, it means that if you’re not participating in Google Ads, it’s going to be harder and harder to get that traffic organically,” said Ray.
