52,2026-10-30,"Brent crude prices are up more than 60 percent compared with pre-war levels, despite rebound in supply.
Save
Share
Oil flows out of the Gulf have increased substantially, easing the huge supply disruption caused by the United States/Israel war on Iran.
Crude exports from the Gulf averaged 24.6 million barrels per day (bpd) in the week to October 18, exceeding the pre-war average, according to market intelligence firm Kpler.
Yet, oil prices continue to rise.
Brent crude, the global benchmark, is trading at its highest prices since April, up more than 60 percent compared with before the war.
On Friday, Brent crude front-month futures – contracts for oil deliveries in December – rose more than 2 percent to briefly top $117 a barrel.
The climbing prices reflect anxiety about the possibility of renewed hostilities in the US war on Iran, dwindling oil stocks, and the increased costs of transporting supplies out of the region, according to market analysts.
“The market is doing exactly what it is supposed to do: It is telling us there is plenty of prompt oil, but there is no end to this war in sight – in fact … escalation is very likely,” Adi Imsirovic, a lecturer in energy systems at the University of Oxford, told Al Jazeera.
“Therefore, futures markets reflect that expectation,” Imsirovic, a veteran oil trader, said.
Oil producers have become increasingly adept at responding to the volatile climate in the Middle East to keep supplies flowing.
Facing the threat of Iranian attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz, shipping lines have established a shuttle system involving ship-to-ship transfers off Oman and the United Arab Emirates to reduce the distance any one vessel must travel in perilous waters.
Shuttle boats carry the oil through the strait and load it onto waiting tankers once they are out of the danger zone.
In August, more than 70 percent of the crude that crossed the strait did so via ship-to-ship transfers, according to Kpler.
Oil producers have also ramped up exports that bypass the strait altogether via Saudi Arabia’s East-West pipeline, which carries oil supplies from its eastern oil fields to its Yanbu port on the Red Sea in the west, and the United Arab Emirates’ Habshan-Fujairah pipeline.
As of September, 40 percent of exports from the Gulf were bypassing the strait, a conduit for one-fifth of global oil and liquefied natural gas supplies during peacetime, compared with 17 percent before the war, according to Kpler data.
Such workarounds have nonetheless come at a cost.
Freight costs have reached as much as $40 per barrel in recent weeks, about 10 times the usual rate, according to data from the London Stock Exchange Group, the surge driven by elevated insurance premiums in addition to logistical challenges.
October has been the deadliest month for attacks on commercial vessels since the start of the war, with 32 incidents logged by the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) as of Friday.
“So barrels are moving, but neither smoothly nor reliably. That should not be read as a sign of normalisation,” Isabel Figuerola-Ferretti, an expert in oil markets at Comillas Pontifical University in Madrid, Spain, told Al Jazeera.
“Delivering a barrel has become far more expensive,” Figuerola-Ferretti said.
While market intelligence data has shown exports reaching pre-war levels at various points in recent weeks, the longer-term supply trend is less clear.
Pavel Molchanov, an investment strategist at Raymond James & Associates, expressed scepticism that supply has returned to the pre-conflict trend.
“The IEA’s most recent production data – for September – shows OPEC Gulf production at 15.8 million bpd, which equates to 75 percent of the pre-war baseline,” Molchanov told Al Jazeera, referring to the International Energy Agency’s latest energy outlook.
“That slightly surpassed July’s 74 percent to reach the highest level since the start of the war, but it’s still a considerable distance from 100 percent.”
Meanwhile, oil stockpiles, a buffer during supply disruptions, are waning.
Observed inventories fell by 10 million barrels in September, taking the global stockpile to 509 million barrels below its pre-war level, according to the IEA.
“The market has moved to a chronic squeeze, driven by concrete supply risks: Hormuz transit, shipping and depleted inventories,” Figuerola-Ferretti said.
With no permanent end to the Iran war in sight, few analysts see prices returning to normal anytime soon.
Brent futures for delivery in October 2027 stood at more than $90 a barrel on Friday, in a sign that markets see normality as a long way off, still.
“When could prices return to $70? Not soon,” Figuerola-Ferretti said.
“It would need durable de-escalation, a return to normal transit and shipping conditions through Hormuz, rebuilt inventories and freight costs back to normal,” she added.
Molchanov, the investment strategist, said he did not believe supply could realistically exceed 85 percent of pre-war levels without a permanent diplomatic solution to the war.
“Once the political groundwork is in place, the next steps will be logistical: Demining Hormuz, bringing shipping crews back to work, and resuming production at currently shut-in oilfields,” he said.

53,2026-10-30,"A Wealth of Common Sense
Posted October 30, 2026 by Ben Carlson
AI is the biggest story in the market this year, this decade and probably this century.
Predicting the boom-bust nature of how this cycle will play out is impossible. That won’t stop people from trying, of course.
The funny thing is you see people are making predictions years out into the future with certainty when the pros can’t even predict what will happen in the next 3, 6 or 12 months.
Stock market earnings are a case in point.
This chart shows how 2026 earnings estimates have changed since the start of the year:
This isn’t earnings estimates 3, 5, 10 years into the future. This is just 2026 earnings!
Analysts estimated S&P 500 earnings of a little more than $310 when the year began. After a few earnings seasons came in much better than expected the year end estimate is now nearly 20% higher.
Not even close.
The S&P 500 is currently up around 12% on the year. Earnings have grown almost 30%. That means that valuations have fallen this year.
Here’s an interesting thought experiment based on these rapidly rising earnings numbers:
Many investors assume valuations go vertical during the market boom times. Instead they are falling. This chart shows what would have happened if forward P/E ratios had simply stayed the same. Stock market returns around the globe would have been much, much higher.
There are reasons this is happening.
Investors might be cautious that all this AI capex spending is inflating earnings and these numbers will come back down to earth. That could be possible.
It’s also true that it’s perfectly normal for there to be a massive disconnect between earnings and stock market returns.
Here’s a look at earnings growth and total returns on the S&P 500 by decade going back to the 1930s:
Sometimes there’s a relationship but it can often breakdown.
Earnings growth and stock prices were more or less in line with one another in the 1940s, 1960s, 2000s, 2010s and 2020s (thus far).
As bad as the stock market did in the 1930s, earnings were even worse. Earnings far outpaced the stock market in the 1970s. The stock market was up far more than profits in the 1950s. Same thing in the 1980s and 1990s in terms of growth.
These divergences can be even greater in any given year:
A wide spread between earnings growth and stock market returns looks to be the norm. The average absolute spread between the two numbers is almost 25%.1
This can be even more pronounced in individual securities and sectors.
For example, The Wall Street Journal broke down the individual components of the earnings growth this year by company:
Ten companies are responsible for around two-thirds of the profit growth this year. Just two companies — Micron and Nvidia — make up one-third of the growth.
Yardeni Research made the point that the market doesn’t appear to trust the earnings of the semiconductors like Micron:
Profits for semiconductors are up nearly 200% while the stocks are up a little more than 40% on the year. And they are currently in a drawdown of more than 20% from the highs.
Fundamentals tell the story but you also have to take into account expectations and what’s priced into stocks. Most of the time no one knows what’s priced in.
In the long run fundamentals drive the stock market. In the short run the market and earnings are not always in lockstep with one another.
This is what makes investing int he stock market equal parts exciting and infuriating.

55,2026-10-30,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Marissa Goldstein, a 40-year-old entrepreneur from the Boston area who lives in Ho Chi Minh City. It has been edited for length and clarity.
In 2016, my husband and I flew to Vietnam with our 1-year-old twins and two bags. We told everyone we'd be back in three months.
Ten years later, we have four kids — two sets of twins — and a life in Ho Chi Minh City that I wouldn't trade. We've taken our kids to about 50 countries and spend summers with our families in Boston, then come back to the chaos of a city we love.
I grew up outside Boston. I studied communications at the University of Maryland, and a trip to Thailand during my junior year abroad was my first taste of Asia. I fell in love.
My husband and I met in high school and married after I graduated. I worked in PR and sales, then got my MBA at Babson College. Around 2015, there was an opportunity to help US companies diversify their supply chains away from China, so we started The Timroon Group, a manufacturing and sourcing company focused on Vietnam.
Have you relocated abroad — especially to Asia — for career opportunities, retirement, or lower living costs? We want to hear from you. Fill out this short form to share your story.
Being home with two babies overwhelmed me. Even though we had an incredible support system, living with my parents in a six-bedroom house in the suburbs. I quickly realized that staying home wasn't for me.
When traveling, though, I felt unstoppable. We headed to Ho Chi Minh City the day after their first birthday.
I loved it right away, especially how people treat children. We'd bring the twins to dinners and big brunches, and the staff would take them and play. I felt really safe. Within a month, we had friends from all over the world. Three months turned into 18.
When the twins were 18 months old, we took an overnight train to Sapa in northern Vietnam. Hmong women were selling their textiles, and I decided to start my own travel accessories brand, working with women artisans. On that same trip, we decided to have one more child. Soon after, we found out we were having twins again.

In January 2020, we returned to Boston, where I grew Raffi Nova, my textile accessories brand. But by 2022, my husband's business was picking up, and we had the itch to return to Vietnam.
We live about 15 minutes from the city center. There are a lot of foreigners living here, several international schools, and everything is within a 10-minute drive.
There are good doctors and hospitals, Michelin Guide-rated street food, French bakeries, and plenty of activities for the kids, from taekwondo to tennis.
The cost of living is much lower, though school and housing add up. Housing in our area ranges from about $1,500 a month for a smaller apartment to around $10,000 for a villa. We're somewhere in between, in a five-bedroom serviced apartment.
We spend about $1,000 a month on groceries, buying fruit and vegetables at the local market, and spending more on meat and imported food. A latte at a cute, bougie café is about $2, so I treat my friends to coffee without thinking twice.
We have a live-out helper who we pay about $900 a month. She cleans, preps groceries, and helps with the kids. Health insurance for our family runs under $10,000 a year.
I get serious sticker shock when I go home in the summer. I take the kids out for bagels, and it's $100 or more.
Schooling is by far our biggest cost. International school fees in the city range from about $8,000 to $30,000 per child per school year. We're at the higher end, but we strongly believe in a good education.
With fees for four kids, we probably spend about the same as we would in the US, where we wouldn't have to pay for school. But there, we wouldn't eat out or travel as much.
Both our big families live in the Boston suburbs.
There's also the weather: hot, hotter, and wet. In rainy season, it pours at school pickup every single day, even when you're sure it won't. The upside is that the kids need only one wardrobe all year.
I see Vietnam is our launchpad to the world.
Last spring, we spent a night in the jungle in Phong Nha, home to Vietnam's largest cave. We've been to Thailand about 20 times. Last February, we rented an RV and drove around New Zealand's South Island. On a trip to Sri Lanka, the kids learned to surf, and we stayed at a homestay in the mountains.
Traveling as six is hard. Most hotels don't allow four children in a room, and we can't fit in a regular car. Our best trips have never been at nice resorts. They're homestays, campsites, and RVs.
That's probably why this life suits us. I never wanted to say, ""This is the house we'll live in for the next 50 years."" Predictability scares me. I thrive in chaos, and with two sets of twins, there's always plenty of it — I wouldn't have it any other way.

56,2026-10-30,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Independent contractors offer flexibility and specialized expertise, but the savings come with trade-offs. 
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
After more than two decades of working with independent contractors, I still believe they’re one of the best ways to run a flexible small business. But I’ve also learned that they’re not nearly as cheap, simple or risk-free as many business owners assume.
There are plenty of advantages. I can hire contractors for specific projects without making a long-term employment commitment. I don’t have to provide employee benefits, paid vacation or other traditional employment perks. I can negotiate project rates, define deliverables and avoid worrying about keeping someone busy when the work dries up. Contractors generally manage their own schedules, tools and methods.
It can be a flexible and cost-effective model. But it’s not all rosy.
Here are seven things I’ve learned about working with independent contractors — and what every business owner should consider before building a business around them.
When you hire a contractor instead of an employee, you may save money on payroll taxes, benefits and other employment-related expenses. But contractors have their own business expenses, and those costs have to be covered somehow. That’s why their hourly rates are often higher than what you’d pay an employee doing comparable work.
Their rates can also change as market conditions shift, making long-term costs less predictable.
This may still be a financially sound decision for your company, but I recommend projecting the total cost of hiring a contractor versus an employee for every position you consider. Don’t assume you’re saving money just because you’re not running payroll.
Independent contractors are independent business owners, freelancers and professionals who provide services to other businesses. When you work with them, you need to understand and comply with federal, state and local tax and labor laws.
The distinction between an employee and an independent contractor generally comes down to the nature of the working relationship, including how much control the company exercises over the worker. Factors can include how the work is performed, who provides the tools and equipment, how the worker is paid and whether they operate an independent business.
I generally don’t have the same payroll tax, benefit and wage-and-hour obligations associated with employees — assuming the worker is properly classified as an independent contractor.
I have written agreements with all my contractors. These agreements document the scope of work, payment terms, deliverables and expectations. But a contract alone doesn’t determine someone’s legal classification. Government agencies look at the actual working relationship, not just what the agreement says.
Tax reporting requirements also apply. Depending on the circumstances, you may need to issue Form 1099-NEC to report payments to contractors.
Run these relationships by your accountant and attorney to make sure you’re compliant. Misclassifying an employee as an independent contractor can result in penalties, back taxes and liability for unpaid benefits and wages.
The rules governing independent contractors aren’t necessarily permanent. Federal and state governments have different approaches to worker classification, and regulations can change with new administrations, court decisions and legislative priorities.
Some regulatory changes make it harder for businesses to classify workers as independent contractors, while others give employers more flexibility. These debates often center on worker protections, benefits and the degree of independence a worker actually has.
The takeaway for business owners is simple: Don’t assume today’s rules will remain unchanged. Pay attention to developments in the jurisdictions where you operate, and periodically review your contractor relationships with your legal and accounting advisers.
Because independent contractors are independent, they’re free to follow the money wherever it takes them. They can take on other clients, accept new projects or move on when a better opportunity comes along.
Many of my contractors serve multiple clients, which I actually encourage. It reinforces the independent nature of the relationship and allows them to maintain their own businesses.
But contractors come and go. Some are more entrepreneurial, restless or focused on short-term opportunities than others. That’s why I’ve built relationships with multiple contractors, including some who compete with one another.
This gives me options when someone becomes unavailable, misses a deadline or doesn’t deliver. It also means I’m not dependent on one person to keep an important part of my business running.
Just remember: Finding good people isn’t easy. Building a reliable network of contractors takes time, and you need to invest in those relationships before you need them.
I’ve also learned that hiring an independent contractor who presents themselves as an expert doesn’t guarantee that they actually have the experience or skills you need.
Anyone can hang out a shingle and call themselves an expert. I’ve worked with so-called professionals who were hired to perform specific jobs but misrepresented their experience and capabilities.
When you hire an employee, there’s often a formal vetting process. You review resumes, conduct interviews, check references and involve other people in the decision.
Hiring a capable contractor requires the same level of due diligence. Ask for work samples, speak with previous clients, check references and clearly define expectations before signing an agreement.
Don’t assume someone knows what they’re doing just because they have a website, a business name or an impressive title. You still need to do your homework.
There’s a difference between the relationship a manager has with an employee and the relationship with an outside contractor. Employees generally work within your organization, while contractors maintain their own businesses and client relationships.
My customers understand this, but sometimes I have to explain that the people I’m sending to their projects aren’t my employees. That can raise an eyebrow.
There’s a perception that employees are more dedicated, accountable and easier to manage than outside contractors. Customers may reasonably believe I have less influence over a contractor servicing them than someone on my payroll. They’re not necessarily wrong.
You can’t hide the relationship, and you shouldn’t try to. Customers aren’t necessarily bothered by contractors. They’re bothered by uncertainty over who’s accountable when something goes wrong.
You need to be prepared to explain how you manage quality, communicate expectations and remain responsible for the work being delivered. The customer is hiring your business, and you need to stand behind the results.
Using too many independent contractors can also affect internal relationships and company culture.
Employees may resent the flexibility contractors enjoy or question why certain responsibilities are outsourced instead of handled internally. They may also wonder whether their own contributions are valued if the company regularly turns to outside professionals.
Contractors can become part of a team, but they aren’t employees. How you manage those relationships matters. There are also practical considerations involving confidential information, company data and access to internal systems. Make sure contractors have appropriate agreements and access controls in place to protect your business.
Hiring a contractor for a short-term project is one thing. Relying on contractors for essential, ongoing responsibilities is another. Think carefully about how those decisions affect morale, accountability and the culture you’re trying to build.
I don’t want to discourage business owners from using independent contractors. After more than two decades, I still rely on them, which should tell you something.
But it’s important to understand the trade-offs. Contractors can offer specialized expertise, flexibility and defined deliverables without the long-term commitment of traditional employment. They can also introduce uncertainty around availability, costs, compliance and continuity.
My advice is to use contractors when you need specialized skills, project-based support and flexibility. Hire employees when you need consistency, direct oversight, institutional knowledge and long-term commitment. The right mix can help you build a more flexible and efficient business. Just don’t assume that outsourcing the work means outsourcing the responsibility.



After more than two decades of working with independent contractors, I still believe they’re one of the best ways to run a flexible small business. But I’ve also learned that they’re not nearly as cheap, simple or risk-free as many business owners assume.
There are plenty of advantages. I can hire contractors for specific projects without making a long-term employment commitment. I don’t have to provide employee benefits, paid vacation or other traditional employment perks. I can negotiate project rates, define deliverables and avoid worrying about keeping someone busy when the work dries up. Contractors generally manage their own schedules, tools and methods.
It can be a flexible and cost-effective model. But it’s not all rosy.

57,2026-10-30,"If you're a tinkerer and have never gotten into modifying retro hardware, you should give it a try. Plenty of units are selling for very cheap because nobody has a use for them, and they can be the perfect shell for your next project.
If you need inspiration, someone scored a vintage rotary phone at a market and turned it into an AI-powered assistant.
 Over on Hackster.io, Ramazan Eren Arslan managed to get their hands on a Deutsche Bundespost FeTAp 791, a German rotary phone. The cool thing about these projects is that the phone itself is pretty cheap; Arslan says he got his at a flea market, but notes you can probably score one even cheaper if you look hard enough online.
Arslan pried the phone open and stuck a Raspberry Pi 3 Model B inside, along with a USB sound card. When you pick up the receiver, you can dial 1 for German, 2 for English, or 3 for Turkish. Whichever number you pick, you'll be directed to Google Gemini 3.8 Live, which will answer your burning questions. It's sort of like those advice hotlines from back in the day, but without the really high calling rates.
Arslan says that this project should work on other rotary phones, so if you have one sitting around (or you've spotted one for cheap in a nearby second-hand store), head over to the Vintage Phone AI Assistant Hackster page and give it a try.
If you want a retro way to talk to ChatGPT, this project harnesses the power of Pi to turn a rotary phone into a ChatGPT hotline

On Security Bite, it’s one of my favorite pastimes to explain why camera covers are virtually useless today. I’ve been called out plenty of times for it. So when Apple patches a bug that lets a malicious app get access to the camera without permission, I find myself in a rather precarious situation.
This week, Apple released iOS 27.2 to the public, and with it, over 160 different vulnerability patches. One of them fixes a bug that could let a malicious app access an iPhone’s or Mac’s camera without the user’s permission. Rest assured, this is not a hardware issue. Apple’s post-2008 change that put the camera module and the green LED indicator on the same circuit is not at fault here.

The bug, tracked as CVE-2026-86990 and credited to researcher Mustafa Calap, lives in CoreMedia, the framework apps use for audio and video. Apple’s notes only say “an access issue was addressed with improved access restrictions.”
That means the permission prompt, the one that asks whether an app can use your camera, could effectively be skipped entirely. On the Mac, that prompt comes from a system called TCC (Transparency, Consent, and Control), the gatekeeper that’s supposed to keep apps away from your camera until you say yes.
So, am I ordering a camera cover now? No. The green light would still come on (for iPhones that’s the green dot in the status bar rather than a physical LED), because the bug went after the permission, not the indicator. The problem is that the indicator only helps if someone’s there to see it. If you happened to be away from your desk, or your iPhone was face down on the table, there’d be nothing standing between that app and a live feed of the surrounding area.
It’s the exact scenario where tools like Patrick Wardle’s OverSight can come in handy. It’s free, open source application, that alerts you every time the camera or mic turns on and which app triggered it. I use it to log activity on all my Macs. Plastic over the camera can’t do that, huh?
There’s no indication from Apple that this one was exploited in the wild, which is good news here. Still, it’s important to update to iOS 27.2 and macOS Golden Gate 27.2, or iOS 26.7.2 and macOS Tahoe 26.7.2 if you’re holding off on the new OS. Older iPhones like the XS and XR get it in iOS 18.7.11.
And if you’ve never looked, now’s a good time to open Settings > Privacy & Security > Camera on iPhone (System Settings > Privacy & Security > Camera on Mac) and see which apps you’ve actually given access to. You might be surprised to find some that no longer use or don’t recognize.
Security Bite is 9to5Mac’s weekly deep dive into the world of Apple security. Each week, Arin Waichulis unpacks new threats, privacy concerns, vulnerabilities, and more, shaping an ecosystem of over 2 billion devices. Every other week on the Security Bite Podcast, he sits down with experts in the field to break down the most pressing topics.

Updated on:  October 30, 2026 / 5:15 PM EDT
            / CBS News
          
The Nasdaq Composite Index tumbled more than 700 points on Friday, a rocky close to October trading amid growing investor concerns about the health of the critical technology sector.
The rout followed a series of earnings reports from major tech companies that fell short of Wall Street's expectations. Intel's stock tumbled nearly 29% on the day, the chipmaker's biggest single-day drop since 1974. Shares of Google's parent, Alphabet, also continued to struggle following a 12% slide on Thursday that erased roughly $500 billion in market value. 
Shares of Nvidia, the maker of advanced chips used to power AI models and a bellwether for the sector, fell 4.2%.
""This has been one of the worst tech earnings seasons in memory, with underwhelming results from a slew of very prominent companies,"" market analyst Adam Crisafulli, head of Vital Knowledge, said in a research note. 
""The slump in tech stocks (and the attendant deterioration in tech sentiment) isn't simply a financial phenomenon and could undermine industry fundamentals by making it more difficult for companies to raise incremental debt and equity,"" he added in a separate report.
The negative sentiment around tech spread to other major stock indices on Friday. The S&P 500 sank 140 points, or 1.8%, while the blue-chip Dow Jones Industrial Average lost 572 points, or 1.1%.
The weaker-than-expected tech earnings reports are heightening concerns that massive investments in AI will not generate the payoff Wall Street expects. Investors are also worried about the debt companies are taking on to finance data center capital expenditures and whether that debt is sustainable.
""Mega-cap stocks have faced increased selling pressure over the past two weeks as elevated AI-related spending and rising financing costs raise concerns over the path to profitability,"" Adam Turnquist, chief cross-asset strategist for brokerage firm LPL Financial, said in an email. ""Recent earnings reports have added to these concerns, revealing slowing growth in select hyperscalers' core businesses.""
Another factor weighing on tech stocks is investor expectations that the Federal Reserve will have to keep raising interest rates to curb economic growth and subdue inflation, which remains elevated. 
The central bank left its benchmark rate unchanged this week, but many economists think it will hike rates as early as December and into 2027. That would make it more expensive for companies to borrow money to build the data centers required to power AI services, cutting into corporate profits. 
Investors have been banking on the explosive growth of AI, which has propelled the stock market to record highs, to continue to dominate. However, that sentiment is now under threat. In a report earlier this week, Ulrike Hoffmann-Burchardi, CIO Americas and global head of equities for UBS' Chief Investment Office, advised clients to ""diversify beyond AI.""
""While we maintain a constructive outlook on the structural growth of the AI theme, high expectations and potentially wide differences in company outcomes underscore the risks of single-stock concentration,"" she said. ""We think investors should ensure their equity portfolios are broadly diversified.""
Still, U.S. stocks remain only 3.4% below their all-time high, boosting the value of workers' 401(k) accounts.
Cryptocurrency prices also slumped after hackers believed to be linked to North Korea stole more than $6 billion from Binance, the world's biggest crypto exchange. The price of bitcoin fell toward $77,000, from more than $86,000 earlier this month.

61,2026-10-30,"Bradford Cox’s return to the stage was already cause for celebration. When Atlas Sound, his long-dormant solo project, was added to the bill of Philadelphia’s Making Time ∞ festival last month, the Deerhunter frontman hadn’t performed live since 2019, save an impromptu noise set at an Athens, GA vintage market in 2023. What few could have predicted was that Cox would debut a whole album’s worth of all-new material. His eight-song set, which I was blessed to experience firsthand, was sparsely arranged—the accompaniment amounted to a set of brass bells, a bass guitar, and Cox’s trusty SP-404—yet transfixing all the same.
Now, you can hear one of those songs in its full-fledged studio incarnation. It’s called “Octoberland” and it also arrives with a haunting music video, directed by Cox himself. Check it out below.
The name Atlas Sound originates from a cassette karaoke machine that a middle-school-age Cox used to create his earliest recordings. Between 2008 and 2011, he put out three albums under the moniker—Let the Blind Lead Those Who Can See But Cannot Feel, Logos, and Parallax—plus the demo collection Bedroom Databank, Vols. 1-4. These records deviated from Deerhunter’s shoegaze and krautrock leanings, placing a greater emphasis on sampling and wall-of-sound pop.
For anyone who missed Atlas Sound at Making Time, fret not: Cox has three more festival dates planned in 2027: Big Ears in Knoxville, Kilby Block Party in Salt Lake City, and Primavera Sound in Barcelona.

63,2026-10-30,"Inflation is weighing on shoppers, but the economy continues to grow.
The federal government has released most of the major economic data left to be unveiled ahead of the Nov. 3 midterm elections, meaning voters already have all of the official economic numbers before they head to the polls.
But other indicators show that the economy ranks as a top concern among Americans, who are growing ever more frustrated with their increasing inability to make ends meet.
The University of Michigan's monthly survey, for example, the latest of which was released last week, shows consumer sentiment currently stands at the second-lowest level ever recorded in the survey's 74-year history.
Additionally, half or more Americans described every household purchase category measured as “unaffordable” in the latest ABC News/Washington Post/Ipsos poll. A 71% majority of respondents now call gasoline unaffordable, up from 28% who said the same in February, before the war in Iran drove up fuel costs.
The average price of a gallon of regular unleaded gas stood at $4.22 a gallon Thursday, $1.19 more than this time a year ago, according to AAA, while a gallon of diesel averages $6.33 a gallon, a $2.65 per gallon increase compared to a year ago.
Still, employers are adding jobs, though hiring has slowed and the economy is continuing to grow.
Speaking at a rally in Corinth, Miss., on Monday, President Donald Trump said the U.S. economy was experiencing ""unbelievable growth, the biggest growth in the history of our country.""
Trump acknowledged the rise in oil prices, but he said the war with Iran had been worth it to prevent Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.
""I don't want to belittle it because it's a big thing but I thought the price of oil was going to go much, much higher and I was fully prepared to do it,"" Trump said.
Here's what to know about the U.S. economy as the midterm election nears.
The Iran war sparked a rise of inflation, squeezing household budgets and leaving shoppers glum.
Prices rose at an annual rate of 3.7% in September, the most recent month on record, federal government data showed. Inflation runs more than a percentage point higher than the Federal Reserve's target rate of 2%.
The pace of price increases is outpacing the average rise in wages, sapping spending power from workers' take-home pay. Wages began to trail inflation in April, exacerbating the budget crunch.
Price hikes have taken a toll on the outlook among consumers. Consumer sentiment stands near the lowest level ever recorded in the 74-year history of the University of Michigan's monthly survey. Shoppers expect inflation to rise even more over the next year, the survey found.
The economy has continued to grow at a robust pace, shrugging off disruption from the Iran war and expanding at an annualized rate of 3.4% over the three months ending in September, according to a government report released on Thursday.
That growth amounted to an acceleration from 2.2% and 2.5% annualized GDP growth in the previous two quarters, respectively, U.S. Commerce Department data showed.
Economic growth so far this year stands roughly in line with the expansion recorded in 2025.
A surge in spending on artificial intelligence has helped boost the U.S. economy in recent quarters. The AI sector contributed an average of 0.56 percentage points GDP growth in each of the first two quarters, accounting for more than 20% of economic growth, according to a study published last month by J.P. Morgan Asset Management.
Consumer spending, which accounts for about two-thirds of economic activity, has also proven resilient, surging 0.9% in August from July, marking the fastest monthly rise since March, federal government data showed.
The labor market is growing at a fairly robust clip, though performance from one month to the next has fluctuated dramatically.
The U.S. added an average of about 68,000 jobs per month over the first nine months of this year, according to an analysis by the Atlanta Federal Reserve. That pace marks an uptick from an average of 10,000 jobs added each month in 2025.
Job gains have proven unstable, however. Employers added only 29,000 jobs in September, the most recent month on record, which marked a sharp slowdown from 133,000 jobs added in August, U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data showed. That figure in August, meanwhile, demonstrated a major upswing from 10,000 jobs lost in July.
Even so, the yearly unemployment rate has remained consistently low since 2017, holding at 4.4% or lower with the exception of pandemic-era performance in 2020 and 2021, according to the BLS.

64,2026-10-30,"


          Architecture reporter
        
Apple has revealed its latest MacBook Pro model, which features the technology brand’s first laptop touchscreen and what it claims is “the world’s best laptop display”.
Sixteen years after Steve Jobs described touchscreen laptops as “ergonomically terrible”, Apple has ventured into the market with its new MacBook Pro, which is available from 6 November at prices ranging from £2,899 to £14,549.
As well as being touchscreen, the laptop features a PROLED XDR display, which Apple said is “the world’s best laptop display”, M5 Pro and M5 Max chips, 128 gigabytes of memory, 16 terabytes of storage, up to 24 hours of battery life and a thinner, lighter design compared to previous models.
It comes in 14- and 16-inch models, weighing three and four pounds respectively. The laptop was designed to be both portable and a powerful tool for professional creatives on the go, whether they’re working on 3D models or playing musical instruments onscreen.
“The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display is a monumental step forward for the Mac,” said Apple chief hardware officer Johny Srouji. 
“PROLED XDR is the world’s best laptop display,” he continued. “And with the magic of Touch coming to MacBook Pro, combined with the game-changing performance of M5 Pro and M5 Max, we’ve created a whole new way for pros to interact with their content, taking their workflows to a completely new level.” 
“The new MacBook Pro is the result of the deep integration between Apple silicon, hardware, and software, and there’s simply nothing else like it.”
Aiming to provide an intuitive experience, the Touch technology allows users to scroll through a webpage by swiping, zoom in on a photo by pinching, drag and drop slides in presentations and double-tap a window to fill the screen.
The touch sensor is built into the display panel rather than layered over it, to give the feel of touching the displayed content itself.
To support the Touch feature, Apple redesigned the laptop hinge for better stability and created real-time video stabilisation to smooth the camera feed when using the touchscreen on a video call.
Apple said that the macOS operating system automatically translates touches into clicks, meaning multiple existing apps will work with Touch right away. These include Adobe Lightroom, Blender, Maxon Cinema 4D, Maxon ZBrush, Notion, Canva, Zoom, Microsoft Word, Excel and PowerPoint.
“Graphic designers can now use the entire display as their canvas, no longer constrained by the area of the trackpad when drawing,” said Apple. 
“Musicians can play software instruments right onscreen, and app developers can test how Touch performs in their iOS and iPadOS apps directly on the MacBook Pro display.”
Apple said the new PROLED XDR display offers “true-to-life colour and unparalleled brightness and contrast”, made possible by combining OLED technology with algorithms that control light across the display.

“The PROLED XDR display offers tandem OLED technology unlike any other, with deep innovations in its layers that keep brightness and grayscale uniform across the entire display, while minimising internal reflections common in traditional OLED designs,” said Apple.
“It’s all backed by advanced algorithms in the display engine that precisely control each pixel in real time, plus a meticulous manufacturing process and precise calibration of every panel for consistency from display to display.”

            Read:
          
The MacBook Pro also has a six-speaker sound system, designed for deeper bass and improved sound clarity compared to previous models, and the Dynamic Island search and notification feature currently used in iPhones.
Other recent releases from Apple include its first folding smartphone, the HomeView dashboard for home controls and the budget-friendly MacBook Neo.
The images are courtesy of Apple.

Tech stocks plunged Friday, erasing more than $1 trillion in value. The Nasdaq 100 index fell 3.4% in afternoon trading, its worst one-day drop since mid-June.
The sell-off pushed the Nasdaq 100 close to what is known as “correction territory,” which is a decline of 10% or more from an index’s last record high.
The broader S&P 500 also sharply dropped 1.8%. The S&P’s tech sector was by far the worst performing of the day, tumbling by more than 4.2%.
As trillion-dollar tech companies spend hundreds of billions of dollars to build out artificial intelligence data centers, investors are looking for clearer signs that all that spending is actually delivering returns.
These questions underpinned one of the day’s worst-performing stocks, Intel, which plunged 29% in its worst-ever single-day drop, according to data dating back to the 1980s.
Several of Intel’s large peers followed it lower. SanDisk plunged 13%; chipmakers AMD, ARM Holdings, Qualcomm and Marvell all fell around 10%.
The seeds of Friday’s massive losses were sown Thursday, however, when Amazon and Intel reported quarterly earnings that failed to reassure investors and analysts.
Shares of Amazon declined by as much as 10% in post-market trading Thursday, before recovering throughout the day Friday. The company’s shares ended Friday down just 1.4%.
The Philadelphia semiconductor index, of which Intel is a member, sank 7.2% on Friday. The “DRAM” ETF, which tracks companies linked to computer memory and storage, tumbled 7%. 
So-called “neocloud” stocks also sold off. These upstart firms aim to build data centers for software firms to utilize. Nebius and CoreWeave dropped more than 10%.
The broader Nasdaq Composite, which tracks the more than 3,000 companies that trade on the exchange, fell 2.8%.
On Thursday, Amazon reported that its spending to build out AI had continued at a faster clip than expected last quarter, while growth in its core online shopping business had slowed. 
The last time Amazon told investors it was upping its AI spending budget, the market rewarded the decision by sending shares up more than 15%.
But a lot has changed in the past three months. Some of the big companies that pay to use AI models have tightened their token budgets. Others have raised questions about exactly how much benefit they’re getting from all the time and resources they’re putting into AI adoption.
In response, some investors are now looking at Big Tech’s massive AI spending through a more skeptical lens.
Nonetheless, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy told shareholders Thursday to brace for even more spending. 
“We still expect 2026 cash [capital expenditures] of approximately $220 billion, the majority for AWS, and expect to spend meaningfully more in 2027,” he said on the company’s earnings call. Amazon’s spending had already been set to double from 2025.
Friday’s losses follow significant selling in shares of Alphabet on Thursday.
The Google parent company shed about $500 billion in market value — its worst day ever and the second-worst wipeout of value in market history. On Friday, the stock dropped another 1.4%.
Intel, meanwhile, told investors that its profits and sales in the fourth quarter would be lower than expected, which sent the stock tanking.
Intel’s stock has surged in recent months as its management implements a sweeping overhaul of the long-ailing chip firm. 
In the midst of that turnaround, the U.S. government took a stake in the company, making the federal government the company’s largest shareholder. 
Even accounting for Friday’s expected drop, the value of the government’s stake in Intel has grown by 280%.
Analysts at Citigroup described the report as “two steps forward, one step back.” 
A major issue highlighted by Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan that contributed to the company’s lackluster performance in the quarter was supply chain issues. 
“I’m disappointed that we were not able to meet strong customer demand in this quarter,” Tan told investors on the company’s earnings call. “We face near-term challenges, including manufacturing, supply shortage, the unprecedented memory crunch and the resulting softness in PC demand.”
Meanwhile, the sell-off in tech shares appeared to increase demand for U.S. Treasuries, which briefly sent yields on government bonds lower.
The big AI companies that are borrowing hundreds of billions for data center build-outs have tapped the debt markets for funds, which has in turn created more competition for capital. 
As those companies increasingly offer better rates of return for lenders than the U.S. government does on bonds, Treasury yields have risen in recent months — partly to keep up with the private market.
Steve Kopack is a senior reporter at NBC News covering business and the economy.

66,2026-10-30,"Stock apps show what their designers decided matters. Build your own and you choose what to watch and when to be told. This guide covers the services people use for each part of the job.
A stock monitoring platform can be a weekend Python script or a polished dashboard. Either way it does five jobs, and every financial API you’ll come across fits one of them.
Data is the hard part. You can write your own alert logic and charts, but you can’t write market data, and that’s where cost and licensing get decided. So most of this guide is about getting prices in. The sections on storage, alerts and charts come later and are shorter.
Before you choose: prices and free-tier limits in this market change often, and providers get bought or renamed. The specific limits here were checked in October 2026. Confirm the rest on each provider’s own pricing page, and read the redistribution terms if anyone besides you will see the data.
Start with how fresh the data has to be. Exchanges charge for real-time prices and every reseller passes that fee on, which is why nearly all free tiers give you delayed or end-of-day data. For a daily summary, that’s fine. If you want an alert the moment a stock crosses a level, you’ll pay for real-time data or get it through a broker.
Most people’s first stock-data script calls Alpha Vantage, one of the oldest free stock APIs. Its useful trick is precomputed technical indicators, so you can ask for a moving average and get one back. The free allowance is 25 requests a day, though. That covers a tutorial but not a watchlist polled through a trading session.
After that, the usual move is to Twelve Data. Its free tier gives you delayed stock data with a bigger daily allowance, enough to refresh a watchlist a few times an hour, and indicators are built in there as well. Streaming is paid.
For a live dashboard on no budget, look at Finnhub. It streams prices over WebSockets on its free tier, which very few providers do, and it bundles news and fundamentals with the quotes. International coverage needs a premium plan.
Two providers matter here. The first used to be Polygon.io and is now called Massive. Plenty of tutorials still use the old name, and the old api.polygon.io host still works. Massive is mostly about U.S. markets. Its free Stocks Basic plan allows five calls a minute and two years of history, which suits learning and end-of-day screens, while real-time trades and quotes start on the paid tiers.
The second comes from a broker. Alongside its commission-free trading API, Alpaca offers a free data plan with real-time U.S. stock prices from a single venue, streamed over WebSockets. One venue means you see that venue’s trades only, so prices can drift a little from the consolidated quote. For a personal dashboard that rarely matters. If your platform might place trades later, starting here also saves you a migration.
Price tells you what happened. For why, you need fundamentals, and Financial Modeling Prep is where a lot of developers went after IEX Cloud shut down in 2024. It pairs price history with financial statements and insider activity. The free plan was listed at 250 calls a day as of mid-2026.
Backtesters tend to like Tiingo, which is known for reliable end-of-day history going back decades at a low price. It has intraday IEX data and news too.
If your interest is research more than live quotes, try Nasdaq Data Link, which used to be Quandl. Its catalog mixes free and paid datasets, and next to the equity data and fundamentals sits a long list of economic series.
U.S. data is everywhere. Outside the United States, coverage is what separates providers. For a portfolio spread over several countries, EODHD has end-of-day prices and fundamentals for many international markets, along with the exchange calendars you’ll need anyway. A simpler option is Marketstack from APILayer, a plain REST API covering more than 70 exchanges. Its free plan is tiny at 100 requests a month, but the paid tiers are cheap.
Asian markets are harder to cover. Based in Hong Kong, iTick covers stocks in several Asian markets, including Hong Kong and mainland China, as well as the U.S., and it offers FIX connections next to REST and WebSocket. It’s newer than most services here. We’d test it properly before relying on it.
Options need their own data. If that’s your interest, Market Data App is built around U.S. options chains with simple REST endpoints. For historical options and volatility research, you can buy straight from the exchange operator behind the VIX through Cboe DataShop. Futures and commodities are covered by Barchart OnDemand, a business-grade set of APIs.
At the serious end, Databento sells exchange-grade tick data and full order books, priced by usage. There’s no permanent free tier. New accounts get a one-time credit to evaluate it, and most of its users are quantitative developers. Few hobbyists will buy from Intrinio, which sells its marketplace of datasets mainly to businesses, but its terms are a useful look at what commercial licensing involves.
Two newer names round things out. In 2024, Finage pitched itself as an IEX Cloud replacement. There’s also Eulerpool, with a free plan for quotes and fundamentals. Its marketing leans on comparison articles in which it ranks itself, so trust your own testing over those rankings.
Almost every Python stock tutorial uses yfinance. It’s free and easy. It also isn’t an official API: the library relies on Yahoo Finance’s internal endpoints, so it breaks without warning when Yahoo changes them, and using it may conflict with Yahoo’s terms. For learning, that’s an acceptable risk. On anything you depend on, a broken endpoint means your alerts stop until someone ships a fix.
If you already have a brokerage account, check its API before paying anyone else. Broker data is often the cheapest real-time feed you can get, and it can see your actual positions. Licensing is the limit. The data is usually for your personal use, so it can’t power a dashboard other people look at.
The most capable option is the Interactive Brokers API, with real-time market data subscriptions and order routing to markets worldwide. It also takes the longest to learn. Schwab customers get the Charles Schwab Developer Portal, which replaced the old TD Ameritrade API, though you’ll need to register your app and wait for approval.
Then there’s Tradier, a brokerage built around its API, which a number of independent trading apps run on. And if you want to test the whole path from alert to order without real money, Alpaca’s trading API gives you a free paper-trading account.
When a stock moves sharply on no obvious news, the explanation is often in a filing. Add filings data and your platform can flag a new quarterly report or an insider trade once it’s filed.
The primary source is free. The SEC’s EDGAR APIs at data.sec.gov return every public company’s filing history as JSON with no key, along with financial-statement data pulled from XBRL. Two things trip people up. The endpoints don’t allow CORS, so call them from a server. The SEC also wants your app named in the User-Agent header, with requests kept under its published rate limit.
Raw XBRL is awkward to work with, and that’s most of the reason people pay for fundamentals instead. Financial Modeling Prep and Finnhub, among others above, do the parsing for you.
Single stocks rarely move alone. A surprise inflation reading can move the whole market in minutes, so a small macro panel next to the watchlist earns its space. The best sources here are free and come from government.
Start with the FRED API from the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. One free key covers hundreds of thousands of series, Treasury yields and CPI among them. If you’d rather go to the agency that produces the numbers, the Bureau of Labor Statistics API has the jobs report and CPI series. For federal debt and Treasury rates, the U.S. Treasury Fiscal Data API needs no key at all.
An alert tells you something happened. A headline next to it tells you why, and plenty of commercial apps still don’t do this well.
For finance news tagged by ticker, Benzinga has structured news alongside analyst ratings, and it’s also available through Massive. A lighter option is Marketaux, which adds sentiment scores for each company named in an article and has a free tier.
General news takes more work. With NewsAPI you filter by company name yourself, and its free plan isn’t allowed in production. The GDELT Project is a huge free dataset of world news coded for tone. It’s more useful for macro and geopolitical signals than for one company’s headlines.
Social data is noisier. The Stocktwits API gives you message streams and the bullish or bearish tags users put on their posts. It needs OAuth and the rate limits are tight, so confirm developer access is still open before you build on it. Reddit mentions are another signal, and the Reddit Data API can count tickers in r/stocks or r/wallstreetbets. Reddit changed its API pricing and terms in 2023, so read the current rules first, especially for commercial use.
Alternative data is anything outside prices and fundamentals. It’s where a home-built platform can show you something the big apps don’t, and also where quality varies most.
Through Quiver Quantitative you can pull congressional stock trades and corporate lobbying records over an API. Options traders tend to look at the paid Unusual Whales API, which tracks U.S. options flow and dark pool prints.
Some of the best alternative data costs nothing. Form 4 filings show insiders buying and selling, and 13F filings show what large institutions held each quarter. Both come through EDGAR. The paid providers sell them already cleaned up.
Use two data providers and small mismatches start causing bugs. One writes Berkshire Hathaway’s class B shares as BRK.B, the other as BRK-B. A scheduler that doesn’t know about market holidays will burn quota polling a closed exchange.
For identity, Bloomberg runs OpenFIGI, a free API that maps tickers and other identifiers to one open standard, so each security keeps the same ID across your sources. For timing, the Python library exchange_calendars knows the holidays and session hours of exchanges worldwide. Massive and some other data APIs also have a market-status endpoint you can check before polling.
Portfolios rarely stay all stocks. Several providers above handle crypto, but specialists are often cheaper.
For crypto prices across exchanges, CoinGecko has a free demo tier and CoinMarketCap a free basic plan. If you want data straight from an exchange, the Binance API streams market data for free where Binance is available. U.S. developers often use the Coinbase Developer Platform instead.
Currencies matter as soon as you hold a stock listed abroad. To convert a mixed portfolio into one currency, Open Exchange Rates covers more than 170 currencies with hourly updates on its free plan. For a daily rate at no cost, Frankfurter is an open-source API serving the European Central Bank’s reference rates.
Trading desks buy from a handful of institutional vendors on annual contracts. You won’t be one of their customers, but they set the standard everyone else is judged against.
That standard is still Bloomberg, delivered through B-PIPE and its other data products, usually alongside a Bloomberg Terminal. The former Refinitiv now sells real-time feeds and deep history as LSEG Data & Analytics.
Research teams buy estimates and ownership data from FactSet, and company financials through Capital IQ from S&P Global Market Intelligence. For fund and ETF research, Morningstar licenses its ratings and analytics to businesses. Brokerages and fintechs are the main buyers of the cloud market data APIs sold by Xignite.
Pick the channel by urgency. A broken price level deserves a push notification or a text. A daily summary can sit in your inbox.
SMS usually means Twilio, billed per message, which also handles voice and WhatsApp. Push is cheaper and just as quick. For personal use, Pushover costs little: send one HTTP request and the alert lands on your phone, with priority levels for the moves that matter. The open-source equivalent is ntfy, which you can use hosted or run yourself, and an alert is a single request to a topic URL. If you’ve built your own app, Google’s free Firebase Cloud Messaging handles push to phones and browsers.
Chat apps work well too. A bot on the free Telegram Bot API can answer commands as well as send alerts, so typing /quote AAPL on your phone gets you a price. To share signals with a small group, Discord webhooks post to a channel in one request. Teams on Slack can do the same with Slack incoming webhooks.
Email suits the slower updates. For digests and earnings reminders, Twilio-owned SendGrid handles transactional email, and Postmark has a reputation for fast delivery, which matters if an email alert is time-sensitive.
General charting libraries struggle with financial data. Candlesticks need special handling, and so do the gaps over nights and weekends when the market was shut.
Many developers use TradingView Lightweight Charts. It’s free and open source, and while it isn’t TradingView’s full charting product, it covers what most dashboards need. If you want indicators and range selectors built in, Highcharts Stock is the mature option, free for non-commercial use and licensed otherwise.
Among general-purpose libraries, Apache ECharts supports candlesticks and copes well with large datasets. Candlesticks need a plugin in Chart.js, so we’d keep it for allocation and performance charts.
Store what you fetch. It lets you backtest alert rules before trusting them, and you stop spending quota on data you already have. Market data is time-series data, and some databases are built for exactly that.
If you already use PostgreSQL, add TimescaleDB. It’s an open-source extension, so you keep SQL and gain fast time-bucketed queries. The company behind it renamed itself from Timescale to Tiger Data in June 2025, and the extension kept its name. A purpose-built alternative is InfluxDB, open source or cloud, widely used for metrics and market ticks. For very high ingestion rates, there’s QuestDB, which is open source and aimed at financial tick data.
With this many choices it’s easy to stall in research. To start, you need one data source, one database, one alert channel, and one chart library. Here are two starting points built from services in this guide.
One piece of advice holds for any build. Keep your data provider behind a single module in your code. Providers get renamed and sometimes shut down, as IEX Cloud users learned in 2024. With one module, a shutdown notice means editing one file. Without it, you’re rewriting the application.
Compiled October 2026. Inclusion isn’t an endorsement. Confirm pricing and data licensing on each provider’s site before you build on it.

67,2026-10-30,"Poundland has been a recognisable presence on high streets for more than 35 years 
""When I told people what I wanted to do, open shops on the high street selling things for one pound, they said, 'how will you find products to sell for a pound, what about inflation, it will never work.'""
That is how Steve Smith, the founder of Poundland, describes the genesis of the budget giant - which went on to employ thousands of people and see over a million customers a week - on his own website, external. 
But now the West Midlands-based retailer is teetering on the edge, after its US-based owners Gordon Brothers cut off a vital funding supply and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group.
Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators
Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse
The curious case of why Poundland is struggling during a cost-of-living crisis
Having spent time on his parents stall since the age of two Wolverhampton-born businessman Smith started out in the very same place - selling items for Â£1 from his own stall.
After a Â£50,000 loan from his parents he opened the first Poundland shop, in The Octagon Shopping Centre in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, on 13 December 1990.
That first day, with a range of 624 products, the store took more than Â£13,000 - ""and Poundland was born,"" Smith said. 
Smith and his wife, Tracy, now based in Shropshire, owned the company for 16 years.
In 2006, he decided the time had come and he sold his final shares in the business for Â£250m, but later said the sale felt it was ""as though somebody had died"".
Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith
But by 2025, the Walsall-based company was itself sold for just Â£1.
Its owner, Polish firm Pepco, confirmed it had sold the brand for a ""nominal"" sum to US investment firm Gordon Brothers.
At the time Poundland had more than 800 stores across the UK and Ireland and employed around 16,000 staff.
""I did pick up the phone when I heard it was for sale but I was a bit too late,"" Smith said after the sale. 
Now, the retailer's bosses have filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which will give the business 10 days to find a rescue deal.
A protest was held outside the Poundland HQ on Thursday, Claire Sutcliffe - who has been at the company for nearly a decade - among those demonstrating. 
Sutcliffe said she was ""very sad"" to hear the news 
Questioned about how she felt, the 49-year-old replied she was ""very sad"", adding: ""Nine years is a lot working for Poundland and they've been really good to me as a Poundland family, really supportive.""
Sutcliffe added that some had worked for the business for 20 years and ""it's just very sad for us all"".
Asked what her message was to owners Gordon Brothers, she replied: ""Please just sell.
""Save our jobs, we've all got families to feed and it's... near Christmas.""
About 25 people took part in a protest at Poundland in Walsall on Thursday
Professor Sarah Montano, a retail expert from the University of Birmingham, said: ""The real problem for Poundland is, when they first started it was really great because everything was Â£1, that was a really unique selling point for them.
""[Today] there's competitors which are offering very similar prices and for quite a lot of products they're offering them cheaper.""
She added that some competitors had ""really got their finger on the key trends"". 
In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been ""working on a formal sale process for Poundland"" and despite ""enormous efforts it has not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole"".
""The process has however attracted several established retail operators who have made formal offers, collectively these cover a significant part of the business, and we believe offer the best available prospects for shareholders, creditors and to preserve jobs,"" they said. 
""We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment.""
They also thanked ""staff, suppliers and customers for their continued support"".

                    systems
                

        CEO predicts massive datacenter CPU boom, coming soon, but production problems mean Chipzilla may not cash in
    
Intel’s share price sank by almost a quarter on Thursday after the company revealed a deep dip in sales of PC processors.
The rout began after the silicon-slinger posted Q3 results that included some nice numbers, such as a 21 percent year on year revenue jump to $16.5 billion, helped by 78 percent growth for datacenter and AI products, and gross margins of 43 percent. The revenue number was at the high end of Intel’s revenue guidance of $15.8-16.8 billion, and the gross margin beat its predictions by two points.
Outweighing those upsides was a decline in revenue for client computing and physical AI products, which saw incoming cash reach $8.1 billion but fall five percent year-over-year and nine percent decline from last quarter.
In prepared remarks [PDF] published to coincide with Intel’s earnings announcement, CEO Lip Bu Tan said “In recent weeks, our customers began signaling a PC slowdown based on memory prices and availability. Year-to-date system end-point prices have increased significantly placing additional pressure on already limited IT budgets.”
“The pace of the PC correction has accelerated over the last two months, as our OEM partners are aggressively managing inventory to match the softer sell through,” he added, before predicting that the market for PC chips will shrink by 12 percent next year and remain tough “until memory availability and prices begin to normalize.”
Which may not be for years, given Korean memory-maker SK Hynix this week predicted that memory shortages will persist until the year 2030. Intel’s biggest business is therefore in trouble.
Tan tried to give investors reasons to be cheerful, by saying Intel sees a “resurgence in demand for CPU” sparked by customers’ desire for computing systems that consume less energy.
“Increasingly, energy efficiency is becoming the most important metric for the AI infrastructure build-out,” he said.
That matters, Tan argued, because “While GPUs are great at ‘thinking tasks,’ deterministic CPUs are far more efficient at ‘finding’ tasks. While a GPU reasoning query can run as high as multiple watt-hours of energy, a single CPU-based query could cost only joules of energy.”
The CEO reasoned that lower energy consumption is important in the context of agentic AI – the idea that we will all soon run many autonomous agents to handle personal and professional scutwork.
“Applying even conservative estimates to the total number of digital agents, and agents serviced per CPU, it is easy to anticipate a massive dollar and unit TAM [total addressable market] for CPUs over the coming five years,” Tan said. “By our estimates, server CPU TAM, largely driven by agentic workloads, could increase as much as 5X from where it is today, from $40B to more than $200B over five years.”
But during its earnings call, Intel execs admitted it may struggle to cash in on that boom in the short and longer term as the company struggles to source key supplies like silicon wafers.
Asked if Intel can meet demand for server CPUs and if investors should therefore expect further growth in datacenter product sales early in Intel’s next financial year, chief financial officer Dave Zinsner declined to offer any guidance.
“There is going to be a strong driver of demand for CPUs,” he demurred. “We see that continuing all through next year. We are working on the supply side of the equation to make sure that we can meet what customers need. We will likely not fully meet what they require, but I think we are going to be in a more challenging situation in the first half of the year than we are in the second half.”
Intel is trying to shift production around its fleet of fabs so it can crank out as much silicon as possible and will even devote some PC chip production capacity to datacenter products. But overall, Chipzilla is clearly in a spot of bother.
CEO Lip Bu Tan said his plan to “rebuild” Intel will be “a multiyear journey” and insisted “We have made enormous strides in these 18 months, including a dramatic re-sizing of our workforce, eliminating middle management and bureaucracy, streamlining our executive ranks and establishing an almost entirely new leadership team.”
He pointed to strong progress on Intel’s manufacturing processes that will bring it closer to parity with TSMC, meaning its own products will become more potent while the company’s foundry business also becomes more attractive.

And he said that Intel has restored an “engineering-centric culture” that will help the company to improve.
But the CEO also admitted “I am disappointed that we were not able to meet strong customer demand in this quarter.”
“We face near term challenges, including manufacturing supply shortages, the unprecedented memory crunch, and the resulting softness in PC demand. But I remain confident in our path forward.”
Investors did not share that confidence. Intel’s shares started the day at $92.96 apiece. Immediately after the company announced its results, shares sank to $73. At the time of writing, after-hours trading has seen the price reach $70.87, a 23.7 percent slump. ®

      Business
  



      Business
  

NEW YORK, Oct 30 : US midterm elections, a heavy slate of corporate earnings and the monthly jobs report will further test stock investors next week, after a series of setbacks for the market-leading AI trade.
The latest AI turbulence stemmed from disappointing quarterly results of Alphabet and Amazon , two hyperscalers spending massively on data centers, while shares of semiconductor company Intel tumbled nearly 29 per cent on Friday following its report. 
Dragged down by tech and AI-related stocks, the S&P 500 sank 1.8 per cent on Friday while the Nasdaq Composite fell 2.9 per cent.
The quarterly results intensified concerns about financing and financial returns needed to support the AI investment theme at the heart of the US bull stock market. The questions are likely to persist, with Apple, AMD and Palantir among the major tech and AI players due to report next week. Also, the midterm elections could determine the outlook for AI regulation.

MIDTERM VOTE COULD AFFECT AI
Tuesday's elections will determine whether Democrats can wrest control of Congress away from President Donald Trump's Republican party. Democrats are widely predicted to win the House of Representatives, but the Senate is more in doubt. Republicans controlled both chambers of Congress during the first two years of the Trump administration. 
Investors are bracing for increasing pushback on data centers, whose impact on energy costs and the environment has led to intense public opposition. Despite doubts that any viable legislation will pose serious risks to AI investment, regardless of which party wins, a Democratic sweep is expected to bring more scrutiny. Investors are also watching local and state races in Texas and elsewhere on Tuesday for their implications for the data-center buildout.
""To the extent that the Democrats really do have a significant blue wave, I think that will be seen as negative for both the AI names, for tech to some extent in general, and maybe even for the market as a whole,"" said Rick Meckler, partner at Cherry Lane Investments.
Defense and healthcare are other stock market areas that could be sensitive to the election results. Any shift in Washington could affect the outlook for fiscal spending that could reverberate through asset prices. A Democratic sweep could bring more regulatory scrutiny, including for Wall Street banks and crypto firms.
ALSO ON TAP: APPLE, AMD RESULTS, JOBS DATA 
Robust earnings performance has underpinned equity gains so far in 2026. With over half of the S&P 500 having reported third-quarter results, overall earnings were on pace to rise 31.6 per cent for the period, according to LSEG IBES data on Friday.
Investors were punishing some reports. For example, Caterpillar shares sank 9 per cent on Thursday after the company stuck to its annual sales growth forecast despite strong demand for its power equipment from data centers.
""The earnings bar is very high,"" said Chris Galipeau, head market strategist at the Franklin Templeton Institute. ""And if you miss or don't jump over that bar, you're in trouble.""
Apple reports on Monday, with the iPhone maker's shares up about 27 per cent so far this year. Results are also expected from semiconductor companies AMD and Qualcomm as well as Palantir, which offers another view of the AI trade. McDonald's, Amgen and ConocoPhillips are among over 100 S&P 500 companies that are due to report next week.
Next Friday's employment report for October will give a critical read into the health of the US economy.
Data on Thursday showed the US economy in the third quarter grew at its fastest pace in a year, as consumers maintained robust spending and businesses doubled down on investment in AI. The Federal Reserve this week upgraded its assessment of the economy as it held interest rates steady.
""I think the missing piece of this strong economic growth narrative is the lack of job growth,"" said Matthew Miskin, co-chief investment strategist at Manulife John Hancock Investments.
 Other areas of the market have picked up the slack as AI and related technology stocks have struggled. For October, the energy and financial sectors posted solid monthly gains, as did defensive groups such as utilities and healthcare. The S&P 500 was last up over 10 per cent on the year. 
""The market is much more broad than people generally want to give it credit for,"" Galipeau said.

Samsung Android Tablets Rumors

Back in late August, a tipster on X claimed that a 17-inch foldable tablet is in development, with multiple versions and test devices being worked on, but didn't say which company is making it. Now, the same tipster has followed up with a short but telling addition, revealing that ""Samsung has already started.""
According to the original post, the device becomes an 8-inch tablet when folded and expands to 17 inches when unfolded. That's all we've got for now, as the tipster says they can't share any more details yet.
How exactly the 8-inch folded state would work is anyone's guess at this point. It could have a 17-inch screen that folds outwards, with only half of it active when closed, or a 17-inch inner screen paired with an 8-inch cover display, like a supersized Galaxy Z Fold. The former would leave the main screen exposed, while the latter would surely make it even more expensive.
This isn't the first time Samsung has been rumored to be working on a foldable tablet - a so-called Galaxy Z Tab was tipped back in 2023, and nothing came of it. Obviously, Samsung Display can make big foldable panels, as it's shown off plenty of prototypes at trade shows over the years, but whether Samsung's mobile division thinks there's a market for such a device is a different question entirely.
Then again, the foldable market looks a bit different now that Apple has finally joined in with the iPhone Duo, so Samsung may be keen to show off just how far it can push the form factor. There's no word on timing yet, so we wouldn't assume it's coming anytime soon.
Source
What took them so long? I've been thinking about a folding book-style tablet a little smaller than an iPad Mini when folded. It would be quite practical.
This is ridiculous. You haven't perfected any foldable but keep adding larger sizes same fragility. I don't know what Samsung is thinking
If packed with flagship specs Count me In

                    ai and ml
                

        Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring
    
Two insurers are teaming up to launch what every high-tech homeowner may soon need: household insurance for robotic mishaps.
Humanoid robots are tipped by some to be the next big technology trend, with industry figures like Nvidia's Jensen Huang predicting they'll be everywhere from factories to the home.
But as with anything AI-driven, things can go horribly wrong, especially when automatons are roaming a home carrying out tasks. They have even been labelled as potential ""botnets in physical form.""
The timing may be apt, then, for the UK's first domestic humanoid robot insurance policy. Specialist insurer Adrian Flux is offering to cover British customers as part of its home insurance range, underwritten by Texas-based Tokio Marine HCC.
The two firms say the product is meant to get ahead of an emerging market, with domestic robots expected to appear in UK homes from 2027. Demand has already appeared in the US, where robotics and AI companies have launched humanoid robots for the home, with early models costing around $20,000.
These are less like the near-human elderly care robot in the BBC comedy series Ann Droid, and more like vaguely human-shaped machines.
The policy covers damage to, and theft of, the robot itself, plus any damage it causes to the home or belongings. It also includes personal liability if the android injures someone or damages their property, and emergency recovery or safe disablement of the humanoid after a sudden or unforeseen malfunction, such as - perhaps - attempting to chop off your fingers or put your cat on the stove.
“As robots start to arrive in people's homes, we wanted to provide clarity on who is responsible if something goes wrong,""  says Gerry Bucke, general manager for the insurance biz Adrian Flux. ""Like the driverless car policy we launched in 2016, it will be updated as the technology and the risks evolve."" 
Beyond the home, investment is pouring into humanoids across a range of applications. Chinese carmaker Chery using them as showroom sales staff, while another firm has demonstrated them in automotive manufacturing.
Elon Musk famously predicted thousands of Optimus robots in Tesla factories by the end of 2025, but still believes its humanoid robot will be the company’s biggest product. ®

Daemonarchy is unaffiliated with the Linux original, built with AI help, and could complicate FreeBSD's debate over AI contributions

But learns tough lessons about AI in the process
