      Singapore
  



      Singapore
  

China's manufacturing network, technological capabilities and research and development offer Singapore firms opportunities to improve their products, lower costs and expand internationally.
China installed 354,000 industrial robots in 2025, accounting for about 60 per cent of global installations. (Image: CNA)

       This audio is generated by an AI tool.
    
SUZHOU, Jiangsu: Singapore companies looking to expand into China should focus on growth sectors such as smart technology, advanced manufacturing and the green economy, said Enterprise Singapore (EnterpriseSG). 
Mr Kow Juan Tiang, the statutory board's executive director for China, said opportunities remain abundant for firms offering innovative solutions. 
He added that China's vast market, manufacturing capabilities and supply chains could also serve as a springboard for expansion into Southeast Asia and beyond. 
“(China) is a very large market that is still showing significant growth, and that represents a lot of opportunities for Singapore companies to expand,” he said during a media trip organised by the agency.

“It’s very important for us to know our core capabilities and the value proposition that we have.” One Singapore company tapping into China's rapidly growing robotics sector is Xnergy, which develops contactless charging solutions for autonomous robots. As factories, warehouses and other industries increasingly deploy robots, keeping large fleets charged and operational has become a growing challenge. Most robots rely on contact charging, which requires physical docking and can lead to wear and tear over time. Xnergy's technology works like universal chargers, allowing robots of different brands and models to charge without needing to be physically plugged in. Robots can automatically return to a charging station when their batteries run low, with charging beginning once they are correctly positioned. Since entering China in 2021, Xnergy has witnessed what its co-founder and CEO Chin Foong Tong described as ""exponential growth"" in the robotics industry.According to the International Federation of Robotics, China installed 354,000 industrial robots in 2025, accounting for about 60 per cent of global installations. 
“It’s very important for us to know our core capabilities and the value proposition that we have.” 
One Singapore company tapping into China's rapidly growing robotics sector is Xnergy, which develops contactless charging solutions for autonomous robots. 
As factories, warehouses and other industries increasingly deploy robots, keeping large fleets charged and operational has become a growing challenge. 
Most robots rely on contact charging, which requires physical docking and can lead to wear and tear over time. 
Xnergy's technology works like universal chargers, allowing robots of different brands and models to charge without needing to be physically plugged in. 
Robots can automatically return to a charging station when their batteries run low, with charging beginning once they are correctly positioned. 
Since entering China in 2021, Xnergy has witnessed what its co-founder and CEO Chin Foong Tong described as ""exponential growth"" in the robotics industry.
According to the International Federation of Robotics, China installed 354,000 industrial robots in 2025, accounting for about 60 per cent of global installations. 
Mr Chin said developing a universal charging solution is technically challenging, given the different software, designs and docking capabilities of robots. 
But China's vast customer base and established supply chains have helped Xnergy develop and scale its technology. 
“China is one of the biggest homogeneous robotic markets in the world,” said Mr Chin, adding that the country offers both a strong supplier network and a large customer base that is open to adopting new technologies. 
The company currently manufactures several thousand chargers annually but aims to increase production by as much as 100 times in the coming years. 
To support that expansion, Xnergy is stepping up its sales and marketing efforts while seeking fresh capital. 
China will remain its manufacturing base, even as the company serves overseas markets including South Korea, Japan and the United States. 
Sustainability is another growth sector, supported by China's push towards a lower-carbon economy. 
It is among the priorities in China's 15th Five-Year Plan, which outlines the country's economic and industrial development goals for 2026 to 2030. 
China has also pledged to peak its carbon dioxide emissions before 2030 and achieve carbon neutrality before 2060. 
“That represents a commitment by the country, and that's why there's a lot of opportunities in this sector,” said EnterpriseSG’s Mr Kow. 
“Beyond renewable energy generation, you have power efficiency, carbon services, etc. The entire ecosystem represents opportunities for Singapore companies to participate in.” 
One company expanding in this space is SP Group, drawing on two decades of experience operating Singapore's Marina Bay district cooling system. 
The utilities provider is expanding its district cooling network in Guangzhou Knowledge City, including a system serving a 53-storey tower. The first phase of the project spans 80ha. The system also provides heating during colder months. 
Unlike the densely built-up Marina Bay financial district, Guangzhou Knowledge City covers a larger area that is still developing. 
Mr Michael Lim, SP Group’s deputy managing director of sustainable solutions China, said he sees growing demand for energy-efficient infrastructure as China places greater emphasis on sustainability. 
“In the past, projects in China were mainly driven by policy. Now, it's a little bit different. We're moving into more of a hybrid structure where there's a lot more emphasis on sustainable solutions, reducing carbon emissions, but of course (with) regulatory support,” he said. 
Mr Lim said the company is prioritising cost-effective, low-carbon energy solutions rather than simply pursuing projects with the largest capacity. 
SP Group plans to triple its capacity across China by 2030, targeting industrial and healthcare developments, including semiconductor suppliers supporting the artificial intelligence boom. 
China's economy was worth about US$20 trillion in 2025. At annual growth of around 5 per cent, that translates to roughly US$1 trillion in additional economic output each year. 
Still, it is a tougher market than before, with a prolonged property slump, cautious consumer spending and intensifying competition. 
But EnterpriseSG said Singapore firms can still carve out opportunities by targeting expanding sectors and leveraging their strengths. 
Mr Kow identified advanced manufacturing, biomedical sciences and sustainability as key growth areas. 
He added that Singapore is generally regarded as trustworthy, with reliable products and solutions. 
However, Mr Kow cautioned that China remains a large and complex market, urging firms to understand local regulations and take steps to mitigate risks. 
Beyond its domestic market, China's manufacturing network, supply chains and technological advances could help Singapore companies develop products and services for international markets. 
“China's manufacturing capability, supply chain and technology development are (what) Singapore companies can tap upon. They can utilise this to expand within China, to other countries in Southeast Asia, or even globally,” said Mr Kow.

73,2026-10-30,"Apple just unveiled the new OLED MacBook Pro. Although the laptop comes with the same chips as the MacBook Pro models introduced early this year, Apple is offering more storage on this new machine, which comes at a higher price. If you were to max out the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display – meaning you chose the 16-inch model with the nano-texture display, higher end M5 Max chip, 128GB of RAM, and 16TB of storage — then you'd end up paying $14,549 before taxes. If you choose not to upgrade the amount of storage and opt for the included 2TB option, you end up saving $7,000 –  almost half of the price of the maxed out MacBook Pro specification.
While this sounds like a staggering price to pay for a laptop — especially one with a processor released earlier this year — Apple is known for charging a lot more for the RAM and SSD in these devices. For example, the also recently-released Mac Studio has finally rolled out a new option with up to 512GB of RAM, which alone costs $15,500. Combined with up to 16TB of storage, which Apple charges $7,500 for, customers could pay up to $29,799 for the M5 Ultra version — or more than double the price of the new MacBook Pro. Prices could be even higher, as you probably need a few extra accessories like a display, keyboard, mouse, and so on. Still, if you're up to the task of paying that much for a Mac, you should know you'll get what's best from the company at this moment.
If you're asking this question, then it probably means this machine is not for you. Since Apple offers unified memory built in to its own processors, users can only choose the setup of their machine once. For example, if you order the laptop with 24GB of RAM, this is what you'll get for the rest of your computer's life since you can't upgrade it. Regarding storage, a cheaper solution would be getting an external SSD drive, since other companies don't charge as much for storage as Apple does. Apple also doesn't let users upgrade the amount of built-in storage on their devices either.
For someone maxing out a new MacBook Pro, they're getting one of the best machines in the market for video editing, coding, running giant local AI models, creating with 3D models, and other complex workflows. With up to 18 core CPU, featuring 12 performance cores and 6 super cores, it's been optimized for power efficient, multithreaded workloads with up to 30% faster performance than the previous chip. The LLM prompt processing is 4x faster with the M5 Max chip with up to 50% increase in graphics performance. Finally, the M5 Max can support up to 614GB/s of memory bandwidth. Another important upgrade from the M4 generation is 2x faster SSD speeds, meaning that accessing data on the computer is faster than ever.
Apple still sells its existing MacBook Pro models with the M5 Pro and M5 Max chips, if horsepower is what you're looking for. You won't get the new display, lighter chassis, or glowing Apple logo on the lid, however. You can max the older model out for as much as $10,149 but with only 8TB of storage. That works out $400 cheaper than the OLED version of the equivalently configured model, for a laptop that ""gets better"" according to BGR's review."
74,2026-10-30,"By 


      Chris Arnold

      Quil Lawrence

      Robert Benincasa
    


                Army veteran Scott Boyd stands in his home with his dog, Jessi, near Columbus, Ohio. A loan modification raised his VA home loan payment by nearly $1,200 every month. Because of his work in military intelligence, Boyd asked that NPR not include his face in any photographs.
                
                    
                    Maddie McGarvey for NPR
                    
                
hide caption

Thousands of military veterans with VA-backed mortgages have been hit with higher interest rates and monthly payments since last year, when the Trump administration shut down a low-interest program designed to help them.
Among veterans who had their loans modified after a financial hardship, more than 15,000 saw their monthly payments increase by at least $150, according to an NPR investigation that obtained previously unreported data from the Department of Veterans Affairs.
Loading...
More than 700 veterans suffered payment increases of $1,000 or more.
Lenders offer loan modifications to enable homeowners to catch up on missed payments. It involves changing the loan terms, and for the vast majority of Americans, it either lowers their payment or keeps it the same, depending on what they can afford. But that's not the case for VA loans, since the VA abruptly ended the program.
NPR found that 63% of veterans who received a loan modification were hit with a payment increase, compared with just 2% for modifications of loans guaranteed by the government-sponsored enterprises Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, which cover most of the U.S. mortgage market.
Loading...
""This is the height of insanity,"" said California Democratic Rep. Mark Takano, the ranking member of the House Veterans' Affairs Committee, after NPR shared its findings.
""These are veterans that are already financially distressed — they were already in trouble,"" he said.
Veterans like Scott Boyd are literally paying the price.
When Boyd was a teenager, his dad died of cancer. He said he saw the military as a clear path to start helping his family.
""My mom signed paperwork when I was 16,"" Boyd recalled. ""I'm like, 'I'm sending my checks home to take care of my mom.'""
He started out in the infantry and saw combat. Later, Boyd worked in Army intelligence in some dangerous places. When he got out in 2002, he had skills that set him up for a career in tech. In 2021, he bought a house near Columbus, Ohio, using a VA home loan. Like other buyers at the time, he got a nice low-interest rate. ""I felt very blessed,"" he said.
But a few years later, Boyd hit a crisis: He lost his job in a mass layoff, and he said his time in combat came back to haunt him in the form of PTSD. He checked himself into the Dayton VA hospital for treatment but fell behind on his mortgage.

                Boyd and thousands of other veterans were pushed into much-higher-cost modified home loans. Hundreds are paying at least $1,000 more a month.
                
                    
                    Maddie McGarvey for NPR
                    
                
hide caption

Most Americans can get help in a situation like this. Mortgage companies don't want to foreclose because both they and the homeowner experience losses. So they have levers they can pull to help people who've fallen behind on payments.
Boyd's lender told him that he didn't have to worry because the VA had a loan forbearance program that would allow him to pause his payments and provide him with an affordable way to catch up after.
""I later found out that wasn't true,"" he said.
It wasn't true because the Trump administration then shut down a key part of the VA's home loan program. It was called the Veterans Affairs Servicing Purchase (VASP) program. Without it, vets like Boyd were stranded with no option but a much-higher-cost modified home loan at today's higher interest rates.
In Boyd's case, he is now stuck paying $1,181 more — every month.
""This is outrageous,"" said Boyd. ""I remember just thinking to myself, 'This is just so broken.'""
Some in Congress agree.
""The VA home loan program should not be making things worse and more stressful for that veteran, a combat veteran, who's been through hell,"" said Takano, about Boyd's case in particular.

                Rep. Mark Takano, D-Calif., speaks on Capitol Hill in 2025.
                
                    
                    Rod Lamkey/AP
                    
                
hide caption

Illinois Republican Rep. Mike Bost, chairman of the House Veterans' Affairs Committee, did not respond to NPR's repeated requests for comment. NPR also reached out to Wisconsin Republican Rep. Derrick Van Orden, a committee member who led the call to wind down the VASP program. He also did not respond to requests for comment.
The VA said in a statement that it helps thousands of veterans ""retain homeownership and/or avoid foreclosure through programs like payment forbearance, loan modifications and individualized repayment plans.""
Before it was shut down, the VASP program was helping tens of thousands of veterans who had fallen behind on their home loan payments by rolling those missed payments into a new mortgage with a low 2.5% interest rate. That made the program a lifeline for vets in today's higher-interest-rate environment.
And Boyd said his mortgage company had been telling him that he too could use VASP when he was able to resume paying his mortgage.
"" I would constantly ask them, 'OK, this is getting further and further behind,' and they were like, 'You have this last lever to pull.'""
This was in the spring of 2025, just months into the second Trump administration. Key Republicans on the House Veterans' Affairs Committee vocally opposed VASP, which the Biden administration created after its own VA home loan disaster. Republicans warned that VASP was putting taxpayers on the hook for thousands of loans to vets who had trouble paying.
""If the Biden version of VASP was allowed to continue, I fear that the home loan will not exist in the future for my fellow veterans,"" said Van Orden during a House Veterans' Affairs Committee hearing on March 11, 2025.

                Rep. Derrick Van Orden, R-Wis., speaks in Eau Claire, Wis., on Oct. 20. He is currently in a tight race for reelection.
                
                    
                    Ellen Schmidt/AP
                    
                
hide caption

But at the same hearing, as well as in letters to Congress and press releases, the mortgage industry and housing groups explicitly warned the Trump administration not to kill the program without first creating another viable option for vets. They said if the VA shut down the program before standing up a replacement, vets would be left with far worse options than other American homeowners. With interest rates up around 7%, they warned that the only option left would be a modified loan at the current higher rates, which would result in foreclosures for those who couldn't afford the higher payments. And that's exactly what has been happening.
"" I was doing the math in my head as they were telling me, you know?"" Boyd said about the loan modification he was presented with. ""If I'm going from 2.75 to 6.75 … it seemed ridiculous,"" he said. Not only would that jump in his interest rate boost his payment by nearly $1,200 every month, but his loan term was extended to 40 years. So he went from about half of his monthly payment going toward principal to now more than 90% of his payment going to cover just the interest.
""This was a foreseeable outcome of abruptly ending a program without a replacement in place,"" said Matthew Douglas, of the Housing Policy Council. He represents the largest mortgage companies in the U.S. and was among those warning the VA not to kill VASP. Asked about NPR's findings, he said, ""It's a mathematical certainty that borrowers are going to have those kind of shocking payment increases."" 
Despite the warnings, the Trump administration ended the VASP program on May 1, 2025 — apparently in coordination with House Republicans, who announced the move before the Department of Veterans Affairs did, even though VASP was ended administratively, with no vote from Congress.
""We — along with many of our colleagues — had serious concerns about the impact VASP would have on not only the future of VA's home loan program, but the mortgage lending business as a whole. Today, the Trump administration rightfully put an end to VA's VASP program,"" Bost and Van Orden said in a joint press release at the time.
In his statement to NPR, VA press secretary Quinn Slaven wrote: ""many leading voices in Congress supported the termination of VASP, which put the entire VA home loan program at risk.""

                Boyd lives with his partner and her four children, whom they are raising together.
                
                    
                    Maddie McGarvey for NPR
                    
                
hide caption

At the same time Van Orden was calling for VASP to be scuttled, he had written a bill to replace it with a new VA Partial Claim Program (PCP). Congress quickly passed that bill with broad bipartisan support in the wake of the VA scuttling VASP. It directed the VA to create an option for vets behind on payments, enabling them to move their missed payments to the back of their loan term. That way, vets could keep their original mortgage and not be forced into a higher-cost loan.
But after ending VASP, it took the VA more than 13 months to design and announce the details of the new program, which it did in June of this year. And the industry was then given another 180 days to implement it. Most mortgage companies still do not offer it.
In that gap, thousands of veterans got modified loans with higher interest rates and payments. The VA's new PCP program won't do anything to help those vets — they'll remain stuck in those modified loans.

                Boyd holds a challenge coin from the Department of Defense that was given to him during his military service.
                
                    
                    Maddie McGarvey for NPR
                    
                
hide caption

NPR asked the VA about the vets who fell into the gap between the two programs. Slaven, the VA spokesman, said in his statement that the VA home loan program ""is based on the premise that while Veterans may need some assistance, they must generally be able to make their mortgage payments.""
Industry groups say raising a struggling homeowner's payment makes it more likely for them to redefault and face foreclosure. Foreclosures on VA loans rose to a 10-year high after VASP was killed, according to industry data. Experts warned in a congressional roundtable in September that time is running out for the government to help these vets or a wave of foreclosures is still to come.
Rep. Takano told NPR that the VA, if it chose to, could put these vets back into lower-cost mortgages or that Congress could pass a law to do it.
""We could do something for those veterans who saw a jump in their interest rates,"" Takano said.

                Boyd says he feels defeated after receiving worse loan options from the VA.
                
                    
                    Maddie McGarvey for NPR
                    
                
hide caption

Outside Columbus, Ohio, Scott Boyd is still managing to make those higher mortgage payments.
When he bought the home, he said, it felt like a guarantee of shelter and stability for him, his partner and the four kids they are raising together.
"" I made a commitment, saying no matter what, I want to build a home that is big enough. …  I wanted to get them in better schools and things like that,"" he said.
He was even able to help when his father-in-law became ill recently; they moved him into the finished basement, where he got home hospice care.
So when he was presented with the terms of that loan modification, he had not only kids in the house but his dying father-in-law too. He'd just gotten back on his feet financially and was being told his options were the loan modification or foreclosure.
""My job was still new, and that still felt shaky. … My PTSD was shaky. … It seemed like the right thing to do was just to take the modification,"" he said.
Two weeks after Boyd signed the loan modification, the VA launched the new Partial Claim Program that would have allowed him to keep his original loan terms with the same monthly payment and low 2.75% interest rate. It took two more months before the first mortgage company offered vets access to the PCP, and most of the industry is still working to stand up the program before the deadline that the VA gave it of Nov. 28.
Boyd said that he doesn't often talk about his service and that he doesn't feel entitled to be treated better than any other American. But he did not expect the VA to give him worse options than those of people who never served.
"" I really felt hurt more than anything. I wasn't angry. I was just like, I felt so defeated,"" Boyd said.
""Everything that I've done for this country and the things that I've been through, the things that I've seen, the things I've had to deal with … it's come down to, 'Yeah, I'm sorry — it's not in our systems yet. … There's nothing we can do about it.'""
NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews.
                Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

Every workplace needs realistic expectations, especially when employees are still learning the job.
The following story is about a new retail employee who was hired to work part-time in the store.
Her task was to stick a price to every candy bar and bag before putting them on the shelves.
She was given three pallets to finish by the end of the day, but after finishing the first pallet, the feedback she got was not what she was expecting at all.
Uh oh! Looks like she didn’t meet the expectations on her. Let’s take a closer look!
Today, I was assigned 3 pallets of candy, each pallet over 4 feet tall.
There were bags of candy, candy bars, and hand-size bags of candy.
It took me 3 hours to finish 1 pallet.
I had to put price stickers on each candy bar and bag because everything had to have a price.
To me, I felt like I was moving at a good pace.
This employee was shocked when she heard the feedback of the store manager.
My boss came over the walkie-talkie and asked me why it was taking me so long to work the candy.
He expected me to finish all 3 pallets, tagged and on the shelf, in 5 hours.
After my shift was over, the team lead and store manager pulled me to the back and asked why it took so long.
I was shocked because I honestly don’t see how I could have gone any faster.
I’m 3 weeks in, with 11 days worked. I am part-time.
She thought the manager’s expectations were unrealistic.
The store manager told me she could finish the 3 pallets, tagged and on the shelf, in the time it took me to do 1.
I’m gonna stay until I find another job.
I find her expectations unreasonable, maybe unrealistic.
I could work the candy to the shelf in an hour if I didn’t have to tag each individual piece.
Her coworkers were also pushing her to work faster.
Most of the folks have been working there for 3 years.
And for the past 2 days, they have been really pushing me to be faster.
I have worked other retail jobs and have never experienced this kind of expectation.
To be honest, expecting a new part-time employee to finish three pallets in five hours sounds demanding.
Perhaps, instead of pressuring her to finish on time, they should show her how to move faster.

76,2026-10-30,"
A Maserati GranTurismo is not something you would normally associate with a copper exterior and a glass canopy that opens like a cockpit. Yet that is exactly what makes the GFG Style Peralta D so intriguing. The one-off grand tourer trades the familiar curves of the Italian coupe for hand-hammered copper panels, a mirrored glass upper body and an unusual arrangement of gullwing doors. It is an ambitious exercise in coachbuilding by Fabrizio Giugiaro, created for Mexican entrepreneur and car collector Carlos Peralta.
Unveiled at the 2026 Pastejé Automotive Invitational in Mexico, the Peralta D is the second project commissioned by Peralta from GFG Style. It follows the Peralta S, a bespoke creation based on the Maserati MC20, introduced in 2025. This time, the starting point is the Maserati GranTurismo, with its 2+2 seating layout offering room for four occupants. The D in its name is a personal reference to Peralta’s daughters, just as the earlier S was dedicated to his sons.
Designer: GFG Style







The most striking bit of the performance car is undoubtedly its bodywork. GFG Style has replaced conventional painted panels with pure copper, shaped and hammered by hand to create the lower body. The metal’s natural reddish finish is left exposed, giving the car a character that would be difficult to achieve with paint alone. Above it, mirrored glass treated in a matching copper tone forms the upper section, creating an almost uninterrupted surface between the roof and body.
The design has a distinctly geometric character, with a wedge-shaped profile that recalls some of Giorgetto Giugiaro’s earlier automotive work. Anthracite-black alloy wheels feature a pattern inspired by the 1970s, while slim lighting elements and diamond-pattern aluminium grilles keep the front end relatively uncluttered. Carbon-fibre components are also present, although they are carefully integrated to avoid interrupting the copper-and-glass finish.







Getting inside is an experience in itself. Rather than using conventional doors, the front section opens through a single-motion glass canopy that tilts forward. The rear passengers have separate gullwing-style openings. The arrangement brings to mind the split-opening engine cover of the 1967 De Tomaso Mangusta, designed by Giorgetto Giugiaro, and gives the Peralta D a distinctive sense of occasion.
The cabin continues the material theme with metallic-effect leather, perforated Alcantara and genuine copper-thread stitching. There are some more personal touches, too. The passenger side of the dashboard houses a dedicated makeup station with cosmetic storage and an illuminated mirror display. At the back, a retractable refrigerator in the centre console is designed to hold a champagne bottle and four copper drinking glasses.


Beneath the bespoke bodywork, the Peralta D retains the GranTurismo’s mechanical foundations, combining a carbon-fibre structure with an aluminium chassis. GFG Style has confirmed a combustion-engine powertrain but has yet to disclose detailed engine specifications or performance figures.
There are no plans to put the Peralta D into production. It is a single commission, built around its owner’s tastes rather than the expectations of a wider market. That freedom is evident throughout the car, particularly in the way traditional metalworking and familiar automotive design references have been brought together in something that looks unlike any standard Maserati.

As we get over seeing the Brewers winning their first ever World Series, it's time to turn our attention to the hot stove! 
With Nov. 2 officially kicking off free agency, expect big-name players such as two-time Cy Young ace Tarik Skubal and Japanese slugger Teruaki Sato to get top dollar this offseason. Here's the latest from across the league:
 
Sonny Gray will receive a $10 million buyout as he is now a free agent. 
Gray led MLB with 18 wins and finished the year with a 2.72 ERA, the eighth-best mark among all qualified MLB pitchers and his lowest total since his All-Star 2015 season. 
As he enters his age-37 season, he remains a reliable arm, having thrown more than 160 innings and making at least 28 starts each of the last four seasons.
 
Bo Bichette is keeping his contract with the Mets, exercising a player option for a $42 million salary in 2027, the team said Friday.
Bichette agreed in January to a $126 million, three-year contract that included a $40 million signing bonus, a $2 million salary for 2026 and $42 million player options for both 2027 and 2028. Declining either option allows him to become a free agent again.
A two-time All-Star shortstop in Toronto, Bichette shifted primarily to third base in New York, where Francisco Lindor plays shortstop. The 28-year-old Bichette hit .260 with 18 homers and 78 RBIs for the disappointing Mets, down from .311 with 18 homers and 94 RBIs when he helped the Blue Jays win the American League pennant in 2025.
Outfielder Ronald Acuña Jr.'s $17 million option was exercised Thursday by the Atlanta Braves, who also exercised a $7 million option on second baseman Ozzie Albies.
Acuña, a five-time All-Star and the 2023 NL MVP, agreed in 2019 to a $100 million, eight-year contract that included the 2027 option, which had a $10 million buyout, and a $17 million option for 2028, which has no buyout.
Albies agreed in 2019 to $35 million, seven-year contract that included $7 million options for 2026 and 2027.
The Associated Press contributed to this report."
78,2026-10-30,"There are devices you need in your life, others for which you can put together a convincing justification – and then there are gadgets you quite simply want.
I said last month that I was wowed by the launch video for the iPhone Duo, despite it falling very clearly into the latter category for me – and playing with one hasn’t changed that in the slightest …
I had a very un-British reaction to the video for the new device.
I had extremely high expectations for this device yet was still blown away by just how good it appears to be. I’m British; the only time we ever use the word “wow” is sarcastically – but I still had to open this piece with it, without a trace of sarcasm.
This device isn’t the best match for me. To me, an iPhone is about 50% ultra-slim camera, 49% pocket computer, and 1% phone. The iPhone 18 Pro is a better device for my needs. However, I was experiencing extreme levels of technolust for the iPhone Duo. I’m not the target market for the iPhone Duo, but I do, absolutely, unquestionably want one.
I this week got a chance to visit an Apple store to spend about half an hour playing with the new device, and that did absolutely nothing to reduce my technolust.
The first thing to say is that the nanotexture surface of the inner display is gorgeous, and my quick snapshots don’t remotely do justice to it. Although intended primarily to disguise the crease, I found it a really beautiful screen. When looking at content with a white background, it’s really paper-like.
Indeed, once I’d been looking at it for a while, it was actually quite jarring to fold the Duo and see the glossy screen on the outside – all the more so when it showed reflections which were not at all visible on the inner display.
I honestly think Apple should have used nano-texture on the outer screen too, and even offer it as an option on other iPhones.
The iPhone Duo of course offers a split-screen view, with a new gesture to invoke it. Even in the very short time I was using the device, I found this almost immediately became second nature. From the home screen you simply drag an app up a little and then either left or right and it snaps into place.
Both apps feel very usable in this configuration, although in my view it would only make sense to use it when you are interacting with both, for example dragging content from one to another, like adding a photo to a note. I’m not much of a multi-tasker and, honestly, most people who claim to be aren’t either. For me the whole point of a phone display this size is greater immersion.
Most of the time, then, I’d want to take advantage of the iPad mini-like size of the display to give more space to a single app. For some content, like photos, it does rely on a matching aspect ratio to take full advantage of the display.
Other photo ratios get windowed, and look far less impressive.
For web browsing and similar applications, I would personally use it primarily in portrait mode.
Apple’s own apps seem well adapted to this mode. For example, the overlapping message pane in the Messages app, which can of course be dismissed.
For me, the least-convincing use case is watching video, where the aspect ratio simply doesn’t match most of today’s content. For example, this is how Foundation looks in the Apple TV app:
Incidentally, the camera punch hole looks very obvious above, but it really isn’t most of the time. With many backgrounds, it’s barely visible and not remotely intrusive. I had to angle the screen very carefully to be able to really maximise its visibility for this close-up:
I’d say it’s more visible in my other photos than it is in real-life.
Then there are the more whimsical orientations …
I have to say the touchscreen MacBook Pro is smaller than I expected pic.twitter.com/uLf4J3qjVi
Technolust:  A perfect term to describe me and the Duo.

Like Ben, I was absolutely wow'd when I got hands on at the Apple Store.  A Duo is in my future ... some day.
I mostly dictate text on an iPhone rather than type it, and can’t imagine anyone would really use the Duo in laptop mode very often, but honestly there’s nothing wrong with a bit of whimsy with the device. It’s in large part a fun thing – even if it is rather pricey as a toy.
The iPhone Duo is a beautiful device, and Apple has clearly put a huge amount of thought into both the hardware and the software.
It’s a very pocketable device, and I really love the idea of having a screen size close to that of the iPad mini on me at all times. I mentioned that I’d been using an iPad mini to preview the iPhone Duo experience earlier this month, and the Duo actually feels even better thanks to smaller bezels and that beautiful paper-like display.
Yes, it’s exceedingly expensive, and yes, having the priciest iPhone lack the best cameras would be a huge compromise for me. But while I still don’t think it would be the best iPhone for my needs, I do absolutely, undeniably want one.

79,2026-10-30,"From the rapidly rising and falling values of Bitcoin to high-profile heists like the hacking of Binance, the world of cryptocurrencies is often in the headlines.
The world's biggest crypto exchange said hackers had gained access to $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) worth of digital assets, and while it has frozen about half, many remained in the hands of the thieves.
These stories are full of specialist language that people outside the industry struggle to understand.
So if you're unsure what the blockchain or a crypto echange or even a cryptocurrency itself are, here are some key terms and what they mean.
While many may struggle with the finer points of crypto, pretty much everyone has heard of its most famous product: Bitcoin. But what actually is it?
Bitcoin is a cryptocurrency, which is to say a type of digital currency. Unlike traditional currencies, Bitcoin is not controlled by centralised financial institutions. 
This makes it popular for people who think decentralisation can bring financial freedom, but it also makes it extremely volatile with it rising and falling in value at the whim of Bitcoin buyers and sellers.
Donald Trump has pledged to make the US the ""crypto capital of the world"" - backtracking on his previous claim that Bitcoin was a ""scam"".
Its price topped a much-awaited threshold of $100,000 in December 2024, then rose to $120,000 in July 2025 as US politicians prepared to debate bills to regulate digital assets.
In October 2025, Bitcoin hit an all-time high of around $126,000, buoyed by a continued wave of institutional investment and positive market sentiment. But it has been known to plummet in value just as quickly as it spikes. 
In early February 2026, its price dipped below $65,000 to effectively wipe out gains achieved under President Trump.
Against the backdrop of these frequent price fluctuations, speculation continues about the true identity of Bitcoin's mysterious inventor known only as Satoshi Nakamoto.
Brit says he is not elusive Bitcoin creator named by New York Times
Seven wild moments from the turbulent story of Bitcoin
Blockchain is the technology underpinning all cryptocurrencies, and many related products like non-fungible tokens (NFTs). In essence, it is a virtual spreadsheet on which all the buying and selling of crypto is recorded. They are arranged in blocks linked together in a giant chain - hence the name.
Every cryptocurrency transaction is individually recorded onto the blockchain by a huge network of volunteers verifying its authenticity by using computer programmes.
The incentive to do this for Bitcoin's network is that the first person to validate transactions is rewarded in Bitcoin. This potentially lucrative process, known as mining, is also controversial because of the incredible amount of energy, external used as people the world over race to be the first to successfully update the blockchain.
This video can not be played
Are cryptocurrencies the future of money?
A crypto exchange is the digital platform where investors can buy, sell and trade cryptocurrencies. 
Similar to traditional investing, a crypto exchange acts as a brokerage where people can transfer traditional money, like pounds or dollars, in exchange for cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin or Ethereum. Most transactions are accompanied by fees.
Binance is the world's biggest, which is part of what makes the hack so monumental.
A crypto wallet is a place where investors hold their cryptocurrency. There are two types, a hot wallet and a cold wallet. 
Hot wallets are connected to the internet, and thus more accessible for quick transfers and easy access. 
Cold wallets are physical devices like specially designed USB sticks that store crypto offline typically for safer and longer term storage.
Man told he can't recover Â£598m of Bitcoin from landfill site
Ethereum is used to describe both the second largest cryptocurrency after Bitcoin, represented by the Ether token, and the blockchain underpinning it. This supports an array of different applications and digital assets, such as non-fungible tokens.
It functions in a similar way to Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies, but in 2022 switched to a greener operating system requiring less computers and energy.
What are NFTs and why are some worth millions?
ETFs are portfolios that let investors bet on multiple assets without having to buy any themselves. Traded on stock exchanges like shares, their value depends on how the overall portfolio performs in real time. They can comprise a combination of gold and silver bullion, for example, or a mix of shares in both technology and insurance companies.
A spot Bitcoin ETF buys the cryptocurrency directly, ""on the spot"", at its current price, throughout the day. 
While some ETFs already contained Bitcoin indirectly, the US approved several spot Bitcoin ETFs in January 2024. This allowed new investors, such as investment management firms like Blackrock and Fidelity, to enter the speculative world of Bitcoin without having to worry about digital wallets or navigating crypto exchanges.
How big banks are becoming 'Bitcoin whales'
Meme coins are a form of cryptocurrency used for fun and speculation. 
They are typically created to capitalise on social media trends or viral memes - such as for internet-famous pygmy hippo Moo Deng. 
But meme coins are heavily criticised for their speculative nature and the risk involved for investors, as they often have little prospect of accumulating value over time.
They can also be susceptible to ""rug pulls"" - where promoters of a cryptocurrency draw in buyers to raise the price of their coin, only to stop trading activity and make off with the money raised from sales.
Some celebrities have faced backlash after launching meme coins.
'A mockery': Trump's meme-coin sparks anger in crypto world
Online star Hawk Tuah girl faces crypto coin criticism
""Stable"" is the key word here - this cryptocurrency differs from others as it is intends to be less volatile in value.
This typically works by the price being linked to an existing asset, for instance currencies like the US dollar or pound sterling, which in theory should make them more stable in price than cryptocurrencies that are not backed by assets.
Stablecoins themselves are usually controlled by companies that provide them, with transactions recorded on digital ledgers. While held up by some as the future of finance, high profile price collapses of stablecoins have alerted regulators to risks for investors and prompted scrutiny over their supposed stability.
Cryptocurrency firm Terraform, behind the crashed TerraUSD and Luna tokens, files for bankruptcy
XRP is a cryptocurrency used by a platform called the XRP Ledger. It was created by the co-founders of financial services company Ripple Labs in 2012 as a cheaper, faster alternative to Bitcoin.
The cryptocurrency has a fixed supply of 100 billion coins, which were created when it launched. Much of it is held by Ripple and periodically released into circulation.
Unlike cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin, transactions made using XRP are verified through consensus - whereby the majority of validators on its peer-to-peer network must agree whether or not a transaction is valid before it is added to its blockchain.
This has been credited with allowing many transactions to take place simultaneously, at high speed and low cost - making it appealing to financial institutions or for processes like cross-border payments. But as with other cryptocurrencies, XRP has received regulatory scrutiny and seen sudden, sharp declines in value.

The Swiss city that lets you pay for most things with bitcoin
The Bitcoin hum that is unsettling Trump's MAGA heartlands

80,2026-10-30,"Every good idea has already been had. All the best hiking shoes have already been designed. Everything that's come after has been, quite literally in the latter case, a retread.
So says od.A, a young footwear brand proud to produce ""nothing new.""
od.A's shoes are beautiful to behold, streamlined slabs of nappa leather and waxed nubuck that look as much like trail shoes' future as they do their past. (The debut Launch Collection is naturally available exclusively at progressive retailer Dover Street Market.)
Because, as artful as the shoes may be — od.A co-founder Albert Arenas tells me that inspiration came from the restless ingenuity of Issey Miyake, the material genius of Stone Island founder Massimo Osti, and the lived-in feel of interior designer Axel Vervoordt — they're also steeped in historic design cues visible only on closer inspection. The shoes were already seeded to a handful of discerning gents in the outdoorsy-style space and each was duly impressed (or maybe just glad to be sent something fresh).
Founded towards the ""end of 2023,"" Arenas says, and ""based between Barcelona and Shanghai,"" od.A's mission is to bring a little humanity back into a realm that lost it long ago. The great functional footwear of yesteryear was beautifully inelegant: clunky, bulbous, and defined entirely by purpose over aesthetic. And you saw the makers' hands in everything from the silhouettes to even the finishing, where nothing was necessarily perfect. And, yet, they were.
More importantly, they lasted, both as objects and as stylistic touchstones. Attempts to reinvent the trail shoe as something wholly new have largely failed — we circle the same few shapes to this day.
od.A knows this. It seeks to reclaim the untamed edge that's since been focus-grouped out of the realm of performance (not performative) outdoor shoes. Its debut silhouettes appear familiar but are actually not quite: an approach-style shoe's handsewn upper makes it look more like a gentleman walking sneaker than a badass hiker. Vibram's Gironda sole renders the 002 classically tough but the refined upper, made even sleeker by a zippered sheath, is unexpectedly elegant. There's even a convertible outdoor loafer.
It's singular stuff, a welcome breaststroke against the tide of an overcrowded footwear market lousy with microplastics and polyesters. Because the best part of these rugged but sensitive shoes is that, like the trail shoes of old, they're destined to only get better with age.
Let's go back to the beginning. What's od.A origin story? 
Albert Arenas: We had worked together in the creative, fashion, and footwear industries for years. We started as [sneaker] collectors. As students, we sought out the shoes we loved and learned from them. We researched the history of each brand and took the shoes apart to study how they were built and understand why some worked, or looked, better than others. That research is still the part we enjoy most. 
The brand name feels open to interpretation. Is it?
In a way, yes, although its original meaning is quite specific. The name comes from oda, Spanish for ""ode"": a tribute. The brand is an ode to the outdoor silhouettes of the 1980s, 1990s and early 2000s, and to the design solutions passed down from one generation of shoes to the next. It also honors ancestral ways of making shoes, and a way of manufacturing built around long-lasting objects.
The name is also an acronym. od.A was born from Outdoor Archive, with the A deliberately set apart so it could take on new meanings over time. Outdoor Artefacts, Outdoor Assembly, Outdoor Anatomy, Outdoor Atmosphere, Outdoor Allegory, and others yet to be defined. We borrow that from wabi-sabi: the acceptance that things change.
So, the name is not open because its meaning is unclear. It is open because it is designed to evolve.
Evolution by looking backwards. Right now, it feels like everyone is looking backwards — the market is saturated with outdoorsy footwear. Does that sort of thing bother you?
The market is a challenge we embraced from the start. We began while ""gorpcore"" was at its height. We make outdoor-inspired shoes, so we are part of that conversation, but our focus has not changed since: od.A has an anthropological basis. We focus on why certain designs and techniques have lasted, and pay little attention to the shoes going viral. A trend needs a name behind it. A shoe that lasts doesn't.
We call it a denial of fashion egotism. For us, that means no single designer or creative director takes the credit. od.A is teamwork. Each of us does our best in our own field, and none of us needs that credit.
That relates to your mantra of ""nothing new,"" i.e. every shoe walks a trail blazed by every previous shoe. Can you talk more about that mentality?
We don't put history on a pedestal; we rework it. Every design starts from a reference. There is a right kind of newness: the kind you can trace back.
Before we drew the 002.1, we saw how ski boots evolved at an exhibition of historic ski equipment in the Vall de Núria in the Pyrenees. They started as laced mountain boots with a sole shaped to fit the binding. Then, to keep snow and water out, the closure moved to a diagonal, and a single piece of tough leather came to protect the front of the foot. The 002.1 keeps that logic, with a hard ripstop panel over the instep and a sealed, waterproof YKK zip. 
Speaking of the process, what was the biggest learning curve?
What we underestimated was time: getting the shoe we had in mind took nearly three years. 
Time, indeed. Three years later, how do you define a ""good"" shoe? 
The one you want to wear every day, or the one you miss when you are wearing something else. To get there, comfort comes first; aesthetics always come second. A shoe you wear every day, exposed to the elements, should age well and look better, or at least more personal, with use.
So, it must perform, and the performance we care about most is durability. The form is never finished: the person wearing it completes it.
Let's go a step bigger. Why shoes at all?
Because a shoe is never only an object. Each design began as a tool shaped by what a community needed and came to say something about who wore it. Studying shoes closely taught us that every construction carries that history: the problem it was solving and the people it was made for. That is what captivates us. 
We come from different cultures, and a shoe is a shared language, the meeting point where we understand each other.
Jake Silbert is Highsnobiety's Director of News. An award-winning writer, Jake has a decade of experience overseeing fashion publications, leading editorial teams, and publishing print media. Previously, Jake lived in Japan following a multi-year obsession with Japanese fashion.

Marijuana


Jacob Sullum
|
10.30.2026 10:00 AM

A decade ago, 54 percent of Massachusetts voters said yes to Question 4, a ballot initiative that legalized possession, home cultivation, and commercial distribution of recreational marijuana. Judging from recent surveys, voters are not inclined to reverse that decision by approving Question 8, an initiative on next Tuesday's ballot.
Question 8, a.k.a. the Eliminate Recreational Marijuana Sales and Allow Limited Possession Initiative, would permit adults 21 or older to possess up to an ounce of cannabis. But they would have to get it from the black market, since Question 8 would not allow them to grow it or buy it from state-licensed retailers.
The initiative ""would keep personal possession of marijuana legal, keep medical marijuana legal, and stop commercial marijuana stores,"" its supporters say. They allege that legalization has impaired ""productivity"" and led to increases in ""fatal car accidents,"" ""workplace injuries,"" ""healthcare costs,"" ""crime (like DUI's),"" ""ER visits,"" and ""mental health conditions like psychosis, severe anxiety, and schizophrenia, particularly in young adults.""
Voters do not seem impressed by those arguments. In eight polls between mid-February and mid-October, support for Question 8 ranged from 20 percent to 41 percent. In the most recent survey, conducted by The Boston Globe, 71 percent of respondents said they planned to vote no on Question 8, which suggests that experience with legalization has substantially increased opposition to pot prohibition in Massachusetts.
Question 8's sponsor, the Coalition for a Healthy Massachusetts, is funded almost entirely by Smart Approaches to Marijuana (SAM). As of October 20, the Virginia-based group had spent $3.7 million on the project, compared to $2.8 million in spending by the opposition, which includes several marijuana businesses.
SAM's co-founder, Kevin Sabet, who served as a drug policy adviser during the Clinton, George W. Bush, and Obama administrations, says he was motivated by the concerns of Massachusetts residents who approached him. ""Many of them voted for this initiative 10 years ago and feel betrayed,"" Sabet told WGBH, an NPR station in Boston. If so, the polls indicate such converts are far outnumbered by former Question 4 opponents who have traveled that road in the opposite direction.
""We're under no illusions that the bluest state in the country is going to pass this easily with flying colors,"" Sabet told WBUR, another Boston NPR station, a few weeks ago. ""But we want to have that conversation.""
The conversation clearly is not going the way Sabet hoped. Massachusetts, which was the fifth state to legalize recreational marijuana, is now one of two dozen, which together account for most of the U.S. population. In October 2025, Gallup put nationwide support for marijuana legalization at 64 percent, which was down a bit from the peak of 70 percent recorded in 2023 but still 16 points higher than in 2012, when Colorado and Washington started this ball rolling.
Public support for legalizing recreational marijuana tends to be even higher in states that have done it. In Civiqs surveys last December, support in 15 states where voters had approved legalization ranged from 62 percent in Missouri to 77 percent in Oregon. It had increased since legalization in every state but New Jersey, where it was down one point from the 67 percent of voters who approved Public Question 1 in 2020. In Massachusetts, support had risen from 54 percent to 76 percent.
The fate of two other drug policy initiatives that voters will consider next week is less clear. As far as I can tell, pollsters have not asked voters about them.
Colorado's Proposition 132 would make manufacturing or distributing any amount of illicit fentanyl a Level 1 drug felony punishable by a mandatory minimum sentence of eight years. Under current law, the penalties vary depending on the amount of fentanyl involved.
In Idaho, House Joint Resolution 4, a proposed constitutional amendment, would strip voters of the power to legalize marijuana or any other psychoactive substance. If the initiative passes, which requires approval by a simple majority, that power will be reserved to the state legislature.

Jacob Sullum is a senior editor at Reason. He is the author, most recently, of Beyond Control: Drug Prohibition, Gun Regulation, and the Search for Sensible Alternatives (Prometheus Books).

82,2026-10-30,"When the Brewers and Rays ​met this year on MLB’s biggest stage, it marked the first World Series clash between two of baseball’s smallest spenders in 35 ⁠years.
Save
Share
The small-market World Series in Major League Baseball wrapped up this week, with the Milwaukee Brewers winning their first title in franchise history.
The title triumph came 44 years after Milwaukee’s only other trip to the Fall Classic, when they lost to the St Louis Cardinals in seven games in 1982.
But beyond the memorable victory, the series’ television ratings made for underwhelming reading, raising questions about baseball’s reach and relevance in a United States sports market dominated by American football and basketball.
Al Jazeera breaks down the numbers and more:
The World Series, the championship series of the 2026 MLB season, saw the American League champion Tampa Bay Rays and the National League champion Milwaukee Brewers compete in a best-of-seven playoff series.
At the end of the October 23-28 playoff series, Milwaukee were crowned winners, as they clinched their first World Series title.
Milwaukee’s 57-year wait for a championship ended on Wednesday after defeating the Tampa Bay Rays 4-1 to wrap up the Fall Classic in five games.

While the final Nielsen ratings report is still pending, estimates show an average of 11.82 million viewers tuned in for the 2026 World Series across the Fox network.
Milwaukee’s 10-1 win at Tampa in Game 3 attracted the lowest audience, 8.33 million, while Game 5, in which Milwaukee sealed the championship, drew the highest with 14.22 million viewers.
The overall average is much lower than the 2025 and 2024 World Series, which drew 15.49 million and 15.1 million viewers, respectively, as the Los Angeles Dodgers won the title both times.
 

 
This year’s World Series’ underwhelming viewership could be linked to the two teams’ relatively low payrolls and markets – the Brewers’ payroll ranks 19th in MLB ($146m), and the Rays are 28th ($95m).
The Brewers also rank as the smallest market team in MLB, generating the lowest local commercial and TV revenue, while the Rays rank squarely in the league’s middle.
Hence, when the Brewers and Rays met this year on MLB’s biggest stage, it marked the first World Series clash between two of baseball’s smallest spenders in 35 ⁠years.
On the other hand, nine-time World Series winners Dodgers, who competed in the last two World Series, boast a star-studded lineup on a hefty payroll, featuring marquee star Shohei Ohtani, former MVP Mookie Betts, 10-time All-Star Freddie Freeman and others.
Those factors keep them in the tier 1 market size category – one that represents the biggest media and population footprints in the sport.
The 2026 World Series’ viewership figures also pale in comparison to those of other major US sports leagues.
Basketball’s 2026 NBA Finals, in which the New York Knicks defeated the San Antonio Spurs 4-1 to end a 53-year drought, attracted a series average of 20.58 million viewers. Game 5 was the most-watched NBA Finals game 5 since 1998, averaging 24.54 million viewers and peaking at 33 million.
Elsewhere in international football, the 2026 FIFA World Cup, held in North America, also produced record numbers. Spain’s 1-0 extra-time win over Argentina delivered a combined average audience of 62.8 million across Fox and Telemundo, making it the most-watched football match ever in the United States.
 

 
American football, by far, is the most-watched sport in the US.
A Nielsen report found that an estimated 125.6 million viewers watched the National Football League’s Super Bowl LX, as the Seattle Seahawks beat the New England Patriots 29-13.
NBC’s presentation of Super Bowl LX ranked as the second most-watched Super Bowl in history, behind the 127.7 million viewers who watched Super Bowl LIX in 2025.
The only major American sports league that performs weakly against MLB is the National Hockey League (NHL), a professional ice hockey league in North America.
An average of 5.2 million viewers tuned in for the 2026 Stanley Cup, won by the Carolina Hurricanes, who defeated the Vegas Golden Knights 4-2.
MLB initially scheduled to host two games in London in mid-2026, with the New York Yankees and Toronto Blue Jays pencilled in to play on June 13 and 14 at London Stadium, the home ground of English football team West Ham United.
But the plan was cancelled due to scheduling issues with the venue and baseball’s television partner.
West Ham played their 2025-26 Premier League season’s final match against Leeds United at the ground on May 24, leaving very little time to convert the field to baseball, which can take up to three weeks.
Furthermore, MLB was unable to schedule games at the same venue later in June because Fox lacked available broadcast slots as that period clashed with the FIFA World Cup.
MLB games have been played abroad on several occasions, from Japan to Australia, since it was first played in Mexico in 2000.
MLB is also working on grassroots initiatives in India, known for its affinity for cricket, and last week hosted MLBx, a fast-paced 3-on-3 baseball competition that brought together baseball and softball stars.
Yes, major US sports leagues have made strides in expanding their international footprints, regularly hosting regular-season games, exhibition matches and special events abroad.
The NFL hosts regular-season games abroad in locations including London, Munich, Madrid, Sao Paulo and Mexico City.
Last month, the league expanded its presence with games in Melbourne, and the NFL’s first game Down Under drew 100,021 fans, making it the seventh most-attended regular-season game in NFL history.
With a large portion of the league’s player base and revenue coming from outside the US, the NBA also plays regular-season and preseason games in international cities like Paris, Abu Dhabi and Mexico City.

BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
Topic:Budget
The Victorian government is forecasting a drop in tax revenue linked to the cooling property market. (ABC News: Danielle Bonica)
Reduced revenue from stamp duty and land taxes is expected to shave more than $1 billion off Victorian budget forecasts over the next four years.
A fiscal update blames higher interest rates and ""other factors"" for the decline in property market activity.
However, there has been a slight reduction to the state's eye-watering debt forecasts.
Victoria's cooling property market has delivered a $5 billion blow to the state's budget forecasts less than a month before the state election.
An average of $1.3 billion a year is set to be shaved from the state's tax revenue over the four-year forecast period, according to a fiscal update handed down today.
""This is primarily driven by downgrades to land transfer duty and land tax, reflecting a decline in property market activity amid higher interest rates and other factors,"" the update said.
The government is still on track to deliver operating surpluses over the outlook period — but the expected average of $300 million a year is a significant reduction from the $1 billion forecast in the May budget.
And net debt will be slightly lower than previously forecast, owing to a lower-than-anticipated debt position in 2025-26.
Debt will climb to $197 billion in 2029-30, about $2 billion less than previously forecast.
But interest expense payments continues to eat into state coffers.
The fiscal update shows annual interest payments will be $8.9 billion this year — $24.3 million a day — rising to $11.9 billion in 2029-30, or $32.8 million a day.
""Victoria's finances are deteriorating and economic headwinds are accelerating,"" said Martin Codina, executive director of the non-partisan campaign group Amplify.
""Victoria urgently needs a plan to stop borrowing and pay down debt so that all Victorians can have confidence in the future,"" he said.
The books spell out nearly half a billion in savings since the last budget and also clarify that a clandestine myki surcharge uncovered by the auditor-general earlier this year, which Premier Ben Carroll said would abolished, will formally cease to exist on January 1. 
The tax-reliant state is still expected to capture $9.2 billion in land transfer duty and $7.6 billion in property-related taxes in the current financial year.
The drop in property-related taxes was just one of several risks to Victoria's economic outlook that the report said ""have increased in recent months and are elevated"".
Falling prices in some parts of Melbourne's housing market is estimated to shrink stamp duty and land tax revenue.  (774 ABC Melbourne: Simon Leo Brown)
Among them was recently announced federal changes to migration.
""If net overseas migration declines by more than currently expected, this could weigh on the longer-term economic outlook for the state through fewer consumers, lower education exports and a smaller pool of workers,"" the report said.
It also warned increased fuel and material costs, some linked to the prolonged conflict in the Middle East, could cause further cost headaches for Victoria's infrastructure projects.
Colin Brooks took on the role of Victorian treasurer in August this year. (ABC News: Danielle Bonica)
Asked if his government took responsibility for the property market downturn, Treasurer Colin Brooks said the entire country had witnessed a similar decline, particularly in the eastern states.
""This is something that's impacting the whole country, driven by inflation, interest rates and also some changes in the federal budget settings as well,"" he said.
Mr Brooks said the $2.4 billion operating surplus recorded last financial year assured the state could responsibly deliver projects moving forward.
Topic:Local Government
Topic:Local Government
Topic:Multiculturalism
Topic:Ambulance Service
Topic:State and Territory Elections
Melbourne has been at the forefront of a cooling property market, with declining auction clearance rates and subdued price growth linked to a slowdown in investment.
Mr Carroll has vowed to reverse increases to land tax unveiled in 2023 as part of a post-COVID budget repair plan if Labor is re-elected next month.
The tax, along with high interest rates and changing tenancy laws, have been credited with an exodus of property investors from Victoria.
In a separate report tabled today, the auditor-general's review of the Treasury's papers showed the state continued to record operating cash deficits, and called for more long-term approach to budget repair.
""The current fiscal strategy remains focused on short-term outcomes. While recent operating financial results have improved, fiscal cash deficits persist, debt remains relatively high, and long-term expenditure pressures continue to grow,'' the report saide
The Coalition has conceded that it will not pause construction on the contentious Suburban Rail Loop despite saying its policy is to ""pause and review"" the $34 billion project.
The government has confirmed pausing construction works, including tunnel boring, would cost at least $1 billion. 
Jess Wilson's opposition has now said that it would not pause construction if elected on November 28.
Shadow Attorney-General James Newbury today qualified that work would only be paused if that did not breach existing contracts. 
The Coalition had previously said workers would still be paid when work was stopped.
""The tools will be downed on anything that is not a breach of contract,"" he said, adding that workers would still be paid.
Works are already underway on the Suburban Rail Loop and the government has said pausing them would cost at least $1 billion. (ABC News: Simon Tucci)
Contracts totalling $15 billion have already been signed, and tunnelling is underway.
The cost of pausing and altogether cancelling the $35 billion SRL East has remained unclear throughout debate about the project's future, but frontbencher Anthony Carbines today said it would cost taxpayers $1 billion to ""stop the project"".
Mr Brooks, the treasurer, said the Coalition had now ""effectively"" adopted Labor's position.
""[It's] a complete backflip, they've effectively admitting they were lying to the Victorian people,'' he said.
Mr Carroll already cut $1 billion from the project, in part by cutting overpasses, walkways and car parks attached to planned stations. 
He has also vowed to find another $1 billion through a ""technical"" review.
The first stage of a broader rail project spanning decades, SRL East is intended to link Cheltenham and Box Hill in Melbourne's east, but has been subject to speculation over whether its price tag and funding model are reliable.

We all want to help out a friend in need. If it’s someone we trust, value, and genuinely care about, offering them a couch and a roof over their head is simply part of what friendship is about — as long as the feeling is mutual, of course.

But sometimes, we don’t truly know who someone is until we live with them, even if they’re childhood friends. That seemed to be the case for a woman on r/AITAH, who had temporarily allowed a friend to stay at her place, only for things to take a nasty turn after an argument. The woman ended up stealing her belongings, and things only went downhill from there.

Getty Images / Unsplash (not the actual photo)





RDNE Stock project / Pexels (not the actual photo)



DrD3adpool

Matt Popovich / Unsplash (not the actual photo)




DrD3adpool
One doesn’t even need to touch on the illegality of what the woman has done — robbery is a crime everywhere. However, we probably do need to address the mental illness aspect. According to data published by the American Psychological Association (APA), the majority of people with mental health conditions are actually non-violent and are far more likely to be victims of crime than perpetrators.
Now, we can’t know for certain what motivated her behavior, but studies also indicate that 50% of individuals with severe mental health disorders have co-occurring substance use disorders. As a result, crimes like theft or even selling stolen items through relatives, as allegedly happened with the cousin, may point to behavioral issues beyond clinical symptoms.
But that’s hardly the only problem in this story. The family ends up taking the lion’s share of the blame, too. Their extreme reaction is a textbook example of systematic enabling and externalized blame. This is a common pattern in families where someone has an untreated mental health condition, since relatives can develop an unhealthy habit of protecting that person from the consequences of their actions.
magnific / Magnific (not the actual photo)
Now, it’s not as though the woman asked to be robbed; however, she didn’t do everything entirely right. This is because psychological studies show that being overly empathetic or a people-pleaser — as the narrator describes herself — can lead someone to prioritize another person’s comfort at the expense of their own safety and mental health.
Now, here’s why the story needed an update. In her testimony, the narrator claimed she pressed charges, and the comment section berated her for it. That’s because under the US criminal justice system, private citizens don’t actually press charges. Instead, victims file police reports, while the decision to prosecute rests entirely with the state, usually through a district attorney or city prosecutor.
With that clarified, what can the woman do? Legal experts generally recommend letting the criminal justice process run its course and allowing the courts to handle matters such as restitution and processing evidence. But tell us, Pandas: do you think the woman should have left the matter behind, or did she do exactly what she had to do? Let us know!

Amazon reports that it earned a net income of $21.1 billion on revenues of $202 billion in the quarter ending September 30, 2026. Those figures represent a decline of 2.4 percent and a gain of 12 percent, respectively, year-over-year (YOY).
“Our AWS investment in functionality and infrastructure is working,” Amazon president and CEO Andy Jassy said. “Our broader selection and speedier delivery are helping us capture more retail market segment share with improving margins, and our newer businesses, ranging from Grocery to Pharmacy to Amazon Leo to Zoox are progressing encouragingly. There has never been a trillion dollar revenue business, and we may have two of them in the next 5-10 years.”
Amazon Web Services is on a tear, with $46 billion in revenues in the quarter, a gain of 39 percent YOY. But Amazon says it still plans to spend $220 billion this year on capital expenditures related to its AI buildout. It spent $77 billion in this quarter alone on property and equipment, far more than expected. And all the spending delivered a $7.6 billion negative free cash flow in the quarter and $42.4 billion for the year.
All that and missing its profit and sales targets triggered an 11 percent decrease in the value of Amazon’s stock.

30 October 2026


Samsung Apple Xiaomi Oppo vivo Mobile hardware Other

According to a new report by research firm Omdia, global smartphone shipments declined 20 percent year-on-year in the third quarter of 2026. Manufacturers shipped a total of 254.7 million units during the quarter.
The report noted that this was the lowest third-quarter shipment volume since 2013, attributing the decline to rising smartphone prices.
Samsung retained its position as the world's largest smartphone maker, shipping 57.8 million units, however, it was down 5 percent year-on-year.
Apple followed closely with 56.5 million iPhones shipped, keeping its shipment numbers unchanged compared to the same quarter last year.
Xiaomi secured the third spot with 30.7 million units shipped, marking a 29 percent decline year-on-year.
Oppo ranked fourth with 23.4 million units, down 39 percent from last year, while vivo rounded out the top five with 19.3 million units shipped, representing a 33 percent decline compared to Q3 2025.
The report notes that brands are focusing on profitable markets and key products amid component shortages, adjusting RAM/storage configurations and trimming model lineups to manage rising costs and protect margins.
Omdia expects global smartphone shipments to decline by a further 12 percent this year, with the market likely to continue falling in 2027, although at a slower pace.

87,2026-10-30,"Bitcoin held above $76,000 after a $6.3 billion Binance hack and a tech-stock sell-off, as momentum indicators pointed to a possible rebound.
Bitcoin (BTC) traded above $77,000 on Friday as crypto market liquidations passed $1.5 billion over 24 hours.

Key points:


Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD dropping as low as $76,320 amid the fallout from a $6.3 billion hack of the largest cryptocurrency exchange Binance. 


BTC/USD one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Rolling 24-hour liquidations of traders across the industry reached $1.56 billion. Long positions accounted for the majority of this figure at $1.31 billion, per CoinGlass data.


BTC/USD vs. crypto liquidations (screenshot). Source: CoinGlass

In its latest update on X, Binance described the attack as “complex,” reporting more than a single security breach during the episode.

“We are still gathering the facts, and will share more directly as we have them,” it wrote.


Downside momentum cooled after the company confirmed that all user losses would be covered from its dedicated SAFU fund and other company resources.

The hack added to macro pressure on crypto, with US stock markets falling on Thursday after tech earnings fell short of expectations. Alphabet missed earnings and revenue targets on Wednesday. In the aftermath, GOOGL fell by 13% to $282, its lowest level since April.


Alphabet stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Intel shares plunged 26% premarket on Friday as the company issued a weaker-than-expected outlook, even though Q3 revenue and earnings beat estimates. Intel said it expects Q4 sales of $15 billion to $16 billion, versus analysts’ projected $17 billion to $18 billion.


Intel stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView


On lower timeframes, BTC price metrics returned to levels not seen since BTC/USD hit multiyear lows near $57,000 in late June. 

Related: Strategy eyes more Bitcoin buys as Saylor touts $30B credit capacity

On Thursday, price closed below the lower band of the Bollinger Bands volatility indicator, a phenomenon last observed on June 25. Such a move reflects that price is trading more than two standard deviations below its 20-day simple moving average (SMA). While Bollinger Bands do not produce buy and sell signals, a close below the lower band can imply that a mean reversion is due. The 20-day SMA currently sits at $81,324.


BTC/USD one-day chart with Bollinger Bands data. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The relative strength index (RSI), a popular leading indicator for price momentum, dropped to 34.7 on its 100-point scale on Friday. This is its lowest reading since June 30 and approaching its “oversold” zone below 30.

On the four-hour timeframe, RSI displayed a bullish divergence from the Bitcoin price, delivering a higher swing low compared to a lower low on BTC/USD. Such divergences can signal weakening seller control and a possible local trend change.



BTC/USD four-hour chart with RSI data. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Along with an updated preview for CBS's Cupertino, S01E04: ""A.J. & Buddy,"" we've got an important programming note about November 8th.

We're back with our weekly look at what's ahead with the first season of Robert King and Michelle King's Mike Colter and Rachel Keller-starring Cupertino has to offer. In S01E04: ""A.J. & Buddy,"" Michael (Colter) and Olivia (Keller) defend the founders of a prediction market site. Of course, that's not an easy thing to do when one of them has fled arrest on a private jet – and with a very familiar face aboard. We've got an official overview, image gallery, trailer, and more waiting for you below. In addition, we've got overviews and images for S01E05: ""Celeste,"" S01E06: ""Harper,"" and S01E07: ""YUNGWOOD"" – as well as this very important programming note about Sunday, November 8th:
CBS will present a special airing of the critically acclaimed new series rated 100% Fresh on Rotten Tomatoes, CUPERTINO, on Sunday, Nov. 8, featuring an all-new episode (""Harper"") at 8:00 PM ET/PT, followed by two encore episodes (""Michael & Olivia"" and ""Vik & Bo""). Cupertino Season 1 Episode 4: ""A.J. & Buddy"" – Michael and Olivia defend the young founders of a prediction market site, one of whom has been arrested while the other flees on his private jet with Christy in tow after a night out together. Meanwhile, Natalia and Nichole spend quality time together at the firm. Amanda Warren guest stars as District Attorney Sally Clausen. Written by Joey Hartstone and directed by Robert King.

Cupertino Season 1 Episode 5: ""Celeste"" – Olivia recruits her friend Celeste to be the lead plaintiff in a class-action lawsuit against an AI training company accused of wage and overtime abuse.
