Written by Keith Eisner and directed by Rosemary Rodriguez.
Cupertino Season 1 Episode 7: ""YUNGWOOD"" – Michael and Olivia try to save art and original music by suing an AI-music studio that is releasing copycat songs and negatively impacting legendary rapper YUNGWOOD's (Method Man) streaming revenue. Also, Michael fights through familial disagreements when he goes up against his dad as opposing counsel. Cliff Smith (Method Man) guest stars as legendary rapper YUNGWOOD. Written by Aurin Squire and directed by Rob Hardy.
CBS's Cupertino is a David vs. Goliath legal drama set in the heart of Silicon Valley that follows a lawyer (Mike Colter) who is being cheated out of his stock options by his former employer, a tech start-up. Refusing to back down, he joins forces with another recently fired attorney (Rachel Keller) to represent those taken advantage of by the tech elite and help them fight back in a high-stakes battle against the Goliaths controlling Silicon Valley. Joining Colter and Keller are Ella Stiller (Christy Palladino) and Nik Dodani (Vikram Malhotra), with Busy Philipps (Natalia Boutella) and Renée Elise Goldsberry (Nichole E. Ferebee).

""We are interested in legal shows only in the way that they reflect what's going on in the reality of the moment,"" Robert shared during a profile of the upcoming CBS series. ""The reality of the moment is tech is everywhere, AI is everywhere, and the Goliaths that control trillions and trillions of dollars and the economy are in one place: Silicon Valley. The difference between what the top people are making and those in the middle and the bottom, and how AI is closing down middle management, there's never been a David versus Goliath-ness of this size, where the Goliaths are so large, and the Davids are so small.""

Produced by CBS Studios, the series is executive-produced by Robert King, Michelle King, Liz Glotzer, and Sam Hoffman.

						UPDATED 21:17 EDT / OCTOBER 29 2026					


																																	by								Mike Wheatley

Updated with Friday stock price:
Intel Corp. somehow managed to beat expectations despite facing softness in the personal computer market, but its weak forecast for the current quarter sparked panic among investors, and its stock plunged more than 30% Friday.
The computer chipmaker reported third-quarter earnings before certain costs such as stock compensation of 52 cents per share, easily beating Wall Street’s guidance of 40 cents per share. Revenue for the quarter rose 21% from a year earlier, to $16.51 billion, edging past the Street’s target of $16.5 billion. All told, the company posted net income of $3.3 billion, down from the $4.1 billion it earned in the same period last year.
However, the results couldn’t mask the poor performance of Intel’s client computing and physical AI business, where revenue dropped 5% from a year earlier, to $8.1 billion, missing the Street’s target. The company was held up by its data center and AI unit, where revenue soared 78%, to $7.3 billion.
The client computing business is struggling because the PC market is currently in decline. Global PC shipments fell 4% in the second quarter according to data from Counterpoint Research, partly due to the soaring costs of memory and other components.
Things are likely to get even worse in the PC market, which is why Intel could only offer a tepid forecast going forward. The company said it’s looking for fourth-quarter earnings of about 25 cents per share at the midpoint of its guidance range, with revenue at between $15 billion and $16 billion. Analysts are targeting earnings of 43 cents per share and sales of $17.1 billion.
Intel’s finance chief David Zinsner said the company sees the PC slowdown continuing until at least the first half of next year. “We will continue making disciplined investments capacity and working capital to increase supply and support future growth,” he told investors.
During the quarter, the company suffered a tragic setback when an explosion occurred at one of its chip fabrication plants in Israel, resulting in the deaths of two workers and serious injuries to a third person. That incident will cost the company about $600 million in lost revenue over the next two quarters, Zinsner told analysts on a conference call. Chief Executive Lip-Bu Tan (pictured) said the “unfortunate and tragic accident” resulted in an “extended shutdown” at the plant, delaying shipments of new 10-nanometer chips.
Today’s stock drop slams the brakes on what had been one of the fastest-growing technology stocks this year. Intel’s stock had been on an upward tear all year, rising more than 150% since January. However, the stock’s growth had already stalled since the beginning of the month amid growing concerns about the tech industry’s runaway spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure. “We will remain disciplined as we invest and we will not spend $1 of capital unless we have confidence in the health of our products and process technologies and clear evidence of customer demand,” Zinsner said in an effort to reassure investors.
Intel is best known for the central processing units that power much of the world’s PCs, but its recent revival has primarily been driven by the emergence of agentic AI workloads. Chatbots such as ChatGPT and Gemini primarily use the graphics processing units sold by rival chipmakers like Nvidia Corp. and Advanced Micro Devices Inc., but AI agents such as Meta Platforms Inc.’s Muse and OpenAI Group PBC’s Dots run more efficiently on CPUs. As agentic AI has grown in popularity this year, so too have Intel’s chips.
“Despite near term headwinds affecting the broader PC industry, we continue to see robust demand for CPUs and more broadly, for all our computing platforms in the long term,” Tan insisted.
However, the demand for Intel’s CPUs has been offset by the struggles of its nascent foundry business, which was spun out as a separate business unit a couple of years ago but has struggled to gain traction with customers. So far, its only major customer is Intel itself.
The company’s fab in Arizona is the only U.S.-based facility that’s able to mass produce advanced logic chips. “We are going to have a couple of customers ready to announce in the next two quarters,” Tan promised analysts on the call. He added that the company is also making “tremendous progress” with its forthcoming 14A chips.
Intel, which had previously fallen behind on its roadmap for seven- and 10-nanometer chips, has been struggling to get back on track but has benefited from a vote of confidence by President Donald Trump, whose administration took a 10% stake in the company last year. Since then, Nvidia and SoftBank Group Corp. have also invested billions of dollars into the chipmaker.
Tan is generally viewed as being more cautious than his predecessor, Pat Gelsinger, who made the decision to spin out the foundry business and try to attract external customers. But at the same time, he has also given the green light to some significant investments such as a new chip fab in Ohio that was first expected to come online last year. So far it hasn’t, but Intel said last week that construction crews have installed the first structural truss at the facility.
Last month, reports emerged that Intel was talking to the high-bandwidth memory chip manufacturer SK hynix Inc. about the prospect of it leasing a part of the Ohio fab’s capacity. The South Korean firm reportedly wants to manufacture some of its memory chips at the new site, but so far nothing concrete has been announced. Tan refused to acknowledge those talks, telling analysts that the company “continues to evaluate unique and highly differentiated opportunities to accelerate our Ohio buildout, and I hope to share more on this in the near future.”

93,2026-10-30,"
  By Anthony Franco |
  at October 30, 2026 3:25pm CDT  
Designated hitter Joc Pederson is back on the open market. Jesse Rogers of ESPN reports that Pederson has declined his end of an $18.5MM mutual option with the Rangers. He is now a free agent.
As with all mutual option decisions, this is essentially a procedural move. It’s been more than a decade since any of these were exercised by both parties. They’re generally a way for the team to delay paying a portion of the guaranteed contract in the form of a buyout, which isn’t due until the end of the season. They’re essentially an unofficial deferral within the contract term.
Pederson’s option was a little bit different, as there’s no buyout. It was effectively put into place as a method for the Rangers to override Pederson’s ability to opt out of his two-year contract last offseason. If he’d opted out, the Rangers could preemptively trigger the 2027 mutual option and retain him for 2026-27. It ended up being a moot point, as Pederson slogged through a miserable ’25 campaign and never considered walking away from this year’s $18.5MM salary.
He finds himself in a quite different situation this time around. Pederson had a much better second season in Arlington, ranking second on the team with 26 home runs. He was their primary leadoff hitter against right-handed pitching and batted .245/.338/.496 across 396 plate appearances with the platoon advantage.
Pederson has never hit left-handed pitching. He offers very little defensive utility, playing a total of 71 innings between first base (63) and right field (eight) this year. It’s a limited profile, but Pederson is generally among the 30 or 40 best hitters in MLB against right-handed pitching. He’ll have a limited number of free agent suitors but should be able to command another eight-figure contract.
His old team in Arizona could use a DH and is looking to improve against righties. Texas will presumably have some interest in bringing him back. The Pirates, Padres, Blue Jays and Orioles all got lackluster production out of the DH spot. The Reds were the worst offensive team in MLB against right-handed pitching and are unlikely to re-sign Eugenio Suárez. This is one of three mutual option decisions for Texas, who’ll also see Kyle Higashioka and Jakob Junis hit the market in the next few days.

94,2026-10-30,"💌 Hey there, it’s Elizabeth from SigNoz!
This newsletter is an honest attempt to talk about all things - observability, OpenTelemetry, open-source and the engineering in between! &This piece took 6 days, 5 hours to be cooked, hope we served. 🌚



There are two popular prophecies floating around tech circles these days.
The first says SRE is the future of all software engineering, that as AI writes more and more code, the humans who remain will be the ones keeping systems alive. The second says AI will devour every tech job alive, SREs included. Neither is particularly useful if you’re an SRE trying to figure out what your Tuesday will look like in 2027.
Let’s ask a more grounded question by looking at what’s already happening: When AI handles 95% of your incident response, do you get worse at handling the 5% that actually matters?

Most of us already use AI for our daily work (our brains a little fried!), and so do SREs. Today’s discussion agenda is not whether AI replaces SREs, but whether AI is quietly making SREs less capable and whether anyone will notice anything before the next novel outage hits. The foundational framework for this entire debate comes from a 1983 research paper that’s eerily prescient.

Note from author: Below is a brief precursor to History of Automation, which you might enjoy if you are into History and Cultures (like me 😉).
In 1983, a cognitive psychologist named Lisanne Bainbridge published a paper called Ironies of Automation. It became one of the most cited papers in human factors research, and its core argument is almost uncomfortably relevant today.
Bainbridge studied what happened when factories and industrial systems automated the work of blue-collar operators. The findings were paradoxical and revealed that the more you automate a process, the more critical the human operator becomes during the rare moments automation fails and the less practice they get, and the worse they become at exactly those interventions. Automation, which was inherently designed to remove humans from the loop, left them with the worst possible job, i.e., long stretches of passive monitoring punctuated by rare, high-stakes crises they were increasingly unprepared for.
Ring any bells yet? 🙂
Basically, I’m drawing a parallel between the AI revolution and industrial automation. Industrial automation reshaped blue-collar work by taking over routine physical tasks, and the workers who remained had to handle exceptions they’d lost the muscle memory for. AI is doing the same thing to knowledge workers by taking over the routine cognitive tasks, the pattern matching, the triage, the known-issue resolution and leaving humans with the rare, complex, ambiguous problems.
The exact problems that require deep expertise, the exact expertise that atrophies when you stop practising.
Now we’re replaying this pattern with AI agents, and the stakes in software systems are only growing.

Let’s take stock of where things stand today in the world of site reliability engineering.
Alert noise reduction and intelligent grouping, runbook execution for known issues, log pattern detection and anomaly flagging, and basic root-cause suggestions from historical incident data, and auto-remediation for well-understood failure modes like restarting a crashed pod or scaling up a service that’s running hot, are all fairly automated today.

Some immediate targets include multi-signal correlation across metrics, logs, and traces, autonomous root-cause analysis for partially understood failures, predictive incident detection before users are affected, AI-driven change risk assessment and automated rollbacks.
PagerDuty frames this as a tiered model.
Tier 1 incidents: Known issues with known fixes get fully automated.
Tier 2 incidents: Partially understood problems receive AI recommendations with human validation.
Tier 3 incidents: Novel, complex, cascading failures stay human-led, with AI providing supporting context.
But here’s the catch.
If human SREs (okay, now we have to use adjectives like human 🥹) only engage with Tier 3 incidents, i.e. the novel, never-before-seen outages, where do they build the intuition to handle them? Intuition is usually developed from years of hands-on incident response, pattern recognition built through repetition, and the kind of gut-level understanding of a system that only develops from painfully waking up at the odd hour to solve the bug that brought the system down.

Subscribe if you wish to read some more hot takes. We are cooking some great ones!

This is where the picture starts getting blurry. Let’s look at some emerging research on AI-induced deskilling across multiple fields, which paints a consistent and concerning picture.
In medicine, a recent study found that endoscopists who used AI assistance for polyp detection saw their unassisted detection rates drop from 28% to 22% after a period of AI use. They got worse at the thing they were supposed to be experts in, not because they forgot the theory, but because they stopped exercising the skill.
In aviation, research has shown that long-haul pilots who rely heavily on autopilot systems experience measurable degradation in situational awareness and manual flying ability. The problem got serious enough that the FAA now mandates more manual flying time to counteract the effect.
Somewhere over the past year, AI stopped being a tool I occasionally reached for and became the first thing I reach for, always. My instinct now is to offload as much as possible and apply my own thinking only where it’s absolutely unavoidable. The problem is that these moments are becoming the only exercises my brain gets, and I can feel the rust.
We can draw a pattern here. The more you let the system handle, the worse you get at handling things yourself and here’s the truly dangerous part, you don’t feel it happening. It gets masked as hyper-productivity. Cognitive research suggests that because AI tools make tasks feel easier and enhance visible performance, users are often unable to accurately judge the true status of their own skills. You feel competent, dashboards look green, and then on a Wednesday, a novel incident hits that doesn’t match any pattern the AI has seen, and you realise the muscle has atrophied.
For SREs, this manifests in specific ways, like we stop reading raw log streams because the AI summarises them, we stop forming hypotheses during incidents because the AI suggests root causes, we stop building mental models of system architecture because the AI maps dependencies for us, and each of these individually looks like a productivity win. Collectively, they hollow out the very expertise that makes an SRE effective when things go sideways in ways nobody anticipated.
But there’s something even more concerning than deskilling, and researchers have started calling it never-skilling. Deskilling means you once had a capability but have since lost it. Never-skilling means you never developed it in the first place. For junior SREs entering the field today in an environment where AI handles most of the incident response workflow, the opportunities to build foundational intuition and muscle through hands-on practice are vanishing.
The training pipeline itself is broken and not self-healing.
SREs realise their skills are degrading and lean more on AI to compensate, which further degrades their skills, creating a vicious cycle from which escape is difficult.
We are definitely not rejecting AI tooling; we are adopting it and integrating it stronger than ever before, because that’s the only way forward.
A few approaches worth considering:
Deliberate inefficiency. Just as the FAA mandates manual flying time even when the autopilot is perfectly capable, SRE teams can designate certain incidents, even the ones the AI could handle, as human-practice opportunities. This can be considered as a long-term investment to keep skills fresh, although it might come at the cost of a super-fast solution
Build for human-in-the-loop, not human-on-the-side. There’s a meaningful difference between a system where a human approves an AI’s recommendation and one where a human actively engages with the problem alongside AI. The former keeps humans in a supervisory role that Bainbridge (the lady who wrote that research paper about 40 years ago) showed leads to vigilance decay, and the latter keeps them cognitively engaged.
Let’s zoom out and take a look at the bigger picture.
Everything we’ve discussed here, the ironies of automation, the deskilling risk, the never-skilling problem, collectively applies well beyond SRE. Software engineering as a whole is navigating the same tension. As AI writes more code, reviews more PRs, and handles more debugging, the same questions apply.
We’re talking about SREs specifically because that’s the world we live in at SigNoz. We build an open-source observability platform, the kind of tool that gives SREs the metrics, traces, and logs they need to understand their systems deeply. For us, this deskilling question is not a rhetorical fad; it directly shapes how we’re building AI into our product.
Our approach is to start with an AI assistant that helps SREs leverage the power of LLMs while keeping humans firmly in control. Eventually, we’ll enable more autonomy but within clear guardrails, and only as trust is earned.
One advantage we have in this space is that, as an observability platform, we sit on the data itself, the metrics, traces, and logs that SREs rely on. Most AI SRE products today integrate with observability tools through APIs, which means they’re working with a limited, second-hand view of your systems. Because we own the data layer, we can build much deeper, more context-aware AI capabilities that understand your system the way an experienced SRE would.
And to answer the burning question in your head, our goal isn’t AI that replaces SREs. It’s AI that supercharges SREs. Unlike ongoing lore, we believe humans will remain essential for the decisions that matter most, especially those that impact production infrastructure.
The future of SRE is human with AI intentionally designed to keep humans sharp, engaged, and ready for the 5% that really counts.

Here’s the LinkedIn post our founder posted a few days ago, which inspired me to write this.

Thanks for reading Observability Real Talk! Subscribe if you wish to read more hot takes. We are cooking some great ones!

The US navy also went through this 50+ years ago. They had the ability to automate nearly everything, but the admiral in charge of the project wisely said that a machine could suggest what to do, but there must always be a human in the loop to actually carry out the action, or we would lose our ability when the machines failed.

95,2026-10-30,"Razer has officially introduced a brand-new Evangelion-themed collection inspired by the iconic anime franchise, featuring an exclusive regional rollout across the Asia-Pacific market. Named Razer | Evangelion (EVA-01), the hardware lineup adopts the signature purple and green colorway of the mecha piloted by Shinji Ikari, targeting both avid gamers and anime collectors seeking a fully coordinated desktop setup. 
 
The collection comprises five core gaming peripherals and furniture items: the DeathAdder V4 Pro mouse, BlackWidow V4 TKL HyperSpeed keyboard, Leviathan V2 X soundbar, Gigantus V2 XXL mousepad, and Iskur V2 X gaming chair. Each piece incorporates thematic design cues to bring the aesthetic of the series directly from the screen into the workspace. 
 
A standout highlight of the collection is the DeathAdder V4 Pro mouse, which ships with a custom display stand styled after the mechanical launch platform used to prepare EVA-01 for combat missions. The mouse itself combines a lightweight ergonomic profile with advanced second-generation HyperSpeed Wireless connectivity. Meanwhile, the BlackWidow V4 TKL HyperSpeed keyboard opts for a compact, tenkeyless layout equipped with hot-swappable mechanical switches, allowing users to customize their tactile typing experience. 
 
For desktop audio and immersion, the Leviathan V2 X soundbar features a compact USB-C powered chassis complete with custom Razer Chroma RGB lighting zones to match the EVA-01 color scheme. The Gigantus V2 XXL mousepad expands the visual theme across the desk surface with a textured micro-weave cloth layout, while the Iskur V2 X chair rounds out the collection with ergonomic lumbar support and themed upholstery. 
 
This release follows Razer’s previous collaboration centered on the EVA-02 unit, further expanding merchandise options for franchise fans. 
In Japan, retail pricing is set at 32,980 yen (~$215) for the mouse, 35,980 yen (~$235) for the keyboard, 22,980 yen (~$150) for the soundbar, 6,980 yen (~$45) for the mousepad, and 69,980 yen (~$455) for the gaming chair. The collection will be sold through Amazon and select regional retailers, with no current plans for a broader international release outside the Asia-Pacific region.
